Homological mirror symmetry for Batyrev mirror pairs
Sheel Ganatra, Andrew Hanlon, Jeff Hicks, Daniel Pomerleano, and Nick Sheridan

Abstract
We prove Kontsevich’s homological mirror symmetry conjecture for a large class of mirror pairs of Calabi–Yau hypersurfaces in toric varieties. These mirror pairs were constructed by Batyrev from dual reflexive polytopes. The theorem holds in characteristic zero and in all but finitely many positive characteristics.



1
Introduction

The earliest constructions of conjectural mirror pairs concern combinatorially defined Calabi–Yau subvarieties of toric varieties. Batyrev conjectured that, if
\Delta
and
\Delta^{*}
are dual reflexive polytopes, then the (crepant resolutions of) anticanonical hypersurfaces in the corresponding toric varieties should be mirror to each other
[
Bat94
]
. Batyrev’s work generalized Greene–Plesser’s earlier construction of conjectural mirror pairs of hypersurfaces in quotients of weighted projective spaces
[
GP90
]
; and it was subsequently generalized by Batyrev–Borisov’s construction of conjectural mirror pairs of complete intersections in toric varieties
[
BB97
]
, which in turn was generalized by Gross–Siebert’s construction of conjectural mirror pairs of toric degenerations
[
GS06
,
GS10
]
. Various other related constructions have also extended mirror symmetry beyond the Calabi–Yau setting.

Kontsevich’s homological mirror symmetry conjecture
[
Kon95
]
posits an equivalence between natural algebro-geometric and symplectic categories associated to mirror pairs. Although homological mirror symmetry has been highly influential in algebraic and symplectic geometry, it has been proved only for a small number of cases in the original setting of mirror pairs of compact Calabi–Yau varieties. Namely, homological mirror symmetry has been proved for elliptic curves in
[
PZ98
,
Pol04
]
, higher-dimensional abelian varieties in
[
KS01
,
Fuk02
,
AS10
,
Abo21
]
, quartics in
\C\mathbb{P}^{3}
in
[
Sei14
]
, Calabi–Yau hypersurfaces in higher dimensional projective spaces in
[
She15
]
, and for Greene–Plesser mirror pairs, together with certain Batyrev–Borisov mirror pairs (‘generalized Greene–Plesser mirror pairs’, which are complete intersections in quotients of products of weighted projective spaces) in
[
SS21
]
. In the present work, we prove homological mirror symmetry for a large new class of Batyrev mirror pairs. One noteworthy feature of our result is that it holds not only in characteristic zero, but also in all but finitely many positive characteristics.

The rest of the introduction is organized as follows: in
Section

1.1
, we introduce the toric data on which our construction of Batyrev mirror pairs depends; and in
Sections

1.2
and
1.3
, we construct the symplectic and algebraic sides of the mirror pair. We then state the homological mirror symmetry conjecture for Batyrev mirrors in
Section

1.4
and our HMS statement for Batyrev mirrors (
Section

1.5
) in
Section

1.5
.
Section

1.6
gives an overview of the proof of
Section

1.5
; we also include a table of notation for the reader’s convenience.

1.1
Toric data

We start by introducing some notation used in the Batyrev construction, following
[
Bat94
,
CK99
]
.
Let:

•

\Bbbk
be a field;

•

M\cong\Z^{n}
be a lattice;

•

\Delta\subset M_{\R}
a reflexive polytope,
\Delta^{*}\subset M^{*}_{\R}
its polar dual;

•

\Sigma
be the fan in
M^{*}_{\R}
dual to
\Delta
(its rays point along the vertices of
\Delta^{*}
), and
\Sigma^{*}
the fan in
M_{\R}
dual to
\Delta^{*}
;

•

Y
be the toric
\C
-variety corresponding to
\Sigma
, and
Y^{*}
the toric
\Bbbk
-variety corresponding to
\Sigma^{*}
.

Note that there is a toric line bundle
\mathcal{L}_{\Delta^{*}}\to Y^{*}
, with a basis of sections indexed by the set
P_{0}:=\Delta^{*}\cap M^{*}
.
We denote the section corresponding to
\mathsf{p}\in P_{0}
by
z^{\mathsf{p}}
; we similarly denote the section of
\mathcal{L}_{\Delta}\to Y
corresponding to
\alpha\in A:=\Delta\cap M
by
z^{\alpha}
.

Let
P\subset P_{0}
be the subset of lattice points lying on a face of
\Delta^{*}
which has codimension
\geq 2
.
Let
\kappa\in(\R_{>0})^{P}
and
K\subset\R
be a subgroup which contains
\kappa_{\mathsf{p}}
for all
\mathsf{p}\in P
.
Our construction will depend on the data
(\Bbbk,M,\Delta,\kappa,K)
.

1.2
Symplectic construction

Consider the following sections of
\mathcal{L}_{\Delta}\to Y
:

W_{0}:=z^{\mathsf{0}},\qquad W_{1}:=\sum_{\alpha\in A\setminus\{0\}}z^{\alpha}.

If
\Sigma^{*}
is simplicial, then for sufficiently large
t\in\R_{>0}
, the hypersurface

X_{t}=\{tW_{0}=W_{1}\}\subset Y

intersects each toric stratum smoothly (this is proved using ideas from tropical geometry, as we explain in Section
2.2
).
When
Y
is not smooth, the hypersurface
X_{t}
need not be smooth; we need therefore to take a crepant resolution of
Y
.
The data for this is determined by
\kappa
.

Let
\psi_{\kappa}:M^{*}_{\R}\to\R
be the largest convex
1
1

1

We use the convention that
f(x)=x^{2}
is convex, in contrast to some of the literature on toric varieties (and
[
Gan+24
]
).
function such that
\psi_{\kappa}(t\cdot\mathsf{p})\leq t\cdot\kappa_{\mathsf{p}}
for all
t\in\R_{\geq 0}
and all
\mathsf{p}\in P
.
The decomposition of
M^{*}_{\R}
into domains of linearity of
\psi_{\kappa}
induces a fan
\Sigma_{\kappa}
.

Definition 1
.

We say that the
MPCP condition
holds if
\Sigma_{\kappa}
is a projective simplicial refinement of
\Sigma
whose rays are generated by the elements of
P
.

{rmk}

MPCP stands for Maximal Projective Crepant Partial desingularization (see
[
Bat94
, Definition 2.2.13]
).
In the language of
[
CK99
, Section 6.2.3]
,
\Sigma_{\kappa}
is called a
simplified projective subdivision
of
\Sigma
, and the MPCP condition holds if and only if
\kappa
lies in the interior of a top-dimensional cone
\text{cpl}(\tilde{\Sigma}_{\kappa})
of the secondary fan (or Gelfand–Kapranov–Zelevinskij decomposition) associated to
P\subset M_{\R}
, where
\Sigma_{\kappa}
is a projective simplicial refinement of
\Sigma
whose rays are generated by
P
.

The morphism of fans
\Sigma_{\kappa}\to\Sigma
induces a toric morphism corresponding to the resolution
Y_{\kappa}\to Y
.
Let
X_{t,\kappa}\subset Y_{\kappa}
be the proper transform of
X_{t}
.
The intersection of
X_{t,\kappa}
with each toric stratum is smooth; so if the MPCP condition holds, then
X_{t,\kappa}
is a maximal projective crepant partial desingularization of
X_{t}
(hence the name of the condition).

Observe that even if the MPCP condition holds,
Y_{\kappa}
may have finite quotient singularities since
\Sigma_{\kappa}
is only assumed to be simplicial.
Therefore,
X_{t,\kappa}
may also have finite quotient singularities.
We would like to understand when
X_{t,\kappa}
is in fact smooth.
Let
Y_{\kappa,0}\subset Y_{\kappa}
denote the pre-image of the toric fixed points of
Y
.
Observe that
X_{t,\kappa}
avoids
Y_{\kappa,0}
.

Definition 2
.

We say that the
MPCS condition
holds if the MPCP condition holds, and, furthermore,
Y_{\kappa}
is smooth away from
Y_{\kappa,0}
.
MPCS stands for Maximal Projective Crepant Smooth desingularization.

We remark that the MPCS and MPCP conditions are equivalent when
\dim_{\C}(Y_{\kappa})\leq 4
(see
[
Bat94
, §2.2]
).
If the MPCS condition holds, then
X_{\kappa}
avoids the non-smooth locus of
Y_{\kappa}
, and if
\Sigma^{*}
is simplicial, then
X_{t,\kappa}
is in fact smooth (again using tropical geometry), and Calabi–Yau by
[
Bat94
, Theorem 4.1.9(i) and Proposition 3.2.2]
.

Definition 3
.

The simplicial fan
\Sigma_{\kappa}
determines a subdivision of
\Delta^{*}
into simplices.
We say that the
condition on characteristic
is satisfied if
\mathrm{char}\,(\Bbbk)
does not divide

•

the affine volume of any of the simplices in the subdivision of
\Delta^{*}
determined by
\Sigma_{\kappa}
;
2
2

2

We normalize the affine volume so that the minimum possible non-zero affine volume of a lattice simplex is
1
.

•

the order of the finite abelian group
M^{*}/L
, where
L
is the subgroup generated by
P
.

We denote the toric boundary divisor of
Y_{\kappa}
by
D^{Y}
.
Note that it has irreducible components
D^{Y}_{\mathsf{p}}
indexed by
\mathsf{p}\in P
.
Let
D^{Y}_{\kappa}:=\sum_{\mathsf{p}\in P}\kappa_{\mathsf{p}}\cdot D^{Y}_{\mathsf{p}}
be the toric
\R
-Cartier divisor with support function
\psi_{\kappa}
.
Because
\psi_{\kappa}
is strictly convex (by our assumption that its domains of linearity are the cones of the fan
\Sigma_{\kappa}
), this divisor is ample, so the first Chern class of the corresponding line bundle is represented in de Rham cohomology by a toric orbifold Kähler form
\omega^{Y}_{\kappa}
(see the discussion in
[
AGM93
, §4]
).
We denote its restriction to
X_{t,\kappa}
by
\omega_{\kappa}
.
Because
X_{t,\kappa}
avoids the non-smooth locus of
Y_{\kappa}
,
\omega_{\kappa}
is an honest Kähler form.
Its cohomology class is Poincaré dual to
\sum_{\mathsf{p}\in P}\kappa_{\mathsf{p}}\cdot[D_{\mathsf{p}}]
, where
D_{\mathsf{p}}:=X_{t,\kappa}\cap D^{Y}_{\mathsf{p}}
.

We now introduce the following notation: for any face
S
of
\Delta
(or
\Delta^{*}
), we define
i(S)
to be the number of interior points on that face. Furthermore, we define
S^{*}
to be the face of
\Delta^{*}
(or
\Delta
) which is dual to
S
.

Lemma 4
.

For
\mathsf{p}\in P_{0}
, let
S_{\mathsf{p}}
denote the face of
\Delta^{*}
containing
\mathsf{p}
in its interior. Then

\#(\text{connected components of
$D_{\mathsf{p}}$})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}0&\text{if $\dim(S^{*}_{\mathsf{p}})=0$}\\
i(S^{*}_{\mathsf{p}})&\text{if $\dim(S^{*}_{\mathsf{p}})=1$}\\
1&\text{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

Proof 1.1
.

The map
Y_{\kappa}\to Y
blows
D^{Y}_{\mathsf{p}}
down to the stratum
Y_{\mathsf{p}}
corresponding to
S^{*}_{\mathsf{p}}
. Then,
D_{\mathsf{p}}
is the preimage of
X\cap Y_{\mathsf{p}}
. This is an ample hypersurface and therefore connected unless
Y_{\mathsf{p}}
is 0-dimensional (in which case it is empty) or
1
-dimensional (in which case it is a union of
i(S^{*}_{\mathsf{p}})
points).
The preimage of a connected set under a toric morphism is connected, so the result follows.

Definition 5
.

We say that the
connectedness condition
is satisfied if for every one-dimensional face
S^{*}
of
\Delta^{*}
, either
i(S^{*})=0
(i.e.,
S^{*}
has affine length
1
) or
i(S)=0
.

1.3
Algebraic construction

We work over a universal Novikov field:

\Lambda_{\Bbbk,K}:=\left\{\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}c_{j}\cdot q^{\kappa_{j}}:c_{j}\in\Bbbk,\kappa_{j}\in K,\lim_{j\to\infty}\kappa_{j}=+\infty\right\}.

(1.1)

As
\Bbbk
and
K
will be fixed throughout the paper, we usually omit them from the notation. Note that
\Lambda
is a field extension of
\Bbbk
, and if
\Bbbk
is algebraically closed and
K
is divisible, it is algebraically closed. Moreover, it has a valuation

\displaystyle\mathrm{val}:\Lambda

\displaystyle\to K\cup\{\infty\}

(1.2)

\displaystyle\mathrm{val}\left(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}c_{j}\cdot q^{\kappa_{j}}\right)

\displaystyle:=\min_{j}\{\kappa_{j}:c_{j}\neq 0\}.

(1.3)

We consider sections of
\mathcal{L}_{\Delta^{*}}\to Y^{*}
:

W_{r}(z):=-z^{\mathsf{0}}+\sum_{\mathsf{p}\in P}r_{\mathsf{p}}\cdot z^{\mathsf{p}},

(1.4)

for
r=(r_{\mathsf{p}})\in\mathbb{A}^{P}_{\Lambda}
.
We define
X^{*}_{r}:=\{W_{r}=0\}\subset Y^{*}_{\Lambda}
.

{rmk}

Observe that we have a correspondence

\text{monomial $z^{\mathsf{p}}$ of $W_{r}$}\leftrightarrow\text{divisor $D_{\mathsf{p}}\subset X$}.

(1.5)

This correspondence is called the ‘monomial–divisor mirror map’ (see
[
AGM93
]
).

1.4
HMS: conjecture

On the
B
-side of mirror symmetry, we consider the
\Lambda
-linear
\Z
-graded dg category of coherent complexes
D^{b}_{dg}Coh(X^{*}_{r})
.
3
3

3

By the results of
[
LO10
,
CS18
]
, dg-enhancements of the derived category of coherent sheaves are unique up to quasi-equivalence.
On the
A
-side of mirror symmetry, we consider (a version of) the Fukaya category
\EuF(X_{t,\kappa},\omega_{\kappa}).
We note that most definitions of the Fukaya category work over ground fields of characteristic zero, whereas we consider mirror symmetry over fields of arbitrary characteristic. We give more details about the version of the Fukaya category we are working with below and in
Section

6.2
. For now, we simply recall that the Fukaya category may be curved, i.e., it may have non-vanishing
\mu^{0}
and therefore not be an honest
A_{\infty}
category.
Therefore, we consider the version whose objects are bounding cochains on objects of the Fukaya category, which we denote by
\EuF(X_{t,\kappa},\omega_{\kappa})^{\mathsf{bc}}
.
It is a
\Lambda
-linear
\Z
-graded cohomologically unital
A_{\infty}
category, whose idempotent completed pre-triangulated closure we denote
\operatorname{Perf}\EuF(X_{t,\kappa},\omega_{\kappa})^{\mathsf{bc}}
.
One part of Kontsevich’s homological mirror symmetry conjecture for Batyrev mirrors then reads:

Conjecture
\themainthm
.

Suppose
\Sigma^{*}
is simplicial. There is a quasi-equivalence of
\Lambda
-linear
\Z
-graded
A_{\infty}
categories

\operatorname{Perf}\EuF(X_{t,\kappa},\omega_{\kappa})^{\mathsf{bc}}\simeq D^{b}_{dg}Coh(X^{*}_{b(\kappa)}),

(1.6)

for some
b(\kappa)\in\mathbb{A}^{P}_{\Lambda}
with
\mathrm{val}(b(\kappa)_{\mathsf{p}})=\kappa_{\mathsf{p}}
.

1.5
HMS: statement

The version of the Fukaya category that we work with is the relative Fukaya category,
\EuF(X_{t,\kappa},D,\kappa;\Lambda_{\Bbbk,K})
, which is constructed in
[
PS23
, Section 1.4]
.

{main}

Suppose that
\operatorname{dim}(X_{t,\kappa})\geq 3
, the condition on characteristic is satisfied,
\Sigma^{*}
is smooth, and the MPCS and connectedness conditions hold. Then:

1.

There is a fully-faithful embedding
D^{b}_{dg}Coh(X^{*}_{b(\kappa)})\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Perf}\EuF(X_{t,\kappa},D,\kappa;\Lambda_{\Bbbk,K})^{\mathsf{bc}}.

2.

Suppose the relative Fukaya category
\EuF(X_{t,\kappa},D,\kappa;\Lambda_{\Bbbk,K})^{\mathsf{bc}}
satisfies the hypotheses enumerated in
[
SS21
, Section 2.5]
. Then Conjecture
1.4
holds with
\EuF(X_{t,\kappa},\omega_{\kappa})=\EuF(X_{t,\kappa},D,\kappa;\Lambda_{\Bbbk,K}).

Various hypotheses appear in Theorem
1.5
that are not present in Conjecture
1.4
. We now provide a short discussion of these hypotheses and where they are used in our proof.

{rmk}

Theorem
1.5
holds for any version of the Fukaya category which satisfies the hypotheses enumerated in
[
SS21
, Section 2.5]
. These hypotheses are expected to hold in whatever version of the Fukaya category one works with (e.g., the general version defined in
[
Fuk+10a
,
Fuk23
]
).
It is shown in
[
PS23
]
that the relative Fukaya category satisfies some of the required properties; the remaining ones will be verified in forthcoming work.
These remaining hypotheses are only needed in the proof of split-generation of the Fukaya category by the image of the embedding from
Section

1.5
(
1
).

{rmk}

One might hope that the MPCS condition could be replaced by the weaker MPCP condition in Theorem
1.5
and that the proof would go through with minimal changes.
In that case,
(X_{t,\kappa},\omega_{\kappa})
would be a symplectic orbifold, so the definition of the Fukaya category would need to be adjusted accordingly (compare to
[
CP14
]
).

{rmk}

If the connectedness condition does not hold, then we need something more to prove Conjecture
1.4
.
That is because there is more than one component of the divisor
D
corresponding to
\mathsf{p}
, leading to more than one direction we can deform
\omega_{\kappa}
and more than one class in symplectic cohomology of
X_{t,\kappa}\setminus D
corresponding to
\mathsf{p}
.
(Deformations in these directions are not ambient: an ambient Kähler class would have the same coefficient for each component of
D
corresponding to
\mathsf{p}
.)
On the other hand, there is only one corresponding direction in which we can deform
X^{*}_{r}
by deforming
r
: we can only deform the coefficient in front of
z^{\mathsf{p}}
.
Batyrev shows that the space of deformations of
X^{*}_{r}
does have the right dimension, but it must be that some deformations cannot be embedded in
Y^{*}
.

{rmk}

Smoothness of the fan
\Sigma^{*}
is employed most essentially in three places:

•

To match various quasi-equivalences of categories, we use the classification of derived autoequivalences of the derived category of smooth Fano varieties from
[
BO01
]
.

•

The Floer-theoretic proofs of homological mirror symmetry for toric varieties due to
[
Abo09
,
HH22
]
work under the assumption that
\Sigma^{*}
is smooth. We note that the results from
[
Kuw20
,
GS22
]
obtained using microlocal sheaves only require
\Sigma^{*}
to be simplicial.

•

The assumption is used in the proof of B-side versality in
Lemma

76
(which is an ingredient in the proof of
Lemma

73
).

Smoothness of
\Sigma^{*}
is also used in various other places to simplify arguments (e.g.,
Lemma

8
,

Lemma

72
, and
Lemma

79
). However, in these latter cases, there exist straightforward ways to get around the smoothness assumption.

{rmk}

We can refine Conjecture
1.4
by specifying that the mirror map
b(\kappa)
should be given by the formulae in
[
CK99
, §6.3.4]
.
We can prove this refined version using the results announced in
[
GPS15
]
, cf.
[
She20a
, Appendix C]
.
By taking care to work throughout over
\Z
, rather than over the coefficient field
\Bbbk
as we have done, we expect that it would be possible to prove the ‘integrality of mirror maps’ conjecture for the mirror pairs considered in this paper, as was done in the case of Greene–Plesser mirror pairs in
[
Gan+24
]
.
However, we have not done this.

Table 1
:
Notation, as well as a rough identification of constructions under the mirror correspondence.

The proof of
Section

1.5
proceeds as follows.
On the A-side, we construct certain compact Lagrangians
L_{i}
in
X_{t,\kappa}\setminus D
, and let
\mathcal{A}_{R}\subset\EuF(X_{t,\kappa},D)
denote the full subcategory with these objects. On the B-side, we let
\mathcal{O}(i)
denote the restriction to
X_{r}^{*}
of the
i
-th power of the anticanonical bundle on
Y^{*}_{\Lambda}
and consider the full subcategory
\mathcal{B}_{R}\subset D^{b}_{dg}Coh(X^{*}_{r})
with objects
\mathcal{O}(i)
.
These categories are deformations of the
\Bbbk
-linear categories
\mathcal{A}_{0}\subset\EuF(X_{t,\kappa}\setminus D)
and
\mathcal{B}_{0}\subset D^{b}_{dg}Coh(\partial Y^{*})
with the corresponding objects.

The bulk of the paper is devoted to constructing a quasi-isomorphism
\mathcal{A}_{0}\simeq\mathcal{B}_{0}
.
Assume for the moment that we have constructed this quasi-isomorphism.
We then apply the versality result from
[
Gan+24
, Proposition A.6]
to upgrade it to a curved filtered quasi-isomorphism
\Psi^{*}\mathcal{B}_{R}\simeq\mathcal{A}_{R}
, where
\Psi
is an appropriate mirror map.
In order to verify the versality hypotheses on the
A
-side, we need to make certain computations in the symplectic cohomology of
X_{t,\kappa}\setminus D
, which are accomplished using the results of
[
GP20
,
GP21
]
(see
Section

6
).
The versality hypotheses on the
B
-side are more routine (see
Section

7
).

Specializing to appropriate
\Lambda
-points allows us to identify the subcategory of
D^{b}_{dg}Coh(X^{*}_{r})
with objects
\mathcal{O}(i)
, and the subcategory of
\operatorname{Perf}\EuF(X_{t,\kappa},D,\kappa;\Lambda_{\Bbbk,K})^{\mathsf{bc}}
with objects
L_{i}
.
Because
X^{*}_{r}
is smooth, the objects
\mathcal{O}(i)
split-generate; by the ‘automatic generation criterion’ of
[
Gan19
,
San21
]
, the objects
L_{i}
also split-generate.
This completes the proof of
Section

1.5
(see
Section

8
).

Now, let us explain how the quasi-isomorphism
\mathcal{A}_{0}\simeq\mathcal{B}_{0}
is constructed.
We start from the homological mirror symmetry result proved by Gammage–Shende in
[
GS22
]
, building on work of Kuwagaki
[
Kuw20
]
and Ganatra–Pardon–Shende
[
GPS24
]
.
Their work produces a commutative diagram

\operatorname{Perf}\mathcal{W}(X_{t,{\kappa}}\setminus D)
D^{b}_{dg}Coh(\partial Y^{*})
\operatorname{Perf}\mathcal{W}(M^{*}_{\C^{*}},\mathfrak{f}_{\Sigma^{*}})
D^{b}_{dg}Coh(Y^{*})
\cup
i_{*}

(1.7)

in which
\mathfrak{f}_{\Sigma^{*}}
is the boundary at infinity of the Fang–Liu–Treumann–Zaslow skeleton, the horizontal functors are quasi-equivalences, the left vertical arrow is the so-called ‘cup functor’ or ‘Orlov functor’, the right vertical arrow is the pushforward along the inclusion
i:\partial Y^{*}\hookrightarrow Y^{*}
, and the diagram commutes up to natural quasi-isomorphism.
In order to place ourselves in a setting where (
1.7
) holds, we first need to deform the affine variety
X_{t,\kappa}\setminus D
and its symplectic structure by ‘tailoring’ it, as in
[
Abo06
]
, to a Weinstein manifold whose Lagrangian skeleton is computed in
[
Zho20
]
.
This is accomplished in
Section

2
, using general results from
Appendix

B
.

One might think that the subcategory
\mathcal{A}_{0}\subset\mathcal{W}(X_{t,\kappa}\setminus D)
could then be defined to be the category mirror to the subcategory
\mathcal{B}_{0}\subset D^{b}Coh_{dg}(\partial Y^{*})
under the quasi-equivalence from (
1.7
).
Unfortunately, life is not so easy:
a priori
, the mirror to this subcategory may consist of non-compact Lagrangians, whereas the objects of the relative Fukaya category
\EuF(X_{t,\kappa},D)
are compact Lagrangians (at least as it has been defined to date, cf.
[
PS23
]
).
Thus, our main task is to show that the objects mirror to
\mathcal{O}(i)
are representable by compact Lagrangians.
We do this by constructing a functor
\cap
which is a ‘partial adjoint’ to
\cup
, and which by construction takes values in the subcategory of compact Lagrangians.
It is ‘partial’ because it is only defined on a certain subcategory
\mathcal{P}\subset\operatorname{Perf}\mathcal{W}(M^{*}_{\C^{*}},\mathfrak{f}_{\Sigma^{*}})
consisting of ‘arclike’ Lagrangians.

{rmk}

The construction of
\cap
is carried out using a new result in the theory of partially wrapped Fukaya categories, proved in
Appendix

A
, which may be of independent interest, and which we now summarize.
Let
j:S_{0}\to S_{1}
be an inclusion of Liouville sectors, inducing a functor
j_{*}:\mathcal{W}(S_{0})\to\mathcal{W}(S_{1})
in accordance with
[
GPS20
]
.
Given a Lagrangian
L\subset S_{1}
, one can form its Yoneda module
\mathcal{Y}_{L}
, and pull it back via
j_{*}
to a module over
\mathcal{W}(S_{0})
.
In general, this pullback module need not be representable, i.e., quasi-isomorphic to the Yoneda module of some geometric Lagrangian in
S_{0}
.
The general form of the result proved in
Appendix

A
gives a criterion under which the pullback module is representable: if
L
is ‘arclike’ with respect to the inclusion
j
, then we can form a Lagrangian called the ‘negative pushoff in
S_{0}
of
L\cap S_{0}
’; and we prove that its Yoneda module is isomorphic to the pullback of the Yoneda module of (the negative pushoff in
S_{1}
of)
L
under
j_{*}
.
The functor
\cup
is defined to be the pushforward functor associated to the inclusion of a standard neighbourhood of the boundary of a Liouville sector. Applying our general result in this case gives the construction of
\cap
on the subcategory of arclike Lagrangians (see
Section

3
).

We consider certain (shifts of) line bundles on
Y^{*}
, denoted by
\mathcal{E}_{i}
, which have the property that
i^{!}\mathcal{E}_{i}=\mathcal{O}(i)
(see §
5
for the precise description of
\mathcal{E}_{i}
).
If the objects
\tilde{L}_{i}
of
\mathcal{W}(M^{*}_{\C^{*}},\mathfrak{f}_{\Sigma^{*}})
mirror to
\mathcal{E}_{i}
were arclike, then we could define
\mathcal{A}_{0}
to have objects
L_{i}=\cap\tilde{L}_{i}
, which are compact.
Because the homological mirror equivalence matches
\cap
with
i^{!}
(by the uniqueness of adjoints), it would necessarily match
\mathcal{A}_{0}
with
\mathcal{B}_{0}
as required.

Unfortunately, we’re still not done: under Kuwagaki’s construction of the homological mirror equivalence which fits in as the top horizontal arrow in (
1.7
), there is no reason for the objects mirror to
\mathcal{E}_{i}
to be arclike.
However, there is a different approach to homological mirror symmetry for Fano toric varieties, due to Abouzaid
[
Abo06
,
Abo09
]
, Hanlon
[
Han19
]
, and Hanlon–Hicks
[
HH22
]
.
They give a different construction of a mirror equivalence
\operatorname{Perf}\mathcal{W}(T^{*}M^{*}_{S^{1}},\mathfrak{f}_{\Sigma^{*}})\simeq D^{b}_{dg}Coh(Y^{*})
under which the objects mirror to
\mathcal{E}_{i}
(and all other toric line bundles) are arclike.
In order to make use of this, we need to show that the homological mirror equivalence constructed by Abouzaid–Hanlon–Hicks agrees with that constructed by Kuwagaki–Ganatra–Pardon–Shende.
We prove this in
Section

4
, using the classification of derived autoequivalences of smooth Fano varieties due to Bondal–Orlov.

This completes the construction of the quasi-isomorphism
\mathcal{A}_{0}\simeq\mathcal{B}_{0}
, and hence the proof of
Section

1.5
: we take
L_{i}
to be the objects
\cap\tilde{L}_{i}
, where
\tilde{L}_{i}
are the arclike Lagrangians mirror to
\mathcal{E}_{i}
under the Abouzaid–Hanlon–Hicks mirror equivalence.
We still need to verify that this equivalence respects certain gradings which are needed in order to apply the versality result; this is done in
Section

5
, using results from
Appendix

C
.

{rmk}

It has long been anticipated that the Floer theoretic approach to toric HMS from
[
Abo06
,
Abo09
]
and the microlocal sheaf approach initiated in
[
Fan+11
,
Fan+12
]
coincide (cf.
[
Fan+12
, Appendix C.2]
). As noted above, Proposition
27
below settles this question in the affirmative in the Fano case.

{rmk}

We also prove that the subcategories
\mathcal{A}_{0}\subset\EuF(X_{t,\kappa}\setminus D)
and
\mathcal{B}_{0}\subset\perf_{\mathsf{dg}}(\partial Y^{*})
split-generate, where
\perf_{\mathsf{dg}}(\partial Y^{*})\subset D^{b}_{\mathsf{dg}}Coh(\partial Y^{*})
denotes the full subcategory of perfect complexes. In particular, this implies (see
Proposition

50
) that Gammage–Shende’s quasi-equivalence

\perf\mathcal{W}(X_{t,\kappa}\setminus D)\simeq D^{b}_{\mathsf{dg}}Coh(\partial Y^{*})

restricts to a quasi-equivalence

\perf\EuF(X_{t,\kappa}\setminus D)\simeq\perf_{\mathsf{dg}}(\partial Y^{*}).

1.7

2
Symplectic setup

We would like to study the Fukaya category of
X_{t,\kappa}\setminus D=\{tW_{0}=W_{1}\}
by viewing it as the fiber over
t
of the potential
W_{1}/W_{0}
on
M^{*}_{\C^{*}}
.
Following
[
GS22
]
, we replace the spaces
M^{*}_{\C^{*}}
and
X_{t,\kappa}\setminus D
with
B
, a Liouville domain, and
F_{0}\subset\partial B
, a Liouville hypersurface in its boundary.
We define a Liouville sector
S_{0}
by removing a neighbourhood of
F_{0}
from
B
.

This construction occurs in several steps.
Section

2.1
describes the Liouville structure on
X_{t,\kappa}\setminus D
.
Section

2.2
deforms the hypersurface to its tropical limit while preserving its smooth toric compactification.
Section

2.3
shows that this tropical limit has skeleton described by
[
Zho20
]
. Finally,
Section

2.4
describes the construction of the sector
S_{0}
and its relation to
F_{0}
.

2.1
Relative Kähler manifold

Assume the MPCS condition holds; then we have a complex manifold
X_{t,\kappa}
embedded inside the complex orbifold
Y_{\kappa}
.
Furthermore, we have an orbifold simple normal crossings divisor
D^{Y}\subset Y_{\kappa}
,
4
4

4

Everything in
[
She20a
]
is written for manifolds, rather than orbifolds. Nevertheless, we make use of the corresponding notions for orbifolds, because it makes the exposition clearer. It would be straightforward to remove the orbifold language from any argument in which it appears because we essentially only work with the symplectic manifolds
X_{t,\kappa}
and
Y_{\kappa}\setminus D^{Y}
, both of which avoid the orbifold locus.
whose intersection with
X_{t,\kappa}
is a simple normal crossings divisor
D\subset X_{t,\kappa}
.
In the terminology of
[
She20a
, Definition 3.32]
,
(X_{t,\kappa},D)
is a sub-
\mathsf{snc}
pair of
(Y_{\kappa},D^{Y})
.

We recall the notion of a relative Kähler form on a
\mathsf{snc}
pair
(X_{t,\kappa},D)
from
[
She20a
, Definition 3.2]
: it consists of a Kähler form on
X_{t,\kappa}
, together with a Kähler potential on the complement of
D
, having a prescribed form near
D
.
It determines ‘linking numbers’, which are strictly positive real numbers associated with the components of
D
.
One may generalize this to the notion of an orbifold relative Kähler form on
(Y_{\kappa},D^{Y})
.

Note that
Y_{\kappa}\setminus D^{Y}\cong M^{*}_{\C^{*}}=M^{*}_{\R}\times M^{*}_{S^{1}}
.
A choice of basis for
M^{*}
determines coordinates
x_{j}
on
M^{*}_{\R}
, and
\theta_{j}
on
M^{*}_{S^{1}}
, so that the functions
z_{j}=\exp(x_{j}+i\theta_{j})
are holomorphic.

We introduce a restricted class of relative Kähler forms on
(Y_{\kappa},D^{Y})
in Definition
120
.
These have the property that the potential
h^{Y}
on
M^{*}_{\C^{*}}
is pulled back from some
\varphi^{Y}:M^{*}_{\R}\to\R
, called a compactification Kähler potential.
The MPCS condition implies that the divisor
D^{Y}_{\kappa}
is ample, and so by
Lemma

121
there exists a compactification Kähler potential
\varphi^{Y}
with corresponding linking numbers
\kappa_{\mathsf{p}}
.
We obtain a relative Kähler form
(\omega^{X},h^{X})
on
(X_{t,\kappa},D)
by restricting this one.

We endow
Y_{\kappa}
with a meromorphic volume from whose restriction to
Y_{\kappa}\setminus D^{Y}=M^{*}_{\C^{*}}
is

\Omega^{Y}|_{M^{*}_{\C^{*}}}=d\log z_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge d\log z_{n}.

Observe that
X_{t,\kappa}\setminus D\subset M^{*}_{\C^{*}}
is cut out by the equation
W_{1}/W_{0}=t
.
Therefore, we have a holomorphic volume form
\Omega^{Y}/d(W_{1}/W_{0})
on
X_{t,\kappa}\setminus D
, and this extends to a holomorphic volume form
\Omega^{X}
on
X_{t,\kappa}
.

2.2
Liouville domain

Associated to the relative Kähler manifold
(X_{t,\kappa},D,h)
from Section
2.1
, we have an exact symplectic manifold
\mathcal{H}_{t,0}:=X_{t,\kappa}\setminus D\subset M^{*}_{\C^{*}}
.
The Liouville one-form is
\lambda^{X}=-d^{c}h^{X}
.
We denote its Liouville completion by
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{t,0}
(this is well-defined because the Liouville vector field applied to
h^{X}
is positive near
\infty
, cf. Lemma
103
).

We wish to make computations in the exact Fukaya category of the Liouville manifold
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{t,0}
.
To do this, we deform
\mathcal{H}_{t,0}
to a Liouville manifold with a combinatorially described skeleton.

The first step is to ‘tailor’ the affine variety
\mathcal{H}_{t,0}
, following
[
Abo06
]
.
Recall that
A=\Delta\cap M
.
We define functions
(\nu,c):A\to\Z\times\{\pm 1\}
which are equal to
(1,-1)
for
\alpha=0
, and
(0,+1)
otherwise.
Then
\mathcal{H}_{t,0}\subset M^{*}_{\C^{*}}
is the vanishing locus of the function

W_{t,0}(z)=\sum_{\alpha\in A}c(\alpha)\cdot t^{\nu(\alpha)}\cdot z^{\alpha}.

We define the corresponding
tropical amoeba

\Pi\subset M^{*}_{\R}
to be the locus of discontinuity of the piecewise-linear function
M^{*}_{\R}\to\R
sending

x\mapsto\max_{\alpha\in A}\left(-\nu(\alpha)+\langle\alpha,x\rangle\right).

Smoothness of
\Sigma^{*}
(which is a hypothesis of Theorem
1.5
) is equivalent to the statement that the subdivision of
\Delta
induced by
\nu
is a unimodular triangulation, i.e., it is a subdivision into simplices of the minimal possible volume.

The components of
M_{\R}^{*}\setminus\Pi
are in one-to-one correspondence with
A
.
We let
C_{\alpha}
denote the closure of the component indexed by
\alpha\in A
. The only interior lattice point of
\Delta
is
0
, so the only bounded component is
C_{0}
, which is equal to
\Delta^{*}
.

We introduce the map

\displaystyle\Log_{t}:M^{*}_{\C^{*}}

\displaystyle\to M^{*}_{\R}

\displaystyle\Log_{t}(x,\theta)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\log t}x.

The image
\mathcal{A}_{t}:=\Log_{t}(W_{t}^{-1}(0))
is referred to as the amoeba. As shown in
[
Mik04
]
, the Gromov-Hausdorff limit of
\mathcal{A}_{t}
as
t
goes to infinity is
\Pi
.

Following
[
Abo06
]
, we can further tailor this convergence by considering an interpolating family of potentials. Namely, we set

W_{t,s}=\sum_{\alpha\in A}(1-s\psi_{\alpha}(t,x))\cdot c(\alpha)\cdot t^{\nu(\alpha)}\cdot z^{\alpha}

for
t>0
and
s\in[0,1]
and where
\psi_{\alpha}
are appropriately chosen cutoff functions (we recall that
x_{i}=\log|z_{i}|
). In particular, for large
t
,
\psi_{\alpha}(t,x/\log t)
is equal to
0
for
x
in a neighbourhood of
C_{\alpha}
, and
1
outside of a larger neighbourhood of
C_{\alpha}
. In fact, we can use explicitly defined cutoff functions as in
[
Zho20
]
.

We now consider the hypersurfaces
\mathcal{H}_{t,s}=W_{t,s}^{-1}(0)
.

Lemma 6
.

For any
\delta>0
, the family
(\mathcal{H}_{t,s})_{s\in[0,1]}
is
\delta
-approximately holomorphic (in the sense of Definition
114
) for
t
sufficiently large.

Proof 2.1
.

Follows from the proof of
[
Zho20
, Proposition 1.7]
, together with
[
CM07
, Lemma 8.3(b)]
.

Definition 7

(Restatement of Definition
118
)
.

A
smooth toric compactification
of a submanifold
\mathcal{H}\subset M^{*}_{\C^{*}}
is a simplicial fan
\Sigma_{\mathcal{H}}
and smooth submanifold
X_{\mathcal{H}}\subset Y_{\Sigma_{\mathcal{H}}}
, such that
\mathcal{H}=X_{\mathcal{H}}\cap M^{*}_{\C^{*}}
,
X_{\mathcal{H}}
avoids the orbifold points, and the intersection of
X_{\mathcal{H}}
with every stratum of the toric boundary divisor is transverse.

Lemma 8
.

For sufficiently large
t>0
, the family
(\mathcal{H}_{t,s})_{s\in[0,1]}
admits a family of smooth toric compactifications.

Proof 2.2
.

Note that it follows from
Lemma

6
that
\mathcal{H}_{t,s}
is a smooth submanifold of
M^{*}_{\C^{*}}
for sufficiently large
t>0
.

We now consider the closure
X_{t,s}
of
\mathcal{H}_{t,s}
in
Y_{\kappa}
.
For each cone
\sigma
of
\Sigma_{\kappa}
, such that
X_{t,s}
intersects the corresponding torus orbit
Y_{\sigma}
, the latter admits a Zariski neighbourhood with coordinates
(w_{\sigma},z_{\sigma})\in\C^{\dim\sigma}\times(\C^{*})^{\dim Y-\dim\sigma}
.
From the construction of the cutoff functions
\psi_{\alpha}
in
[
Zho20
, Section 1.2]
, it is straightforward to check that in a neighbourhood of
Y_{\sigma}
they are independent of
w_{\sigma}
; in other words, there exist cutoff functions
\psi^{\sigma}_{\alpha}(t,z_{\sigma})
such that
\psi_{\alpha}(t,(w_{\sigma},z_{\sigma}))=\psi^{\sigma}_{\alpha}(t,z_{\sigma})
.
As a result, in this neighbourhood of
Y_{\sigma}
, we have
X_{t,s}=\left(W_{t,s}^{\sigma}\right)^{-1}(0)
where

W_{t,s}^{\sigma}(w_{\sigma},z_{\sigma})=\sum_{\alpha\in\Delta}(1-s\psi^{\sigma}_{\alpha}(t,z_{\sigma}))\cdot c(\alpha)\cdot t^{\nu(\alpha)}\cdot(w_{\sigma},z_{\sigma})^{\alpha}

is a smooth function.

The toric morphism
Y_{\kappa}\to Y
blows
Y_{\sigma}
down to
Y_{\hat{\sigma}}
, where
\hat{\sigma}
is the smallest cone of
\Sigma
containing
\sigma
.
Let
\Delta_{\sigma}
denote the face of
\Delta
dual to
\hat{\sigma}
.
Then
z^{\alpha}
vanishes along
Y_{\sigma}
for
\alpha\notin\Delta_{\sigma}
.
To show that
X_{t,s}
is smooth in a neighbourhood of
Y_{\sigma}
, and intersects it transversely, it then suffices to show that

X_{t,s}\cap Y_{\sigma}=\sum_{\alpha\in\Delta_{\sigma}}(1-s\psi^{\sigma}_{\alpha}(t,z_{\sigma}))\cdot z_{\sigma}^{\alpha}

is smooth.
However, since
\Delta_{\sigma}
is unimodular, this is simply the product of a tailored pair of pants with
(\C^{*})^{j}
for some
j
, which is smooth.

We now choose a potential
\varphi:M^{*}_{\R}\to\R
which is homogeneous of weight
2
and adapted to
\Delta^{*}
in the sense of
[
Zho20
, Definition 2.8]
, which induces a Liouville manifold structure on
M^{*}_{\C^{*}}
.
By restricting, we then have a Liouville manifold structure on
\mathcal{H}_{t,1}
, which has well-defined Liouville completion
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{t,1}
by Lemma
105
.

Lemma 9
.

For sufficiently large
t>0
, we have a Liouville isomorphism
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{t,0}\cong\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{t,1}
.

Proof 2.3
.

The Liouville isomorphism is constructed in two steps.
First, we fix
\mathcal{H}_{t,0}
and linearly interpolate between the potential
\varphi^{Y}
coming from the toric compactification of
\mathcal{H}_{t,0}
, and the potential
\varphi
which is homogeneous of weight
2
and adapted to
\Delta^{*}
.
By Lemma
103
, the latter also has a well-defined Liouville completion; and this completion is Liouville isomorphic to that of
\mathcal{H}_{t,0}
by Lemma
104
.

The second step is to deform
\mathcal{H}_{t,0}
to
\mathcal{H}_{t,1}
using the family
\mathcal{H}_{t,s}
, equipped with the fixed homogeneous potential
\varphi
.
This deformation induces a Liouville isomorphism by Lemma
106
(whose hypotheses are verified by Lemmas
8
and
6
).

2.3
Skeleton

The differential of the potential
\varphi:M^{*}_{\R}\to\R
(which we recall is homogeneous of weight
2
and adapted to
\Delta^{*}
) defines a diffeomorphism

\displaystyle\Phi:M^{*}_{\R}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\sim}M_{\R},

\displaystyle\Phi(x)

\displaystyle=d\varphi_{x}.

We use it to identify

M^{*}_{\C^{*}}=M^{*}_{\R}\times M^{*}_{S^{1}}\xrightarrow{(d\varphi,\id)}M_{\R}\times M^{*}_{S^{1}}=T^{*}M^{*}_{S^{1}},

(2.1)

which fits into a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{*}_{\C^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{*}M^{*}_{S^{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{*}_{\R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{\R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Log}
(
2.1
)
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}

(2.2)

where
\pi
is the natural projection.
This identification identifies the Liouville one-form
-d^{c}\varphi
on
M^{*}_{\C^{*}}
with the standard Liouville one-form on the cotangent bundle.

To be more concrete, let us choose a basis for
M^{*}
, inducing coordinates
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
on
M^{*}_{\R}
,
(\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{n})
on
M^{*}_{S^{1}}
, and
(u_{1},\ldots,u_{n})
on
M_{\R}
.
With respect to these coordinates, we have

\Phi(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})=\left(\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x_{1}},\ldots,\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x_{n}}\right)

and

-d^{c}\varphi=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x_{j}}d\theta_{j}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}u_{j}d\theta_{j}.

Associated to the fan
\Sigma^{*}\subset M_{\R}
of
Y^{*}
is a combinatorially-defined (negative) FLTZ skeleton

{\mathbb{L}}_{\Sigma^{*}}=\bigcup_{\sigma\in\Sigma^{*}}\sigma\times\sigma^{\perp}\subset M_{\R}\times M^{*}_{S^{1}}=T^{*}M^{*}_{S^{1}},

(2.3)

with boundary at infinity

\mathfrak{f}_{\Sigma^{*}}=\left({\mathbb{L}}_{\Sigma^{*}}\setminus\left(\{0\}\times M^{*}_{S^{1}}\right)\right)/\R\subset\partial_{\infty}(T^{*}M^{*}_{S^{1}}).

(2.4)

For
t\gg 0
, the complement of the tailored amoeba
\Log_{t}(\mathcal{H}_{t,1})
has a unique bounded component, whose closure we denote by
\mathcal{C}_{0}
.
By
[
Zho20
, Proposition 1.9]
,
\mathcal{C}_{0}
can alternatively be characterized as the sublevel set of the function
H_{\mathcal{C}_{0}}:M^{*}_{\R}\to\R_{\geq 0}
:

1\geq H_{\mathcal{C}_{0}}:=\sum_{\alpha\in A\setminus\{0\}}(1-\psi_{\alpha}(t,x))t^{\langle\alpha,x\rangle-1}

(2.5)

The region
\mathcal{C}_{0}
is not necessarily convex. However, it is star-shaped:

Proposition 10
.

For
t
sufficiently large, there exists
\epsilon>0
so that
H_{\mathcal{C}_{0}}
is radially increasing, that is,

dH_{\mathcal{C}_{0}}(x\partial_{x})>0

at all points
x
such that
1-\epsilon\leq H_{\mathcal{C}_{0}}(x)\leq 1
where
x\partial_{x}
is the radial vector field on
M_{\R}^{*}
.

Proof 2.4
.

By combining Propositions 1.12(3) and 1.13(2) of
[
Zho20
]
, we can choose
t
sufficiently large so that the outward unit conormal bundle on
\mathcal{C}_{0}
is arbitrarily close in the unit conormal bundle of
M_{\R}^{*}
to the Legendrian given by the union of the outward unit conormals to the strata of
C_{0}
. The vector field
x\partial_{x}
pairs positively with the latter as
C_{0}
is a convex polytope containing the origin. The result then follows from the compactness of the level sets of
H_{\mathcal{C}_{0}}
.

Corollary 11
.

For
t
sufficiently large,
\Log_{t}^{-1}(\mathcal{C}_{0})
is a Liouville domain completing to
M^{*}_{\C^{*}}
.

Proof 2.5
.

By the commutative diagram (
2.2
),
\Log_{t}^{-1}(\mathcal{C}_{0})=\pi^{-1}(\Phi^{-1}(\log t\cdot\mathcal{C}_{0}))
.
The Liouville vector field for the standard Liouville one-form on
T^{*}M^{*}_{S^{1}}
is equal to the radial vector field
u\partial_{u}
.
Thus it suffices to show that
\Phi^{-1}(\log t\cdot\mathcal{C}_{0})
is star-shaped.
As
\varphi
is homogeneous of weight
2
outside a small compact set, and in particular near
\log t\cdot\partial\mathcal{C}_{0}
, we have that
\Phi_{*}(x\partial_{x})
is (pointwise) positively proportional to
u\partial_{u}
in that region (cf.
[
Zho20
, Proposition 2.7]
).
Thus it suffices to check that
\log t\cdot\mathcal{C}_{0}
is star-shaped, which is true by Proposition
10
.

We then have the following result of
[
Zho20
]
, which is a generalization of
[
GS22
, Theorem 6.2.4 and Lemma 6.2.5]
:

Proposition 12
.

For
t
sufficiently large, the intersection
\Lambda_{\Sigma^{*}}\coloneqq{\mathbb{L}}_{\Sigma^{*}}\cap\partial\Log_{t}^{-1}(\mathcal{C}_{0})
is the skeleton of
\mathcal{H}_{t,1}
.

Proof 2.6
.

First,
[
Zho20
, Theorem 2]
shows that for
t
sufficiently large, the skeleton of
\mathcal{H}_{t,1}
is a subset of
\partial\Log_{t}^{-1}(\mathcal{C}_{0})
. Next,
[
Zho20
, Theorem 3]
identifies the projection of the skeleton of
\mathcal{H}_{t,1}
to
\partial_{\infty}T^{*}M^{*}_{S^{1}}
with
\mathfrak{f}_{\Sigma^{*}}
. As this is also true for
\Lambda_{\Sigma^{*}}
and both are contained in
\partial\Log^{-1}_{t}(\mathcal{C}_{0})
, they must coincide.

2.4
Liouville sector

From this point forward, we assume that
t
is chosen sufficiently large so that all the statements in the previous section hold. The following result is due to
[
Zho20
]
:

Proposition 13
.

The Liouville vector field
Z=u\partial_{u}
is nowhere tangent to
\mathcal{H}_{t,1}
in a neighbourhood of
\Lambda_{\Sigma^{*}}
.

Proof 2.7
.

The component of
Z
that is symplectically orthogonal to
\mathcal{H}_{t,1}
is positively proportional to the Hamiltonian vector field of
\operatorname{Im}(W_{t,1})
along
\Log_{t}^{-1}(\mathcal{C}_{0})
(and hence along the skeleton, by Proposition
12
) by
[
Zho20
, Proposition 5.3]
, and thus nonvanishing in a neighbourhood of the skeleton.

We now follow
[
GS22
, Corollary 6.2.6]
to construct a sutured Liouville domain (in the sense of
[
GPS20
, Definition 2.14]
) from
(\Log_{t}^{-1}(\mathcal{C}_{0}),\Lambda_{\Sigma^{*}})
. We repeat the argument for clarity and later reference.

Lemma 14
.

There exists a Liouville subdomain
B\subset M^{*}_{\C^{*}}
completing to
M^{*}_{\C^{*}}
, together with a Liouville subdomain
F_{0}\subset\mathcal{H}_{t,1}
completing to
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{t,1}
, such that
F_{0}\subset\partial B
.

Proof 2.8
.

The Liouville flow gives an identification
M^{*}_{\C^{*}}\setminus M^{*}_{S^{1}}\simeq\partial\Log_{t}^{-1}(\mathcal{C}_{0})\times\R
, which identifies
\partial\Log_{t}^{-1}(\mathcal{C}_{0})
with
\partial\Log_{t}^{-1}(\mathcal{C}_{0})\times\{0\}
.
We choose a Liouville subdomain
F_{0}\subset\mathcal{H}_{t,1}
, completing to
\mathcal{H}_{t,1}
, which is a neighbourhood of
\Lambda_{\Sigma^{*}}
on which the Liouville vector field is not tangent to
\mathcal{H}_{t,1}
.
Then, the projection of
F_{0}
to
\partial\Log_{t}^{-1}(\mathcal{C}_{0})
is an immersion, and the projection of
\Lambda_{\Sigma^{*}}
is an embedding; so by shrinking
F_{0}
further, we may arrange that the projection of
F_{0}
is also an embedding.

Thus,
F_{0}
is identified with the graph of a function on a hypersurface in
\partial\Log^{-1}_{t}(\mathcal{C}_{0})
.
Extending the function to all of
\partial\Log_{t}^{-1}
and taking its graph produces the boundary of a new Liouville domain
B
which contains
F_{0}
in its boundary.

From the sutured Liouville domain
(B,F_{0})
, we construct a Liouville sector
S_{0}\subset M^{*}_{\C^{*}}
, in the sense of
[
GPS20
, Definition 2.4]
, in accordance with
[
GPS20
, Definition 2.14]
.
We briefly recall the construction.
We apply the Reeb flow on
\partial B
to obtain a neighbourhood of the form
F_{0}\times\R_{|t|<\epsilon}\subset\partial B
and set
S_{0}\subset M^{*}_{\C^{*}}
to be the complement of the set
\R_{>0}\times\R_{|t|<\epsilon}\times F_{0}
where the first coordinate is the flow coordinate for the Liouville vector field from
F_{0}\times\R_{|t|<\epsilon}\subset\partial B
. The
1
-defining function
I_{0}
is given (on the boundary of the cornered sector) by the linear extension of
-t
.
By construction, we have that there is a small neighbourhood
U
of
F_{0}\subset\partial S_{0}
on which

F_{0}=I_{0}^{-1}(0)\cap U=\left(\operatorname{Im}\,W_{t,1}\right)^{-1}(0)\cap U.

(2.6)

The boundary can then be smoothed as in
[
GPS20
, Remark 2.12]
.
By
[
GPS20
, Lemma 2.32]
, the sector embedding
S_{0}\hookrightarrow(M^{*}_{\C^{*}},F_{0})
presents the pair
(M^{*}_{\C^{*}},F_{0})
, where
F_{0}
here denotes the projection to
\partial_{\infty}M^{*}_{\C^{*}}
of
F_{0}
, as the stopped convex completion of the sector
S_{0}
, that is, as the inclusion of
S_{0}
into the pair
(\bar{S_{0}},F_{0})
where
\bar{S_{0}}
is the convex completion in the sense of
[
GPS20
, §2.7]
, and
F_{0}
is the copy of
F_{0}
lying at
-\infty
in the completion region
F\times\C_{\operatorname{Re}\leq 0}
, where
F:=\widehat{F_{0}}
is the Liouville completion of
F_{0}
.

For the construction in
Section

4.4.1
, we will need one further geometric feature of the sector
S_{0}
.

Lemma 15
.

We may choose the pair
(B,F_{0})
such that there exists a neighbourhood
V
of
{\mathbb{L}}_{\Sigma^{*}}
and an extension of the
1
-defining function
I_{0}
to a cylindrical neighbourhood
\Nbd^{Z}(\partial S_{0})
of
\partial S_{0}
in
M^{*}_{\C^{*}}
such that

I_{0}^{-1}(0)\cap V\cap\Nbd^{Z}(\partial S_{0})=\left(\operatorname{Im}W_{t,1}\right)^{-1}(0)\cap V\cap\Nbd^{Z}(\partial S_{0}).

Proof 2.9
.

We have
Z=Z_{F_{0}}+Z^{\perp}
, where
Z_{F_{0}}
is the Liouville vector field of
F
and
Z^{\perp}
is symplectically orthogonal to
TF_{0}
.
Let
g=\operatorname{Im}W_{t,1}
; then we have that
X_{g}
is positively proportional to
Z^{\perp}
along
\Lambda_{\Sigma^{*}}
by
[
Zho20
, Proposition 5.3]
.
If
R
is the Reeb vector field of
\partial B
, then we have

\displaystyle dg(R)

\displaystyle=\omega(X_{g},R)

\displaystyle\propto\omega(Z^{\perp},R)

\displaystyle=\omega(Z,R)\quad\text{as $\omega(Z_{F_{0}},R)=0$}

\displaystyle=\lambda(R)=1

along
\Lambda_{\Sigma^{*}}
.
It follows that
dg(R)>0
in a neighbourhood of
\Lambda_{\Sigma^{*}}
in
\partial B
.
Hence, by shrinking
F_{0}
and choosing
\epsilon
sufficiently small, we may arrange that
dg(R)>0
along
F_{0}\times\R_{|t|<\epsilon}
.
It follows that the map

F_{0}\times\R_{|t|<\epsilon}\xrightarrow{(\id,g)}F_{0}\times\R_{g}

is a diffeomorphism onto its image.
As
g(f,0)=0
, we may write
t=t(f,g)
as a function of
f\in F_{0}
,
g\in\R
, with
t(f,0)=0
.

We now choose
\Nbd^{Z}(\partial S_{0})
, so that there is a smooth retraction
\pi:\Nbd^{Z}(\partial S_{0})\to\partial S_{0}
.
We choose
V
so that
V\cap\Nbd^{Z}(\partial S_{0})=\pi^{-1}(F\times\R_{|t|<\epsilon})
.
We define
\pi_{F}:V\cap\Nbd(\partial S_{0})\to F_{0}
to be the composition of
\pi
with the projection to
F_{0}
.
We now extend the defining function
I_{0}
to
V\cap\Nbd^{Z}(\partial S_{0})
by setting it equal to
I_{0}(x)=-t(\pi_{F}(x),g(x))
.
Because
I_{0}=-t
along
F_{0}\times\R_{|t|<\epsilon}
by construction, this function extends
I_{0}
; it also has the property that
I_{0}=0
if and only if
g=0
.
We now extend
I_{0}
arbitrarily over the rest of
\Nbd^{Z}(\partial S_{0})
.

3
(Partially) wrapped Fukaya categories, cup and cap functors

3.1
(Partially) wrapped Fukaya categories

Recall from
[
GPS24a
,
GPS20
]
the construction of the partially wrapped Fukaya category
\mathcal{W}(X,\mathfrak{f})
of a Liouville sector
X
stopped along a closed subset
\mathfrak{f}\subset(\partial_{\infty}X)^{\circ}
; when
\mathfrak{f}=\emptyset
we write
\mathcal{W}(X):=\mathcal{W}(X,\emptyset)
. Objects are cylindrical exact Lagrangians
L
in
X
disjoint from
\partial X
and
\mathfrak{f}
. As described in
[
GPS24
, Section 5.3]
, by equipping
X
and then its Lagrangians with grading/orientation data, one can ensure the resulting category is
\Z
-graded and defined over a ring of arbitrary characteristic.

In particular, to the Liouville sector
S_{0}
introduced in Section
2.4
, together with the holomorphic volume form
\Omega_{S_{0}}:=\Omega^{Y}|_{S_{0}}
, we have a
\Z
-graded
\Z
-linear partially wrapped Fukaya category
\mathcal{W}(S_{0})
whose objects are cylindrical exact Lagrangians
L
disjoint from
\partial S_{0}
, equipped with spin structures and gradings relative to
\Omega_{S_{0}}
.
To be precise about the latter choice, we have a squared phase function
L\to S^{1}
defined by the argument of
\Omega_{S_{0}}(v_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge v_{n+1})^{2}
, where
(v_{1},\ldots,v_{n})
is a basis of
TL
; and the grading of
L
consists of a lift of this squared phase function to
\R
. Note that there are natural maps

\mathcal{W}(S_{0})\to\mathcal{W}(M^{*}_{\C^{*}},F_{0})\to\mathcal{W}(M^{*}_{\C^{*}},\mathfrak{f}_{\Sigma^{*}})

(3.1)

which are quasi-equivalences as shown in
[
GPS24a
, Corollary 3.9]
(using the fact that
(M^{*}_{\C^{*}},F_{0})
is the stopped convex completion of
S_{0}
). These quasi-equivalences make it clear that
\mathcal{W}(S_{0})
does not depend on any of the choices involved in constructing
S_{0}
.

{rmk}

The approach taken to gradings and orientations in
[
GPS24
, Section 5.3]
is equivalent, but formulated in a slightly different language.
The grading/orientation data we consider for
S_{0}
consist of the constant map
S_{0}\to K(\Z/2,2)
(we consider spin structures, rather than relative spin structures), together with the map
\mathrm{LGr}(S_{0})\to S^{1}
given by the squared phase map.
It is easy to see from the definitions how our choice of spin structure and grading on a Lagrangian
L
induces a choice of grading/orientation data on
L
in the sense of
[
GPS24
, Section 5.3]
. Moreover, our choice of grading/orientation conventions is compatible with that made in
[
GPS24
, Section 5.3]
, as the tautological polarization of
S_{0}\subset T^{*}M^{*}_{S^{1}}
is a trivial bundle (agreeing with our choice of trivial map to
K(\Z/2,2)
) and has phase
0
with respect to our choice of holomorphic volume form.

We also have a
\Z\oplus M^{*}
-graded version
\mathcal{W}(S_{0})^{M^{*}}
, whose objects are objects of
\mathcal{W}(S_{0})
equipped with an additional lift of
L
to the universal cover of
S_{0}
(an ‘anchoring’, in the terminology of
[
Fuk+10
]
).
The additional grading then arises from
\pi_{1}(S_{0})=H_{1}(S_{0})=M^{*}
(cf.
[
She15
, Section 3.1]
).
There is a fully faithful functor
\mathcal{W}(S_{0})^{M^{*}}\to\mathcal{W}(S_{0})
which forgets the anchoring on objects, and the
M^{*}
-grading on morphisms. We can, of course, apply the same construction to obtain
Z\oplus M^{*}
-graded categories
\mathcal{W}(M^{*}_{\C^{*}})^{M^{*}}
and
\mathcal{W}(M^{*}_{\C^{*}},\mathfrak{f}_{\Sigma^{*}})^{M^{*}}
and a
\Z\oplus M^{*}
-graded analog of (
3.1
).

Associated to the (finite-type, convex) exact symplectic manifold
\mathcal{H}_{t,0}
, together with the holomorphic volume form
\Omega_{\mathcal{H}_{t,0}}:=\Omega^{X}|_{\mathcal{H}_{t,0}}
, we have a
\Z
-graded
\Z
-linear wrapped Fukaya category
\mathcal{W}(\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{t,0})
, whose objects are cylindrical exact Lagrangians equipped with spin structures and gradings relative to
\Omega_{\mathcal{H}_{t,0}}
.
As before, we have a
\Z\oplus M^{*}
-graded version
\mathcal{W}(\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{t,0})^{M^{*}}
, whose objects are objects of
\mathcal{W}(\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{t,0})
equipped with a lift of the composition
L\to\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{t,0}\hookrightarrow M^{*}_{\C^{*}}
to the universal cover of
M^{*}_{\C^{*}}
.

{rmk}

To simplify notation, we will typically write
\mathcal{W}(\mathcal{H}_{t,0})
for the wrapped category
\mathcal{W}(\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{t,0})
. More generally, we will apply the same convention when discussing wrapped categories of other incomplete exact symplectic manifolds that arise in our arguments (which we may do because in all such cases, the completion is well-defined).

{rmk}

As in
Section

3.1
, our choice of grading/orientation data on
\mathcal{H}_{t,0}
can be interpreted in the language of
[
GPS24
, Section 5.3]
. From the equation
\Omega_{\mathcal{H}_{t,0}}\wedge dW_{t,0}=\Omega_{M^{*}_{\C^{*}}}
, we have that our grading/orientation data on
\mathcal{H}_{t,0}
agrees with the grading/orientation data induced by the stable polarization of
\mathcal{H}_{t,0}
coming from the tautological polarization of
T^{*}M^{*}_{S^{1}}
and the trivialization of the normal bundle of
\mathcal{H}_{t,0}
by
dW_{t,0}
.

As it will play a central role going forward, we introduce the abbreviated notation
F=\widehat{F_{0}}=\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{t,1}
. We can then similarly associate to
F
a
\Z
-graded
\Z
-linear wrapped Fukaya category
\mathcal{W}(F)
and a
\Z\oplus M^{*}
-graded version
\mathcal{W}(F)^{M^{*}}
.
However,
F_{0}
is not a holomorphic submanifold, and thus it is more natural to induce grading/orientation data on
F
directly from a stable polarization.
Indeed, as a Liouville hypersurface of
B\subset M^{*}_{\C^{*}}
,
F_{0}
has a natural stable polarization induced by the tautological polarization of
T^{*}M^{*}_{S^{1}}\simeq M^{*}_{\C^{*}}
and the trivialization of the normal bundle of
F_{0}
by the pair
(Z^{\perp},R_{\varphi})
where
Z^{\perp}
is the projection of the Liouville vector field on
M^{*}_{\C^{*}}
to the symplectic orthogonal of
F_{0}
and
R_{\varphi}
is the Reeb vector field on
\partial B
. Equivalently, the trivialization of the normal bundle can be seen as coming from a standard neighbourhood of
\partial S_{0}
produced in
[
GPS20
, Proposition 2.25]
.

In fact, the wrapped Fukaya categories of
\mathcal{H}_{t,0}
and
F
are quasi-equivalent:

Lemma 16
.

There is a
\Z\oplus M^{*}
-graded quasi-equivalence

\mathcal{W}(\mathcal{H}_{t,0})^{M^{*}}\simeq\mathcal{W}(F)^{M^{*}}

for any sufficiently large
t
.

Proof 3.1
.

From
Lemma

9
, we have a Liouville isomorphism
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{t,0}\simeq\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{t,1}=F
, resulting in an
M^{*}
-graded quasi-equivalence. It remains only to check that the Liouville isomorphism respects our chosen grading/orientation data. To show this, we work in terms of stable polarizations (see
Section

3.1
and
Section

3.1
).

Through the deformation from
\mathcal{H}_{t,0}
to
\mathcal{H}_{t,1}
via the family
\mathcal{H}_{t,s}
, we have a homotopy of grading/orientation data via the stable polarization of
\mathcal{H}_{t,s}
obtained from the tautological polarization of
T^{*}M^{*}_{S^{1}}
and the trivialization of the normal bundle to
\mathcal{H}_{t,s}
by
dW_{t,s}
. We now must compare this grading/orientation data on
\mathcal{H}_{t,1}
with that on
F
. However,
[
Zho20
, Proposition 5.3]
shows that on a subset of
\mathcal{H}_{t,1}
homotopy equivalent to both
F
and
\mathcal{H}_{t,1}
the Hamiltonian vector field
\mathsf{X}_{\operatorname{Im}\,W_{t,1}}
of
\operatorname{Im}\,W_{t,1}
is positively proportional to
Z^{\perp}
. Thus, the oriented frames
\left(Z^{\perp},R_{\varphi}\right)
and
\left(\mathsf{X}_{\operatorname{Im}\,W_{t,1}},\mathsf{X}_{\operatorname{Re}\,W_{t,1}}\right)
cannot rotate relative to each other around any cycle, i.e., they induce homotopic trivializations of the normal bundle.

{rmk}

The equivalence of the grading/orientation data in the proof of
Lemma

16
and part of the isomorphism of Liouville structures have recently also been proved in
[
SB24
, Theorem 9.2]
.

3.2
Cup functor

By virtue of the fact that
S_{0}\subset(M^{*}_{\C^{*}},F_{0})
is the inclusion into the stopped convex completion of
S_{0}
, the complement of
S_{0}
can be identified with the stopped Liouville sector
(F\times\C_{\operatorname{Re}\leq 0},F_{0})
, where
F\times\C_{\operatorname{Re}\leq 0}\hookrightarrow M^{*}_{\C^{*}}
is the canonical neighborhood of
F_{0}\subset\partial_{\infty}M^{*}_{\C^{*}}
.
The sector inclusion
(F\times\C_{\operatorname{Re}\leq 0},F_{0})\hookrightarrow(M^{*}_{\C^{*}},F_{0})
induces (after appeal to
[
GPS24a
, Corollary 3.9]
) a functor
\mathcal{W}(F\times\C_{\operatorname{Re}\leq 0},\mathfrak{f}_{\Sigma^{*}})\to\mathcal{W}(M^{*}_{\C^{*}},\mathfrak{f}_{\Sigma^{*}})
. By composing with stabilization
[
GPS24a
, §8.4]
, we obtain a functor
\mathcal{W}(F)\to\mathcal{W}(M^{*}_{\C^{*}},\mathfrak{f}_{\Sigma^{*}})
, which we call in this paper the cup functor
\cup
(compare
[
GPS24
, §7.3]
,
[
GPS24a
, Example 10.7]
,
[
Syl19
, §2.4]
).

There is an equivalent realization of the
\cup
functor with target the quasi-equivalent
\mathcal{W}(S)
, where
S
is a small deformation of
S_{0}
(deformed along the lines of
[
GPS20
, Proposition 2.28]
) which has
exact boundary
in the sense of
[
GPS20
, Definition 2.10]
. From the fact that
S
has exact boundary, one obtains an embedding of Liouville sectors
F\times T^{*}[0,1]\hookrightarrow S
covering
\Nbd^{Z}\partial S
— called here a
boundary sector embedding
— whose induced pushforward can then be composed with stabilization to define
\cup:\mathcal{W}(F)\to\mathcal{W}(S)
.

In
Lemma

17
below, we recall a particular method of constructing the deformation
S
, close to that appearing in the proof of
[
GPS24a
, Theorem 1.28]
, as a subsector of
(M^{*}_{\C^{*}},F_{0})
contained in an arbitrarily small cylindrical neighborhood of
S_{0}
with boundary outside
S_{0}
. We also review how the deformation argument provides us with an extended defining function
I
on
S
which has controlled deviation from, and some compatibilities with, our previously fixed extended 1-defining function
I_{0}
for
\partial S_{0}
. We define
(S,I)
to be the outcome of applying the following lemma to our
(B,F_{0})\subset M^{*}_{\C^{*}}
with associated sector and 1-defining function
(S_{0},I_{0})
constructed in
Section

2.4
and
Lemma

15
. We will use the third property of
(S,I)
below in
Section

4.4
.

Lemma 17
.

Let
(B,F_{0})
be a sutured Liouville domain with
B
completing to a Liouville manifold
M
, with
S_{0}\subset M
the associated Liouville sector.
Let
I_{0}
be any extended defining function defined in a neighbourhood of
\partial S_{0}
within
M
(for instance, the one from §
2.4
and
Lemma

15
).
In that setting, there exists a small deformation
S_{0}\subset S\subset(M,F_{0})
, along with an extended 1-defining function
I
defined near
\partial S
satisfying the following properties:

1.

S
is contained in
S_{0}^{+}:=
an arbitrarily small outward
X_{I_{0}}
pushoff of
S_{0}
.

2.

There is a boundary sector embedding
F\times T^{*}[0,1]\hookrightarrow S
(and in particular
S
has exact boundary) living in the complement of
S_{0}
and fitting into a commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\times T^{*}[0,1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(M\backslash S_{0}^{\circ},F_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(M,F_{0})}

(3.2)

where the horizontal inclusions induce equivalences on wrapped Fukaya categories.

3.

There is a Hamiltonian vector field
V
on
S\backslash S_{0}^{\circ}
which (while not necessarily linear near infinity on the whole region) agrees with
X_{I}
near
\partial S
and
X_{I_{0}}
near
\partial S_{0}
and carries
\partial S_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\partial S
.

Proof 3.2
.

We begin with
S_{0}\hookrightarrow(M,F_{0})
and
I_{0}
as previously constructed. Let
S_{0}^{+}
be the outward flow of
S_{0}
by
X_{I_{0}}
for some (unspecified)
small time
a>0
.
On the region

S_{0}^{+}\backslash S_{0}^{\circ}=\Nbd^{Z}(\partial S_{0}^{+})
,
I_{0}
determines
an identification with
(F\times T^{*}[0,a],\lambda_{F}+\lambda_{T^{*}[0,a]}+df)
for some function
f
satisfying properties as in
[
GPS20
, Proposition
2.25]
and where
F=\widehat{F_{0}}
; importantly,
f
may not be compactly supported
(otherwise we’d be done). After rescaling
I_{0}
, we identify
T^{*}[0,a]=T^{*}[0,1]
for simplicity.

Next, as in the proofs of
[
GPS24a
, Theorem 1.28]
and

[
GPS20
, Proposition 2.28]
(and closer in construction to
the former), we deform the Liouville one-form
\lambda\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lambda_{t}}}{{\leadsto}}\lambda^{\prime}
within the
F\times T^{*}[0,1]
region above to turn off the
df
term in a
subshell
F\times T^{*}[\frac{2}{5},\frac{3}{5}]
without changing
\lambda

outside of
F\times T^{*}[\frac{1}{5},\frac{4}{5}]
. Concretely we take the straight line homotopy between
\lambda_{0}:=\lambda
and
\lambda_{1}:=\lambda^{\prime}=\lambda_{F}+\lambda_{T^{*}[0,1]}+d(\varphi(s)f)
where
\varphi(s)=1
for
s
outside
[\frac{1}{5},\frac{4}{5}]
and
\varphi(s)=0
for
s
inside
[\frac{2}{5},\frac{3}{5}]
, and extend this to a homotopy
\lambda_{t}
on
M
which is constant outside this region.
The family
\lambda_{t}
is a convex-at-infinity deformation of Liouville structures as in loc. cit. It therefore induces a Liouville isomorphism
\Phi:(M,\lambda_{1})\to(M,\lambda_{0})
, which is identity inside
S_{0}
and outside
S_{0}^{+}
by
[
CE12
, Proposition 11.8]
.
Now with respect to the new Liouville form
\lambda_{1}
consider
S_{1}:=S_{0}\cup(F\times T^{*}[0,\frac{3}{5}])
which sits between
S_{0}
and
S_{0}^{+}
and is a Liouville subsector of
(M,\lambda_{1})
. We observe the following:

•

S_{1}
is contained in
S_{0}^{+}
;

•

S_{1}
has a boundary sector embedding
(F\times T^{*}[\frac{2}{5},\frac{3}{5}],\lambda_{F}+\lambda_{T^{*}[\frac{2}{5},\frac{3}{5}]})\hookrightarrow S_{1}
which lives in
S_{1}\backslash S_{0}^{\circ}
. Now identify
T^{*}[\frac{2}{5},\frac{3}{5}]
(by scaling up in the base and shrinking in the fiber) with
T^{*}[0,1]
.

•

The inclusion of
F\times T^{*}[\frac{2}{5},\frac{3}{5}]
into
S_{0}^{+}\backslash S_{0}^{\circ}
is deformation equivalent to a trivial inclusion, and hence by
[
GPS24a
, Lemma 3.4]
induces an equivalence on wrapped Fukaya categories;

•

Similarly, the inclusion of
S_{1}
into
S_{0}^{+}
is deformation equivalent to a trivial inclusion, and hence induces an equivalence on wrapped Fukaya categories.

•

The vector field
X_{I_{0}}
is a Hamiltonian vector field (indeed we haven’t changed
\omega
!) taking
\partial S_{0}
to
\partial S_{1}
. The associated Hamiltonian function
I_{0}
is an extended 1-defining function near
\partial S_{0}
and
\partial S_{1}
, but may fail to be linear on all of
S_{1}\backslash S_{0}^{\circ}
(given our modifications to
\lambda
).

Now push forward by
\Phi
: let
S:=\Phi(S_{1})
with extended 1-defining function
I:=\Phi_{*}(I_{0})
. Since
\Phi|_{S_{0}}=id
and
\Phi|_{M\backslash(S_{0}^{+})^{\circ}}=id
, we see that
S
satisfies
Item

1
.
The remaining conditions other than the top horizontal arrow of
Eq.

3.2
inducing an equivalence follow immediately from taking
\Phi
of the above bullet points.
To study the effect of the top horizontal arrow of
Eq.

3.2
on wrapped Fukaya categories, first use
\Phi
of the third bullet point to obtain an equivalence
\mathcal{W}(F\times T^{*}[0,1])\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathcal{W}(S_{0}^{+}\backslash S_{0}^{\circ})
.

The desired equivalence therefore follows from (by composing with) the claim that the sector embedding
(S_{0}^{+}\backslash S_{0}^{\circ})\hookrightarrow(M\backslash S_{0}^{\circ},F_{0})
induces an isomorphism on wrapped Fukaya categories.
To establish the claim, it suffices to replace
S_{0}^{+}
by the sufficiently small time outward flow
(S_{0}^{+})^{\prime}
of any other extended 1-defining function
I^{\prime}
by the following sandwiching argument, compare
[
GPS24a
, Lemma 3.4]
: Denote by
\Nbd^{Z}(\partial S_{0})_{I^{\prime}}^{+a}
the complement of
S_{0}^{\circ}
in the outward time-
a
flow of
S_{0}
by
X_{I^{\prime}}
. Then observe that for any
I_{1}
,
I_{2}
there exist sufficiently small
a<c
,
b<d
and a sequence of inclusions
\Nbd^{Z}(\partial S_{0})_{I_{1}}^{+a}\hookrightarrow\Nbd^{Z}(\partial S_{0})_{I_{2}}^{+b}\hookrightarrow\Nbd^{Z}(\partial S_{0})_{I_{1}}^{+c}\hookrightarrow\Nbd^{Z}(\partial S_{0})_{I_{2}}^{+d}
. Since any composition of adjacent arrows
\Nbd^{Z}(\partial S_{0})_{I_{j}}^{+i_{1}}\hookrightarrow\Nbd^{Z}(\partial S_{0})_{I_{j}}^{+i_{2}}
is a trivial inclusion, inducing an equivalence on wrapped Fukaya categories
[
GPS20
, Lemma 3.41]
, it follows that
\mathcal{W}(\Nbd^{Z}(\partial S_{0})_{I_{1}}^{+a})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathcal{W}(\Nbd^{Z}(\partial S_{0})_{I_{2}}^{+b})
.

It is now enough to show the claim holds after replacing
I_{0}
with the extended 1-defining function
I_{0}^{\prime}=-e^{s}t
, with associated
vector field
X_{I_{0}^{\prime}}=-\frac{\partial}{\partial s}+t\frac{\partial}{\partial t}+Z_{\lambda_{F_{0}}}
.
Here recall that in local coordinates
S_{0}
is the complement
of the set
(\R_{>0})_{s}\times\R_{|t|<\epsilon}\times F_{0}
in
M
(with smoothed boundary) where
s
is the symplectization and
t
is a Reeb coordinate as in
the previous section, and the Liouville one-form is
\lambda=e^{s}(dt-\lambda_{F_{0}})
.
The outward flow
S_{0}^{+}
of
S_{0}
by
X_{I_{0}}
for a small
time
a>0

has
\partial S_{0}^{+}
equal to (a smoothing of) the boundary of
the set
(\R_{>a})_{s}\times\R_{|t|<e^{-a}\epsilon}\times{F_{0}}^{-a}
, where

F_{0}^{-a}
denotes the result of inward flow by
Z_{\lambda_{F_{0}}}
.
In particular,
S^{+}
is the sector constructed from the sutured pair
(B^{+a},F_{0}^{-a})\subset M
using the neighborhood
\R_{|t|<e^{-a}\epsilon}\times{F_{0}}^{-a}
. Hence
(M,F_{0})
is up to deformation the stopped convex completion of
S^{+}
by
[
GPS20
, Lemma 2.32]
, and therefore also the convex completion along the boundary component
\partial S^{+}
of
S^{+}\backslash S_{0}^{\circ}
. In particular, we may apply
[
GPS24a
, Corollary 3.9]
to the inclusion
(S_{0}^{+}\backslash S_{0}^{\circ})\hookrightarrow(M\backslash S_{0}^{\circ},F_{0})
(note while loc. cit. is stated for the “full” stopped completion of a sector, it also applies immediately to the stopped convex completion along a single boundary component of a sector with disconnected boundary) to obtain
an equivalence
\mathcal{W}(S_{0}^{+}\backslash S_{0}^{\circ})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathcal{W}(M\backslash S_{0}^{\circ},F_{0}^{-a})
which factors through the equivalence (by loc. cit. again)
\mathcal{W}(M\backslash S_{0}^{\circ},F_{0})\to\mathcal{W}(M\backslash S_{0}^{\circ},F_{0}^{-a})
. Hence
\mathcal{W}(S_{0}^{+}\backslash S_{0}^{\circ})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathcal{W}(M\backslash S_{0}^{\circ},F_{0})
is an equivalence.

Using
S
and
F\times T^{*}[0,1]\hookrightarrow S
given to us in
Lemma

17
, we immediately obtain a boundary inclusion functor

\mathcal{W}(F\times T^{*}[0,1])\to\mathcal{W}(S)

(3.3)

fitting into a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{W}(F\times T^{*}[0,1])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{W}(F\times\C_{\operatorname{Re}\leq 0},\mathfrak{f}_{\Sigma^{*}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{W}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{W}(M^{*}_{\C^{*}},\mathfrak{f}_{\Sigma^{*}})}

(3.4)

where the horizontal arrows are equivalences. Here we have implicitly applied
[
GPS24a
, Corollary 3.9]
and also used the fact that
(M\backslash S_{0}^{\circ},F_{0})

up to deformation is a model for the canonical
F\times\C_{\operatorname{Re}\geq 0}
neighborhood of
F_{0}
in
M
at infinity
[
GPS20
, Lemma 2.32]
.

Next, there is a Künneth/stabilization embedding
stab:\mathcal{W}(F)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathcal{W}(F\times T^{*}[0,1])

[
GPS24a
, §8.4]
, defined by multiplying with a cotangent fiber using the Künneth embedding

stab:\mathcal{W}(F)\xrightarrow{-\otimes\R}\mathcal{W}(F)\otimes\mathcal{W}(T^{*}[0,1])\xrightarrow{\text{K\"{u}nneth}}\mathcal{W}(F\times T^{*}[0,1]).

Here we are considering the object
\R
of
\mathcal{W}(T^{*}[0,1])
given by the cotangent fibre
\{x=\frac{1}{2}\}
, equipped with the standard spin structure (which in particular induces the standard orientation on
\R
).
The squared phase function is then constant equal to
2\mathrm{arg}(\Omega_{T^{*}[0,1]}(\partial_{y}))=\pi\in S^{1}
.
We choose the grading to be equal to
\pi\in\R
.
Note that these choices agree with the grading/orientation data on
T^{*}[0,1]
and
\R
induced by the tautological polarization of
T^{*}[0,1]
.

Composing with the inclusion/equivalence
\mathcal{W}(F\times T^{*}[0,1])\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathcal{W}(F\times\C_{\operatorname{Re}\leq 0},\mathfrak{f}_{\Sigma^{*}})
gives a stabilization functor
stab:\mathcal{W}(F)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathcal{W}(F\times\C_{\operatorname{Re}\leq 0},\mathfrak{f}_{\Sigma^{*}})
.
5
5

5

It is tempting to pick the stabilization functors
stab:\mathcal{W}(F)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathcal{W}(F\times T^{*}[0,1])
and
stab:\mathcal{W}(F)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathcal{W}(F\times\C_{\operatorname{Re}\leq 0},\mathfrak{f}_{\Sigma^{*}})
completely independently and then argue that they are the “same” after including the first codomain into the second. While this is expected to hold, it is not shown in
[
GPS24a
]
that Künneth is functorial under arbitrary sector inclusions or independent of all choices made. However, in order to apply the results of
[
GPS24
]
later, we are free to pick one such stabilization functor once and for all.
The needed structural results from
loc. cit.
— articulated in
Theorem

28
below — hold for any such choice.

As in
[
Syl19
, Section 2.4]
, we then define the cup functor

\cup:\mathcal{W}(F)\to\mathcal{W}(S)

(3.5)

by composing
stab
with (
3.3
). At the start of this subsection, we defined
\cup
as the composition
\mathcal{W}(F)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle stab}}{{\to}}\mathcal{W}(F\times\C_{\operatorname{Re}\leq 0},\mathfrak{f}_{\Sigma^{*}})\to\mathcal{W}(M^{*}_{\C^{*}},\mathfrak{f}_{\Sigma^{*}})
. The commutative diagram (
3.3
) immediately implies:

Corollary 18
.

For
S
as in the previous Lemma, there is a commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{W}(F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{W}(F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{W}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{W}(M^{*}_{\C^{*}},\mathfrak{f}_{\Sigma^{*}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cup}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cup}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

(3.6)

Note that
\cup
is a
\Z
-graded functor with respect to the natural
grading/orientation data on
F
described in
Section

3.1
. As
described in
Section

3.1
for
\mathcal{W}(S_{0})
, there is also a
\Z\oplus M^{*}
graded version
\mathcal{W}(S)^{M^{*}}
of the wrapped Fukaya category of

S
(with
\mathcal{W}(S_{0})^{M^{*}}\to\mathcal{W}(S)^{M^{*}}
a quasi-equivalence), and it is straightforward
to enhance (
3.5
) to a
\Z\oplus M^{*}
-graded functor

\cup^{M^{*}}:\mathcal{W}(F)^{M^{*}}\to\mathcal{W}(S)^{M^{*}}

(3.7)

3.3
Cap functor

We can associate to the cup functor (
3.5
) a formal adjoint functor
\cup^{*}:\mathcal{W}(S)\to\operatorname{Mod}\mathcal{W}(F)
by composing the pullback of modules with Yoneda. In good situations, this functor is representable (i.e., there is an adjoint to the
\cup
-functor).
For example, in the setting of symplectic Landau-Ginzburg models, it is known that the cup functor is spherical
[
AG
,
Syl19
]
, and therefore has both left and right adjoints. The right adjoint is usually called the
cap
functor as for a class of Lagrangian submanifolds
L\subset S_{0}
it can be geometrically described as “intersection of
L
with the fiber
F
”.
Several incarnations of this functor have been studied by various authors such as
[
AS
,
AG
,
AS19
,
Sei24
]
with different geometric hypotheses.
In the remainder of this section, we define the version of the cap functor that we will use on a particular class of Lagrangians that we now define.

Definition 19

(Restatement of
Definition

92
)
.

Let
L\subset S
be a compact Lagrangian submanifold with
\partial L\subset\partial S
. We say that
L
is
I
-arclike
if
L
is transverse to
\partial S
, and
I
is constant along some neighbourhood of
\partial L
in
L
. Here
I
is the same extended
1
-defining function which was used in the definition of the cup functor.

In
Appendix

A
, we introduce a subcategory
\mathcal{P}(S)\subset\mathcal{W}(S)
of
negative pushoffs of (
I
-)arclike Lagrangians
, and show that intersection with the fiber
F
is adjoint to the cup functor. More precisely, in
Theorem

84
, we show there exists an
A_{\infty}
functor

\cap:\mathcal{P}(S)\to\mathcal{F}(F)

which sends a negative-pushoff
L^{-}
of an arclike Lagrangian
L
to the boundary
\partial L
(which is compact, and in particular defines an object of the compact Fukaya category
\mathcal{F}(F)\subset\mathcal{W}(F)
).
Furthermore, there exist functorial isomorphisms

\mathrm{Hom}_{H\mathcal{W}(S)}(\cup K,L)\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{H\mathcal{W}(F)}(K,\cap L)

for all objects
K
of
\mathcal{W}(F)
,
L
of
\mathcal{P}(S)
which lift to an equivalence of suitable
\mathcal{W}(F)\!-\!\mathcal{P}(S)
bimodules on the chain level.
Thus,
\cap
behaves like a right adjoint to
\cup
restricted to
\mathcal{P}(S)
. We say that
\cap
is a
partially-defined right adjoint
to
\cup
defined along
\mathcal{P}(S)
, in the terminology of
Appendix

A
. The construction of cap is agnostic as to the grading structures imposed upon our categories; in particular, it also gives us a
\Z\oplus M^{*}
graded version of
\cap
:

\cap^{M^{*}}:\mathcal{P}(S)^{M^{*}}\to\mathcal{F}(F)^{M^{*}}.

(3.8)

4

In this section, we recall and analyze homological mirror symmetry relating the sector
S_{0}
and its boundary fiber
F
on the A-side to
Y^{*}
and its toric boundary
\partial Y^{*}
on the B-side.

33

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{30.61684pt}{-19.0992pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 42.36 -26.43)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
