and the convergence of the spectral sequence.

Lemma 57
.

We have a canonical surjective map

\displaystyle\Lambda^{2}M_{\Bbbk}\to H^{2\oplus 0}_{log}(X_{t,\kappa},D),

(6.16)

and, for any
\mathsf{p}\in P
, a canonical isomorphism

\displaystyle M_{\Bbbk}\cong H^{3\oplus\mathsf{p}}_{log}(X_{t,\kappa},D).

(6.17)

Proof 6.7
.

Note that the restriction map
\Lambda^{i}M_{\Bbbk}\cong H^{i}(M^{*}_{\C^{*}};\Bbbk)\to H^{i}(\mathcal{H}_{t,0};\Bbbk)
is surjective for
i<\dim_{\C}(X)
by the affine Lefschetz hyperplane theorem
[
Dim92
, Theorem 6.5]
, and in particular for
i=2
, by our assumption that
\dim_{\C}(X)\geq 3
. This gives rise to (
6.16
).

Let
\mathring{Y}_{\mathsf{p}}\subset Y_{\kappa}
denote the complement of all of the divisors except
D_{\mathsf{p}}^{Y}.
The real oriented blowup
\hat{Y}_{\mathsf{p}}
of
\mathring{Y}_{\mathsf{p}}
along the open stratum of
D_{\mathsf{p}}^{Y}
is then a manifold with boundary
S_{\mathsf{p}}^{Y}
, the normal circle bundle over the open stratum of
D_{\mathsf{p}}^{Y}
. It is not difficult to see that
S_{\mathsf{p}}^{Y}
is homeomorphic to
M^{*}_{S^{1}}\times\Delta_{\mathsf{p}}
, where
\Delta_{\mathsf{p}}
is the open codimension-one face of the moment polytope of
Y_{\kappa}
, and the inclusion map
S_{\mathsf{p}}^{Y}\to\hat{Y}_{\mathsf{p}}
is a homotopy equivalence. The bundle
SD_{\mathsf{p}}
is also a trivial
S^{1}
-bundle over the interior of
D_{\mathsf{p}}
, so again employing the affine Lefschetz hyperplane theorem (together with the Künneth formula), we have that the restriction map
M_{\Bbbk}\cong H^{1}(S_{\mathsf{p}}^{Y};\Bbbk)\to H^{1}(SD_{\mathsf{p}};\Bbbk)
is an isomorphism, yielding (
6.17
).

Proposition 58
.

For any
\mathsf{p}\in P
, the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces SH^{2\oplus 0}(\mathcal{H}_{t,0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces SH^{3\oplus\mathsf{p}}(\mathcal{H}_{t,0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda^{2}M_{\Bbbk}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{\Bbbk}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{\mathsf{p}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces PSS}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mathsf{p}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces PSS_{log}}

(6.18)

Here, the map
L_{\mathsf{p}}
denotes the map
[PSS_{log}(\gamma_{\mathsf{p}}),-]
given by Lie bracket with
PSS_{log}(\gamma_{\mathsf{p}})
, and
\iota_{\mathsf{p}}
denotes the map given by contracting with
\mathsf{p}\in M^{*}.

Proof 6.8
.

Let
\alpha
be a class in
H^{2}(\mathcal{H}_{t,0})
and let
\Delta
denote the BV operator on symplectic cohomology. It is standard that
\Delta(PSS(\alpha))=0
; furthermore,
\Delta(PSS_{log}(\gamma_{\mathsf{p}}))=0
by

[
GP21
, Lemma 4.29]
. Applying the BV-equation, we therefore have

\displaystyle[PSS_{log}(\gamma_{\mathsf{p}}),PSS(\alpha)]=\Delta(PSS(\alpha)*PSS_{log}(\gamma_{\mathsf{p}})).

(6.19)

Applying Proposition
55
, this becomes:

\displaystyle[PSS_{log}(\gamma_{\mathsf{p}}),PSS(\alpha)]=\Delta(PSS_{log}(r_{\mathsf{p}}^{*}(\alpha))),

(6.20)

where in this equation we view
r_{\mathsf{p}}^{*}(\alpha)
as an element in
H^{4\oplus\mathsf{p}}_{log}(X_{t,\kappa},D)
.

To compute the BV-operator, note that the
S^{1}
-action on
SD_{\mathsf{p}}
induces a degree
-1
cohomology operation:

\displaystyle B_{\mathsf{p}}:H^{*}(SD_{\mathsf{p}})\to H^{*-1}(SD_{\mathsf{p}}).

(6.21)

By functoriality of the real oriented blowup
[
AK10
]
, we have a commutative square:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces SD_{\mathsf{p}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{\mathsf{p}}^{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{X}_{\mathsf{p}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{Y}_{\mathsf{p}}}

(6.22)

It follows
r_{p}^{*}(\alpha)
can also be viewed as the restriction of a class on
S_{\mathsf{p}}^{Y}\cong M^{*}_{S^{1}}\times\Delta_{\mathsf{p}}.
Observe that all of the Borel–Moore homology classes on
M^{*}_{S^{1}}\times\Delta_{\mathsf{p}}
can be canonically represented by
\Bbbk
-linear combinations of submanifolds. In particular, by intersecting these submanifolds with
SD_{\mathsf{p}}
, we see that
r_{p}^{*}(\alpha)
is Poincaré dual to a class which can be represented by
\Bbbk
-linear combinations of proper maps from a smooth, oriented manifold. We can therefore combine (
6.20
) with
[
GP21
, Lemma 4.29]
again to show that

\displaystyle[PSS_{log}(\gamma_{\mathsf{p}}),PSS(\alpha)]=PSS_{log}(B_{\mathsf{p}}(r_{\mathsf{p}}^{*}(\alpha))).

(6.23)

To conclude, note that the inclusion of circle bundles
SD_{\mathsf{p}}\subset S_{\mathsf{p}}^{Y}
is
S^{1}
-equivariant and consequently there is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda^{2}M_{\Bbbk}\cong H^{2}(S_{\mathsf{p}}^{Y})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(SD_{\mathsf{p}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{\Bbbk}\cong H^{1}(S_{\mathsf{p}}^{Y})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(SD_{\mathsf{p}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{\mathsf{p}}^{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{\mathsf{p}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

(6.24)

On
S_{\mathsf{p}}^{Y}
, the
S^{1}
-action is just given by the action of the circle
\operatorname{exp}(\mathsf{p})
(here, we are again identifying
S_{\mathsf{p}}^{Y}
with
M^{*}_{S^{1}}\times\Delta_{\mathsf{p}}
). It is then straightforward to see that
B_{\mathsf{p}}^{Y}=\iota_{\mathsf{p}}
from which the result follows immediately.

6.2
Relative Fukaya category

Since we assume the connectedness condition holds, the intersection of each component
D^{Y}_{\mathsf{p}}
with
X_{t,\kappa}
is either connected (when
\mathsf{p}\in P
) or empty (otherwise), by Lemma
4
.
Thus we have
H^{2}(X_{t,\kappa},X_{t,\kappa}\setminus D;\R)\cong\R^{P}
.
Recall the notion of a ‘nice’ cone
\mathsf{N}\subset\R^{P}
from
[
She20a
, Definition 3.20]
.

Lemma 59
.

There exists a nice cone
\mathsf{N}\subset\R^{P}
containing
\kappa
in its interior.

Proof 6.9
.

Follows from
[
She20a
, Corollary 3.34]
, by the connectedness condition and the fact that
\kappa
was restricted from a relative Kähler form on
(Y_{\kappa},D^{Y})
.

We will consider the relative Fukaya category
\EuF(X_{t,\kappa},D,\mathsf{N})
, defined as in
[
PS23
]
.
There are some choices involved in the construction, which we now specify.
First, the category will be
\Z\oplus M^{*}
-graded.
To define its coefficient ring, let
\mathrm{NE}_{A}\subset H_{2}(X_{t,\kappa},X_{t,\kappa}\setminus D)\cong\Z^{P}
be the cone dual to
\mathsf{N}
and let
\Bbbk[\mathrm{NE}_{A}]
be the monoid ring generated by
\mathrm{NE}_{A}
.
For
u\in\mathrm{NE}_{A}
, we equip the generator
r^{u}\in\Bbbk[\mathrm{NE}_{A}]
with the grading
0\oplus[\partial u]
, where
[\partial u]
is the class of the boundary of
u
in
H_{1}(M^{*}_{\C^{*}})=M^{*}
. The coefficient ring of the relative Fukaya category will be
R_{A}=\Bbbk[[\mathrm{NE}_{A}]]
, the
\Z\oplus M^{*}
-graded completion of
\Bbbk[\mathrm{NE}_{A}]
.

The relative Fukaya category
\EuF(X_{t,\kappa},D,\mathsf{N})

[
PS23
,
She20a
]
is a
\Z\oplus M^{*}
-graded,
R_{A}
-linear, curved filtered
A_{\infty}
category.
It is a deformation of the
\Bbbk
-linear exact Fukaya category
\EuF(X_{t,\kappa}\setminus D)
. Let us recall the key elements of the construction. Let
h_{1}=h^{X}
denote the Kähler potential for
\omega
chosen in
Section

2.1
and let
c
be a constant which is larger than the biggest critical value of
h_{1}
. Then the subspace

\mathcal{S}_{1}:=\{h_{1}\leq c\}

is a Liouville domain with completion
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{t,0}
. An object of the relative Fukaya category is a compact exact Lagrangian
L\subset\mathcal{S}_{1}
, equipped with a grading and spin structure.
The grading consists of a lift of the phase function
L\to S^{1}=\R/\Z
determined by
\Omega^{X}
to
\R
, together with a lift of
L
to the covering space of
X_{t,\kappa}\setminus D
classified by the map

\pi_{1}(X_{t,\kappa}\setminus D)\to H_{1}(M^{*}\otimes_{\Z}\C^{*})=M^{*}.

Morphisms are Hamiltonian chords between Lagrangians.
The grading of the Lagrangians allows us to specify a degree of each chord (in the grading group
\Z\oplus M^{*}
), so that our morphism spaces are graded
R_{A}
-modules.
The definition of the
\Z
-grading, using the lift of the phase function, is standard; the
M^{*}
-grading is defined to be the difference between the lifts of the Lagrangians at the ends of the chord. Let
\mathcal{A}_{R}
denote the resulting curved deformation of the category
\mathcal{A}_{0}
.

Let
\mathsf{p}\in P
, and
r_{\mathsf{p}}\cdot a_{\mathsf{p}}
be the corresponding first-order deformation class of
\mathcal{A}_{R}
. In the discussion below, we recall only those technical details concerning the definition of this class which will be relevant to the proof of Proposition
61
— for full details, see
[
She20a
, §4.4]
.
We set
\mathcal{J}(X_{t,\kappa})
to be the space of
\omega
-compatible almost-complex structures on
X_{t,\kappa}
. By
Lemma

53
, we can choose a different Kähler potential
h_{2}
for
\omega
which is infinite along
\cup_{\mathsf{q}\neq\mathsf{p}}D_{\mathsf{q}}.
We let
\mathcal{S}_{2}
be a Liouville domain corresponding to
h_{2}
which is chosen so that there is a proper containment:

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{1}\subset\mathcal{S}_{2}.

(6.25)

Set
\lambda_{1}=-dh_{1}\circ J_{0}
and
\lambda_{2}=-dh_{2}\circ J_{0}
, where
J_{0}
denotes the standard integrable almost-complex structure.

Using the Liouville flow, we can and will assume that all of the Lagrangians
L_{i}
lie in
\mathcal{S}_{1}.
We let
\mathbf{L}=(L^{(0)},...,L^{(s)})
be a tuple of objects in
\mathcal{A}_{0}
. We set
\mathfrak{R}_{i}(\mathbf{L})
to be the moduli space of holomorphic discs with boundary punctures
\zeta_{0},\zeta_{1},...,\zeta_{s}
in cyclic order and
i
interior marked points, where
i=0
or
1
, modulo biholomorphisms of the disc; when
i=1
, we denote the interior marked point by
z_{int}
. We label the boundary components of the discs by
L^{(j)}
. We denote the universal curve by
U_{\mathfrak{R}_{i}(\mathbf{L})}\to\mathfrak{R}_{i}(\mathbf{L})
. We make universal choices of strip-like ends
\varepsilon_{j}
at each puncture (one negative end at
\zeta_{0}
, the remaining ends positive). We let:

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{\mathfrak{R}_{i}(\mathbf{L})}:=\Omega^{1}(U_{\mathfrak{R}_{i}(\mathbf{L})}/\mathfrak{R}_{i}(\mathbf{L}),C^{\infty}(X_{t,\kappa})),\quad\mathcal{J}_{\mathfrak{R}_{i}(\mathbf{L})}:=C^{\infty}(\mathcal{U}_{\mathfrak{R}_{i}(\mathbf{L})},\mathcal{J}(X_{t,\kappa})).

(6.26)

We choose universal families of perturbation data
(K,J)\in\mathcal{K}_{\mathfrak{R}_{i}(\mathbf{L})}\times\mathcal{J}_{\mathfrak{R}_{i}(\mathbf{L})}.
As usual, these choices (including the choice of strip-like ends) must be made consistently with previously chosen Floer data and the gluing operation for surfaces.

Our arguments will involve working with different classes of perturbation data.
For the definition of the affine Fukaya category
\EuF(X_{t,\kappa}\setminus D)
, and the first-order deformation class
a_{\mathsf{p}}
, we assume that
(K,J)\in\mathcal{K}^{aff}_{\mathfrak{R}_{i}(\mathbf{L})}\times\mathcal{J}^{aff}_{\mathfrak{R}_{i}(\mathbf{L})}
, where

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}^{aff}_{\mathfrak{R}_{i}(\mathbf{L})}

\displaystyle:=\{K\in\mathcal{K}_{\mathfrak{R}_{i}(\mathbf{L})},K_{r}=0\text{ outside }\mathcal{S}_{1}\text{ for all }r\in\mathfrak{R}_{i}(\mathbf{L})\}

(6.27)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}^{aff}_{\mathfrak{R}_{i}(\mathbf{L})}

\displaystyle:=\{J\in\mathcal{J}_{\mathfrak{R}_{i}(\mathbf{L})},J_{r}=J_{0}\text{ outside }\mathcal{S}_{1}\text{ for all }r\in\mathfrak{R}_{i}(\mathbf{L})\}.

(6.28)

Label the boundary punctures by chords
y_{0}
from
L^{(s)}
to
L^{(0)}
and
y_{j}
from
L^{(j-1)}
to
L^{(j)}
. The class
a_{\mathsf{p}}
is then defined by counting solutions
(r,u)
, where
r\in\mathfrak{R}_{1}(\mathbf{L})
and
u
is a solution to Floer’s equation:

\left\{\begin{aligned} &u:\Sigma_{r}\to X,\\
&(du-X_{K_{r}})^{0,1}=0,\\
&u(z)\in L^{C}\text{ for }z\in C\subset\partial\Sigma_{r},\\
&u(\varepsilon_{\zeta}(s,t))\to y_{\zeta}(t)\text{ for }\zeta\text{ a boundary puncture}\end{aligned}\right.

(6.29)

which additionally satisfies:

\left\{\begin{aligned} &u\cdot D_{\mathsf{p}}=1;u\cdot D_{\mathsf{q}}=0,\mathsf{q}\neq\mathsf{p}\\
&u(z_{int})\in D_{\mathsf{p}}.\end{aligned}\right.

(6.30)

The following is a minor variation on the argument from
[
She20a
, §4.4]
:

Lemma 60
.

The count of rigid solutions to (
6.29
), (
6.30
) defines a class
a_{\mathsf{p}}\in HH^{2\oplus\mathsf{p}}(\mathcal{A}_{0}).

This class is independent of the choice of perturbation data
(K,J)
.

Proof 6.10
.

Following the standard strategy in Floer theory, we consider the compactifications of moduli spaces of dimension
\leq 1
. We note that all of the Lagrangians
L_{i}
are Lagrangian spheres and hence are exact for both the primitives
\lambda_{1}
and
\lambda_{2}
. The integrated maximum principle (see
[
She20a
, Lemma 4.3]
) implies that all of our solutions are confined to lie in
\mathcal{S}_{2}
. In particular, no sphere bubbling can occur in our moduli spaces. Another application of the integrated maximum principle implies that for any disc bubble component
u_{bubble}
:

\displaystyle u_{bubble}\cdot D_{\mathsf{p}}\geq 0;\quad\text{ if }u_{bubble}\cdot D_{\mathsf{p}}=0,u_{bubble}(\Sigma_{r})\subset\mathcal{S}_{1}.

(6.31)

With these two observations, the argument of
[
She20a
, §4.4]
implies that this construction still defines a Hochschild cocycle, and that the corresponding Hochschild cohomology class is independent of the choice of perturbation data.

The class
a_{\mathsf{p}}
is equal to the first-order deformation class of the relative Fukaya category (cf.
[
PS23
, §9.4]
and
[
She20a
, Assumption 5.2]
).

As noted above, the definition of the log PSS morphism requires deforming the symplectic form in the same cohomology class so that the divisors
D
admit a regularization. By a Moser argument, we can equivalently recast this as deforming the divisors
D_{\mathsf{q}}
to symplectic divisors
\tilde{D}_{\mathsf{q}}
which admit a regularization (see
[
GP21
, §3]
or
[
McL12
, §5]
). Note that we can assume that this deformation is supported in an arbitrarily small neighbourhood of the codimension two strata of
D
. In particular, we can (and do) assume that:

•

the divisors
\cup_{\mathsf{q}}\tilde{D}_{\mathsf{q}}
remain disjoint from
\mathcal{S}_{1}
;

•

the divisors
\cup_{\mathsf{q}\neq\mathsf{p}}\tilde{D}_{\mathsf{q}}
remain disjoint from
\mathcal{S}_{2}
, and
\tilde{D}_{\mathsf{p}}\cap\mathcal{S}_{2}=D_{\mathsf{p}}\cap\mathcal{S}_{2}
.

Proposition 61
.

We have

\displaystyle a_{\mathsf{p}}=\mathcal{C}\mathcal{O}(PSS^{log}(\gamma_{\mathsf{p}}))\in HH^{2\oplus\mathsf{p}}(\mathcal{A}_{0}).

(6.32)

Proof 6.11
.

We first define a version of the class,
a_{\mathsf{p}}^{log}
, which is adapted to the log PSS setup. To prepare for this, we introduce the class of perturbation data
\mathcal{K}^{log}_{\mathfrak{R}_{i}(\mathbf{L})}\times\mathcal{J}^{log}_{\mathfrak{R}_{i}(\mathbf{L})}
consisting of all
(K^{log},J^{log})
such that for each
r\in\mathfrak{R}_{i}(\mathbf{L})
:

(a)

For
i=0
,
K_{r}^{log}
has non-positive curvature outside of
\mathcal{S}_{1}
;

(b)

X_{K_{r}^{log}}
preserves each component
\tilde{D}_{\mathsf{q}}
;

(c)

J_{r}^{log}
satisfies

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}=-dh_{1}\circ J_{r}\text{ on a contact shell about }\partial\mathcal{S}_{1},

(6.33)

(d)

J_{r}^{log}
makes each component
\tilde{D}_{\mathsf{q}}
into an almost-complex submanifold.

We choose perturbation data
(K^{log},J^{log})\in\mathcal{K}^{log}_{\mathfrak{R}_{i}(\mathbf{L})}\times\mathcal{J}^{log}_{\mathfrak{R}_{i}(\mathbf{L})}
such that for
i=0
, the restriction of our data to the union of
\mathcal{S}_{1}
and a small contact shell of
\partial\mathcal{S}_{1}
agree with the perturbation data previously chosen for the affine Fukaya category.
The integrated maximum principle implies that any Floer solution
u
satisfying
u\cdot\tilde{D}_{\mathsf{q}}=0
for all
\mathsf{q}\in P
is confined to
\mathcal{S}_{1}
. Therefore, while the perturbation data
(K^{log},J^{log})
for
i=0
may not agree with the data previously chosen for the affine Fukaya category, the resulting moduli spaces are isomorphic; so the disagreement does not affect the affine Fukaya category.

We now define the class
a_{\mathsf{p}}^{log}
by counting rigid solutions to (
6.29
), for the perturbation data
(K^{log},J^{log})
, which satisfy

\left\{\begin{aligned} &u\cdot\tilde{D}_{\mathsf{p}}=1;u\cdot\tilde{D}_{\mathsf{q}}=0,\mathsf{q}\neq\mathsf{p}\\
&u(z_{int})\in\tilde{D}_{\mathsf{p}}.\end{aligned}\right.

(6.34)

To control the compactification of dimension
\leq 1
moduli spaces, note first that by positivity of intersection, for any disc bubble component
u_{bubble}
, we have that for all
\mathsf{q}\in P
,

\displaystyle u_{bubble}\cdot\tilde{D}_{\mathsf{q}}\geq 0.

(6.35)

As in the proof of
Proposition

55
, Corollary
54
then rules out sphere bubbling in the moduli spaces and it follows that equality holds in (
6.35
) for
\mathsf{q}\neq\mathsf{p}
. From here, we have that if
u_{bubble}\cdot\tilde{D}_{\mathsf{p}}=0
as well, the component
u_{bubble}
does not carry the interior marked point (again using positivity of intersection) and so
u_{bubble}(\Sigma_{r})\subset\mathcal{S}_{1}
by the integrated maximum principle. It now follows, as in the proof of Lemma
60
, that the argument of
[
She20a
, §4.4]
goes through to define a Hochschild cocycle, whose corresponding Hochschild cohomology class is independent of choices. Finally, a standard degeneration of domain argument (see
[
Ton19
, Theorem 1.1]
for the analogous result in the context of log Calabi–Yau varieties) shows that

\displaystyle a_{\mathsf{p}}^{log}=\mathcal{C}\mathcal{O}(PSS^{log}(\gamma_{\mathsf{p}})).

(6.36)

It remains to prove that
a_{\mathsf{p}}^{log}=a_{\mathsf{p}}
.
The key technical issue requiring attention here is that the definition of the log PSS map uses almost-complex structures which preserve
\cup_{\mathsf{q}}\tilde{D}_{\mathsf{q}}
, and in particular, do not belong to
\mathcal{J}^{aff}
. Moreover, requiring the almost-complex structure to preserve
\tilde{D}_{\mathsf{p}}
is potentially inconsistent with requiring the almost-complex structure to be of contact type along
\partial\mathcal{S}_{2},
which is needed for the argument of Lemma
60
.
We resolve this by enlarging the families of almost-complex structures used to define
a_{\mathsf{p}}^{log}
and
a_{\mathsf{p}}
, to
\mathcal{J}^{1}
and
\mathcal{J}^{0}
respectively, so that one can still define Hochschild cohomology classes using perturbation data from these enlarged families, the classes are still independent of choices, and the families have non-empty intersection.

We first enlarge the family of almost-complex structures used to define
a_{\mathsf{p}}
. Let
\mathcal{J}^{0}_{\mathfrak{R}_{i}(\mathbf{L})}\subset\mathcal{J}_{\mathfrak{R}_{i}(\mathbf{L})}
be the subset of families of almost-complex structures which for all
r\in\mathfrak{R}_{1}(\mathbf{L})
satisfy:

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}=-dh_{1}\circ J_{r}\text{ on a contact shell about }\partial\mathcal{S}_{1},

(6.37)

\displaystyle\lambda_{2}=-dh_{2}\circ J_{r}\text{ on a contact shell about }\partial\mathcal{S}_{2}.

(6.38)

We choose
(K^{0},J^{0})\in\mathcal{K}^{aff}_{\mathfrak{R}_{i}(\mathbf{L})}\times\mathcal{J}^{0}_{\mathfrak{R}_{i}(\mathbf{L})}
such that for
i=0
, the choice agrees with our previous choice of perturbation data in the definition of the affine Fukaya category.

By the same argument as in the definition of
a_{\mathsf{p}}
, we can define a Hochschild cohomology class
a^{0}_{\mathsf{p}}
by counting solutions to (
6.29
) (for perturbation data
(K^{0},J^{0})
) which now satisfy
Eq.

6.34
. This class is independent of choices.
As in the proof of Lemma
60
, all solutions satisfying (
6.34
) or (
6.30
) are contained in
\mathcal{S}_{2}
. Because
\tilde{D}_{\mathsf{p}}\cap\mathcal{S}_{2}=D_{\mathsf{p}}\cap\mathcal{S}_{2}
, solutions satisfy (
6.34
) if and only if they satisfy (
6.30
).
Now observing that
\mathcal{J}^{aff}_{\mathfrak{R}_{1}(\mathbf{L})}\subset\mathcal{J}^{0}_{\mathfrak{R}_{1}(\mathbf{L})}
, it is immediate that the class
a^{0}_{\mathsf{p}}
agrees with the previously defined
a_{\mathsf{p}}
.

Now we enlarge the family of perturbation data used to define
a_{\mathsf{p}}^{log}
.
We define
\mathcal{J}^{1}_{\mathfrak{R}_{1}(\mathbf{L})}\subset\mathcal{J}_{\mathfrak{R}_{1}(\mathbf{L})}
to be the space of families of almost-complex structures
J^{1}
such that
J_{r}^{1}
satisfies (
6.37
) and makes each component
\tilde{D}_{\mathsf{q}}
,
\mathsf{q}\neq\mathsf{p}
into an almost-complex submanifold.

We choose
(K^{1},J^{1})\in\mathcal{K}^{aff}_{\mathfrak{R}_{i}(\mathbf{L})}\times\mathcal{J}^{1}_{\mathfrak{R}_{i}(\mathbf{L})},
such that for
i=0
our choice agrees with the previously chosen
(K^{log},J^{log})
over the union of
\mathcal{S}_{1}
and a small contact shell. We obtain a Hochschild cocycle
a_{\mathsf{p}}^{1}
whose corresponding Hochschild cohomology class is independent of choices. To see this, note that positivity of intersection still implies that for any disc bubble component
u_{bubble}
(arising in a compactification of dimension
\leq 1
moduli spaces),

\displaystyle u_{bubble}\cdot\tilde{D}_{\mathsf{q}}\geq 0,\mathsf{q}\neq\mathsf{p}.

(6.39)

As before, Corollary
54
then rules out sphere bubbling in the moduli spaces and it follows that equality holds in (
6.39
). We again have that if
u_{bubble}\cdot\tilde{D}_{\mathsf{p}}\leq 0
,
u_{bubble}(\Sigma_{r})\subset\mathcal{S}_{1}
, this time by the integrated maximum principle. This implies that the argument of
[
She20a
, §4.4]
again goes through. Because
\mathcal{J}^{log}_{\mathfrak{R}_{1}(\mathbf{L})}\subset\mathcal{J}^{1}_{\mathfrak{R}_{1}(\mathbf{L})}
, we have
a_{\mathsf{p}}^{1}=a_{\mathsf{p}}^{log}.

Finally, we observe that
\mathcal{J}^{1}_{\mathfrak{R}_{1}(\mathbf{L})}\cap\mathcal{J}^{0}_{\mathfrak{R}_{1}(\mathbf{L})}
is non-empty; thus we have
a_{\mathsf{p}}^{1}=a^{0}_{\mathsf{p}}.

Thus
a_{\mathsf{p}}=a_{\mathsf{p}}^{log}
as required.

6.3
A-side versality

Lemma 62
.

Let
\mathsf{p}\in P
, and
r_{\mathsf{p}}\cdot a_{\mathsf{p}}
be the corresponding first-order deformation class of
\mathcal{A}_{R}
.
Then
a_{\mathsf{p}}
spans
\mathrm{HH}^{2\oplus\mathsf{p}}(\mathcal{A}_{0})\cong\Bbbk
.

Proof 6.12
.

By
Proposition

61
,
a_{\mathsf{p}}=\mathcal{C}\mathcal{O}(PSS^{log}(\gamma_{\mathsf{p}})).

The result now follows from
Corollary

44
, together with the fact that
SH^{2\oplus\mathsf{p}}(\mathcal{H}_{t,0};\Bbbk)\cong\Bbbk
is spanned by the class
PSS^{log}(\gamma_{\mathsf{p}})
by Proposition
56
.

For any
\mathsf{p}\in P
, we define the ‘
\mathsf{p}
obstruction map’

\displaystyle\Obs_{\mathsf{p}}:\mathrm{HH}^{2\oplus 0}(\mathcal{A}_{0})

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{HH}^{3\oplus\mathsf{p}}(\mathcal{A}_{0})

\displaystyle\Obs_{\mathsf{p}}(\alpha)

\displaystyle:=[a_{\mathsf{p}},\alpha],

where
[-,-]
denotes the Gerstenhaber bracket.

Proposition 63
.

Consider the ‘total obstruction map’

\displaystyle\Obs:\mathrm{HH}^{2\oplus 0}(\mathcal{A}_{0})

\displaystyle\to\bigoplus_{\mathsf{p}\in P}\mathrm{HH}^{3\oplus\mathsf{p}}(\mathcal{A}_{0}),

\displaystyle\Obs(\alpha)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\mathsf{p}\in P}\Obs_{\mathsf{p}}(\alpha).

We have that
ker(\Obs)=0
.

Proof 6.13
.

By
Corollary

44
and
Proposition

61
, we translate
\Obs_{\mathsf{p}}
into
[PSS^{log}(\gamma_{\mathsf{p}}),-]
(and hence
\Obs
into
\sum_{\mathsf{p}\in P}[PSS^{log}(\gamma_{\mathsf{p}}),-]
). Now the result follows immediately from
Proposition

58
together with the fact that, by our assumption on the characteristic of
\Bbbk
, the vectors
\mathsf{p}
span
M^{*}_{\Bbbk}
.

Lemma 64
.

For any
\mathsf{p}\notin P\cup\{0\}
,
\mathrm{HH}^{2\oplus\mathsf{p}}(\mathcal{A}_{0})=0
.

Proof 6.14
.

By Corollary
44
,
\mathrm{HH}^{2\oplus\mathsf{p}}(\mathcal{A}_{0})\cong SH^{2\oplus\mathsf{p}}(\mathcal{H}_{t,0}).
By Proposition
56
,
SH^{2\oplus\mathsf{p}}(\mathcal{H}_{t,0})=0
for any
\mathsf{p}\notin P\cup\{0\}.

{rmk}

As noted in Remark
5.1
, the arguments of
[
Gan13
]
which enter into the proof of Corollary
44
(by way of Proposition
41
) use quadratic Hamiltonians for the construction of symplectic cohomology. On the other hand, the constructions of
[
GP20
,
GP21
]
use (directed systems of) Hamiltonians of linear growth as the model for symplectic cohomology. These two models for symplectic cohomology are compared in
[
Rit13
, Lemma 18.1]
.

7
Deforming the B-side

In
Section

7.1
, we give an explicit chain-level model of a natural
\Z\oplus M^{*}
-graded lift of the dg category
\mathcal{B}_{R}
deforming
\mathcal{B}_{0}
defined in the introduction, and in
Section

7.2
, we analyze the associated first-order deformation classes of
\mathcal{B}_{0}
. The key outputs of this construction and analysis are
Lemma

72
and
Lemma

74
, which collectively show that
\mathcal{B}_{R}
and its first-order deformation classes satisfy conditions needed to subsequently appeal to the versality criterion in
[
Gan+24
, Proposition A.6]
.

7.1
The dg category
\mathcal{B}^{dg}_{R}

We set
R_{B}=\Bbbk[[r_{\mathsf{p}}]]_{\mathsf{p}\in P}
and regard this as an
M^{*}
-graded ring by letting
r_{\mathsf{p}}
have degree
-\mathsf{p}\in M^{*}
. We let
\mathfrak{m}_{B}
denote the unique maximal ideal of
R_{B}
. To simplify notation, throughout this section, we will drop the subscript and denote this ideal simply by
\mathfrak{m}
.

Next, let
Y^{*}_{R}:=Y^{*}\times_{Spec(\Bbbk)}Spec(R_{B})
, and consider the subscheme

X^{*}_{R}:=\{W_{r}=0\}\subset Y^{*}_{R},

where
W_{r}
is as in (
1.4
) (with
r_{\mathsf{p}}
interpreted as above).

Lemma 65
.

The variety
X_{R}^{*}
is flat over
R_{B}
.

Proof 7.1
.

X_{R}^{*}
is a hypersurface in a smooth variety and hence Cohen-Macaulay of Krull dimension
\operatorname{dim}(R_{B})+n
. The result now follow follows from the “miracle flatness theorem” (
[
Stacks
,
Tag 00R4
]

) if we note that
\operatorname{dim}(\partial Y^{*})=n
.

Let
\mathbb{G}_{m}:=\mathbb{G}_{m}(\Bbbk)
.
Note that we have the toric
M_{\mathbb{G}_{m}}
-action on
Y^{*}
, and also an
M_{\mathbb{G}_{m}}
-action on
Spec(R_{B})
, via its
M^{*}
-grading; these combine to give an
M_{\mathbb{G}_{m}}
-action on
Y^{*}_{R}
.
We observe that this action preserves
X^{*}_{R}
, because
W_{r}
has degree
0\in M^{*}
(using the fact that
z^{\mathsf{p}}
has degree
\mathsf{p}
, and
r_{\mathsf{p}}
has degree
-\mathsf{p}
).

•

Let
Y^{*}=\cup_{\sigma}\mathcal{V}_{\sigma}
be the standard toric covering of
Y^{*}
by affine spaces
\mathcal{V}_{\sigma}\cong\mathbb{A}^{n+1}_{\Bbbk}
indexed by the maximal cones of the fan and let
\partial Y^{*}=\cup_{\sigma}\mathcal{W}_{\sigma}
be the covering of
\partial Y^{*}
given by intersecting
\mathcal{V}_{\sigma}
with
\partial Y^{*}
.

•

Let
Y_{R}^{*}=\cup_{\sigma}\mathcal{V}_{\sigma,R}
denote the induced cover of
Y_{R}^{*}
and
X_{R}^{*}=\cup_{\sigma}\mathcal{W}_{\sigma,R}
the induced affine cover of
X_{R}^{*}
.

We next define an
R_{B}
-linear dg category
\mathcal{B}^{dg}_{R}
whose objects are
\mathcal{O}(i)
. The morphism spaces in this category are given by Čech complexes of internal
\underline{Hom}
sheaves:

\displaystyle\hom_{\mathcal{B}^{dg}_{R}}(\mathcal{O}(i),\mathcal{O}(j)):=\check{C}^{*}(\{\mathcal{W}_{\sigma,R}\},\underline{Hom}(\mathcal{O}(i),\mathcal{O}(j))).

(7.1)

Composition in the category is induced by the shuffle product together with the local tensoring operation on internal
\underline{Hom}
sheaves (cf.
[
Sei14
, §5a]
). We choose the equivariant lift of
\mathcal{O}(i)
corresponding to the support functions
i\mathbf{F}_{\Delta}
(as in §
5
), so that this dg category becomes
\mathbb{Z}\oplus M^{*}
-graded. We note that the
\Bbbk
-linear dg category
\mathcal{B}^{dg}_{R}\otimes_{R_{B}}R_{B}/\mathfrak{m}
is a model for
\mathcal{B}_{0}.

Lemma 66
.

The
R_{B}
-modules
\hom_{\mathcal{B}^{dg}_{R}}(\mathcal{O}(i),\mathcal{O}(j))
are flat.

Proof 7.2
.

The Čech complexes
\check{C}^{*}(\{\mathcal{W}_{\sigma,R}\},\underline{Hom}(\mathcal{O}(i),\mathcal{O}(j)))
are finite direct sums of global functions
\Gamma(\cap_{\sigma_{i}}\mathcal{W}_{\sigma_{i},R},\mathcal{O}_{\cap_{\sigma_{i}}\mathcal{W}_{\sigma_{i},R}})
on finite overlaps
\cap_{\sigma_{i}}\mathcal{W}_{\sigma_{i},R}
of the open cover. By Lemma
65
, the coordinate rings
\Gamma(\cap_{\sigma_{i}}\mathcal{W}_{\sigma_{i},R},\mathcal{O}_{\cap_{\sigma_{i}}\mathcal{W}_{\sigma_{i},R}})
are flat over
R_{B}
and thus
\check{C}^{*}(\{\mathcal{W}_{\sigma,R}\},\underline{Hom}(\mathcal{O}(i),\mathcal{O}(j)))
is flat over
R_{B}
.

Lemma 67
.

The cohomology groups
H^{*}(\hom_{\mathcal{B}^{dg}_{R}}(\mathcal{O}(i),\mathcal{O}(j)))
are free
R_{B}
-modules of finite rank, for any
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof 7.3
.

It suffices to prove that the groups
H^{q}(X^{*}_{R},\mathcal{O}(j-i))
are all free over
R_{B}.
This is a straightforward application of the “cohomology and base change” theorem
[
Har77
, Theorem 3.12.11]
; note that theorem applies because
X_{R}^{*}
is flat over
R_{B}
by Lemma
65
. Namely, note that at the fiber over
\mathfrak{m}\in Spec(R_{B})
,
H^{q}(\partial Y^{*},\mathcal{O}(j-i))=0
if
q\notin\{0,n\}.
Item (a) of
[
Har77
, Theorem 3.12.11]
then implies that

\displaystyle H^{q}(X^{*}_{R},\mathcal{O}(j-i))=0\text{ if }q\notin\{0,n\}.

(7.2)

For
q=0
, note that by item (b) of
[
Har77
, Theorem 3.12.11]
, the vanishing of
H^{1}(X^{*}_{R},\mathcal{O}(j-i))
from (
7.2
) implies that the natural map:

\displaystyle\phi^{0}:H^{0}(X^{*}_{R},\mathcal{O}(j-i))\otimes_{R}R/\mathfrak{m}\to H^{0}(\partial Y^{*},\mathcal{O}(j-i))

(7.3)

is surjective. Applying item (b) of
[
Har77
, Theorem 3.12.11]
again, we see that

H^{0}(X^{*}_{R},\mathcal{O}(j-i))
is free. (Note that condition (i) of part (b) of
[
Har77
, Theorem 3.12.11]
is vacuous in this case.) The same argument applies for
q=n
if we note that
H^{n+1}(X^{*}_{R},\mathcal{O}(j-i))=0
(automatically for dimension reasons) and
H^{n-1}(X^{*}_{R},\mathcal{O}(j-i))=0
by (
7.2
).

To put ourselves in a setting appropriate for formal deformation theory arguments, it will be important to arrange that the morphism spaces in our dg category are complete with respect to the
\mathfrak{m}
-adic filtration. For the remainder of this section, given a graded
R_{B}
-module
N
, we will let
N^{\wedge}
denote its graded completion with respect to the
\mathfrak{m}
-adic filtration.

Definition 68
.

We let
\mathcal{B}_{R}
denote the dg category whose objects are given by
\mathcal{O}(i)
and whose morphism spaces are given by the
M^{*}
-graded
\mathfrak{m}
-adic completions of the corresponding morphism spaces in
\mathcal{B}_{R}^{dg}
. In other words:

\displaystyle\hom_{\mathcal{B}_{R}}(\mathcal{O}(i),\mathcal{O}(j)):=\hom_{\mathcal{B}_{R}^{dg}}(\mathcal{O}(i),\mathcal{O}(j))^{\wedge}.

(7.4)

Lemma 69
.

The natural functor
i^{\wedge}:\mathcal{B}^{dg}_{R}\to\mathcal{B}_{R}
is a quasi-isomorphism.

Proof 7.4
.

Fix
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}.
We wish to show that

\displaystyle i^{\wedge}:\hom_{\mathcal{B}_{R}^{dg}}(\mathcal{O}(i),\mathcal{O}(j))\to\hom_{\mathcal{B}_{R}}(\mathcal{O}(i),\mathcal{O}(j))

(7.5)

is a quasi-isomorphism.
By Lemma
67
, the cohomology groups
H^{*}(\hom_{\mathcal{B}^{dg}_{R}}(\mathcal{O}(i),\mathcal{O}(j)))
are free
R
-modules of finite rank. Thus, we may define a quasi-isomorphism

\displaystyle f:H^{*}(\hom_{\mathcal{B}^{dg}_{R}}(\mathcal{O}(i),\mathcal{O}(j)))\to\hom_{\mathcal{B}_{R}^{dg}}(\mathcal{O}(i),\mathcal{O}(j)))

(7.6)

by sending each basis element to a chain-level representative (where the LHS is regarded as a chain complex with vanishing differential). Moreover, for any
q\geq 0
, the induced maps

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}^{q}\otimes_{R_{B}}H^{*}(\hom_{\mathcal{B}^{dg}_{R}}(\mathcal{O}(i),\mathcal{O}(j)))\to\mathfrak{m}^{q}\otimes_{R_{B}}\hom_{\mathcal{B}_{R}^{dg}}(\mathcal{O}(i),\mathcal{O}(j))

given by tensoring
f
with
\mathfrak{m}^{q}
are also quasi-isomorphisms. This follows because
\hom_{\mathcal{B}_{R}^{dg}}(\mathcal{O}(i),\mathcal{O}(j))
is a bounded complex of flat modules by Lemma
66
and
H^{*}(\hom_{\mathcal{B}^{dg}_{R}}(\mathcal{O}(i),\mathcal{O}(j)))
is free of finite rank. Using flatness again to identify
\mathfrak{m}^{q}\otimes_{R_{B}}\hom_{\mathcal{B}_{R}^{dg}}(\mathcal{O}(i),\mathcal{O}(j))\cong\mathfrak{m}^{q}\hom_{\mathcal{B}_{R}^{dg}}(\mathcal{O}(i),\mathcal{O}(j))
, it follows that
f
is a filtered quasi-isomorphism, namely, for any
q\geq 0
, the maps:

\displaystyle f_{q}:\mathfrak{m}^{q}H^{*}(\hom_{\mathcal{B}^{dg}_{R}}(\mathcal{O}(i),\mathcal{O}(j)))\to\mathfrak{m}^{q}\hom_{\mathcal{B}_{R}^{dg}}(\mathcal{O}(i),\mathcal{O}(j))

(7.7)

are quasi-isomorphisms.

Now to prove the lemma, it suffices to prove that the composition
i^{\wedge}\circ f
is a quasi-isomorphism. The
E_{1}
-pages of the spectral sequences induced by the
\mathfrak{m}
-adic filtrations on these chain complexes are all given by

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{q}E_{1}^{*-q,q}\cong H^{*}(\hom_{\mathcal{B}_{0}}(\mathcal{O}(i),\mathcal{O}(j)))\otimes Gr^{q}_{\mathfrak{m}}(R_{B}),

(7.8)

and the induced maps between them are isomorphisms because
f
is a filtered quasi-isomorphism.
As the
\mathfrak{m}
-adic filtrations on the source and target of
i^{\wedge}\circ f
are complete and exhaustive (the former because it is free of finite rank, the latter by construction), the map is a quasi-isomorphism, as required.

{rmk}

[Cf. Section 5b in
[
Sei14
]
] Lemma
69
can be viewed as a special case of the formal GAGA theorem
[
Gro61
, Section 4]
.

Definition 70
.

Let
N
be a graded
R_{B}
-module.
We say that
N
is
topologically free
if there exists an isomorphism
N\simeq((N/\mathfrak{m}N)\otimes R_{B})^{\wedge}
lifting the natural map
N\to N/\mathfrak{m}N
.

Definition 71

(Cf. Remark 2.2 in
[
Sei14
]
)
.

A
topological

R_{B}
-linear dg category is a dg category whose hom-spaces are topologically free
R_{B}
-modules.

Lemma 72
.

The category
\mathcal{B}_{R}
is a topological
R_{B}
-linear dg category.

Proof 7.5
.

As in Lemma
66
, the Čech complexes
\check{C}^{*}(\{\mathcal{W}_{\sigma,R}\},\underline{Hom}(\mathcal{O}(i),\mathcal{O}(j)))
are finite direct sums of global functions on finite overlaps of the open cover. Such overlaps are indexed by (not necessarily maximal) cones
\sigma
of
\Sigma^{*}
; we denote the overlap indexed by
\sigma
by
\mathcal{W}_{\sigma,R}
, sitting inside the corresponding open subset
\mathcal{V}_{\sigma,R}\subset Y^{*}_{R}
.
It then suffices to prove that
(\Gamma(\mathcal{W}_{\sigma,R},\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}_{\sigma,R}}))^{\wedge}
is topologically free.

We may choose a basis of
M_{\R}
such that the first
j
basis vectors form a basis for
\sigma
(because
\Sigma^{*}
is smooth).
This induces an isomorphism
\mathcal{V}_{\sigma,R}\cong(\mathbb{A}_{\Bbbk})^{j}\times(\mathbb{G}_{m}(\Bbbk))^{n-j}\times Spec(R_{B})
. We may choose coordinates on
\mathcal{V}_{\sigma,R}
so that its ring of functions is given by
R_{B}[\xi_{1},\cdots,\xi_{j},\xi_{j+1}^{\pm},\cdots,\xi_{n}^{\pm}]
and so that the defining function
W_{b}
for
\mathcal{W}_{\sigma,R}
is given by:
W_{b}=-\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{j}+\sum_{\alpha}b_{\alpha}\xi^{\alpha}
where
\alpha\in(\mathbb{Z}^{\geq 0})^{j}\times(\mathbb{Z})^{n-j}
and
b_{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{m}.

Now take
\mathcal{W}_{\sigma}
to be the hypersurface in
\mathcal{V}_{\sigma}=Spec(\Bbbk[\xi_{1},\cdots,\xi_{j},\xi_{j+1}^{\pm},\cdots,\xi_{n}^{\pm}])
cut out by
-\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{j}=0.
Let
S\subset(\mathbb{Z}^{\geq 0})^{j}\times(\mathbb{Z})^{n-j}
denote the set of vectors such that one of the first
j
entries is zero. The set
\{\xi^{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in S}
defines a
\Bbbk
-basis for the ring of functions on
\mathcal{W}_{\sigma}
. For the remainder of the argument, we set
\mathcal{Q}=R_{B}[\xi_{1},\cdots,\xi_{j},\xi_{j+1}^{\pm},\cdots,\xi_{n}^{\pm}]
. Consider the natural map of
R_{B}
-modules:

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{\alpha\in S}R_{B}\cdot\xi^{\alpha}\to\frac{\mathcal{Q}}{(W_{b})}\cong\Gamma(\mathcal{W}_{\sigma,R},\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}_{\sigma,R}})

and let

\displaystyle\left(\bigoplus_{\alpha\in S}R_{B}\cdot\xi^{\alpha}\right)^{\wedge}\to\left(\frac{\mathcal{Q}}{(W_{b})}\right)^{\wedge}

(7.9)

denote its graded completion.

The map (
7.9
) is surjective by Nakayama’s lemma for complete modules
[
Stacks
,
Tag 00M9 (i)
]
.
9
9

9

The reference
[
Stacks
,
Tag 00M9 (i)
]
is stated for standard completions as opposed to graded completions. However, the proof carries over without change to graded completions.
To prove this map is injective, suppose to the contrary that
f_{0}
is a non-zero
\mathfrak{m}
-adically convergent series which is in the kernel of (
7.9
). Note that the sequence

\displaystyle 0\to\mathcal{Q}^{\wedge}\xrightarrow{W_{b}\cdot}\mathcal{Q}^{\wedge}\to\left(\frac{\mathcal{Q}}{(W_{b})}\right)^{\wedge}\to 0

(7.10)

is exact by
[
Stacks
,
Tag 00M9 (iii)
]
and Lemma
65
. This means that

\displaystyle f_{0}=W_{b}f_{1}\in\mathcal{Q}^{\wedge}

(7.11)

for some
f_{1}\in\mathcal{Q}^{\wedge}
. As
\mathcal{Q}^{\wedge}
is complete and
f_{1}\neq 0
, there exists
r\geq 0
such that
f_{1}\in\mathfrak{m}^{r}\mathcal{Q}^{\wedge}\setminus\mathfrak{m}^{r+1}\mathcal{Q}^{\wedge}.
This means we can expand

f_{1}=\sum_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}\xi^{\alpha}+O(\mathfrak{m}^{r+1})

with
c_{\alpha}\notin\mathfrak{m}^{r+1}
for some
\alpha
. Equation (
7.11
) then implies that

\displaystyle f_{0}=\sum_{\alpha}-c_{\alpha}(\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{j}\cdot\xi^{\alpha})+O(\mathfrak{m}^{r+1}).

(7.12)

But this is impossible since the right-hand side of (
7.12
) involves monomials
\xi^{\alpha}
with
\alpha\notin S.

7.2
B-side versality

Throughout this section, we fix
\mathsf{p}\in P
.
Set
\Bbbk_{\epsilon}:=\Bbbk[\epsilon]/\epsilon^{2}
and consider the homomorphism
j_{\mathsf{p}}:R_{B}\to\Bbbk_{\epsilon}
defined by letting
j_{\mathsf{p}}(r_{\mathsf{p}})=\epsilon
,
j_{\mathsf{p}}(r_{\mathsf{q}})=0
for
\mathsf{q}\neq\mathsf{p}.

The homomorphism
j_{\mathsf{p}}
is
\mathbb{Z}\oplus M^{*}
-graded if
\epsilon
is given degree
(0,-\mathsf{p})\in\Z\oplus M^{*}
. Consider the base change of
\mathcal{B}_{R}
along
j_{\mathsf{p}}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{\epsilon}:=\mathcal{B}_{R}\otimes_{R_{B}}\Bbbk_{\epsilon}.

(7.13)

The category
\mathcal{B}_{\epsilon}
is a
\Z\oplus M^{*}
-graded infinitesimal deformation of
\mathcal{B}_{0}
and hence gives rise to a first-order deformation class
b_{\mathsf{p}}\in\mathrm{HH}^{2\oplus\mathsf{p}}(\mathcal{B}_{0})
. The main result of this section is the following:

Lemma 73
.

The class
b_{\mathsf{p}}
is non-zero, for each
\mathsf{p}\in P
.

{rmk}

The proof of
Lemma

73
uses our assumption that
\Sigma^{*}
is smooth (see the proof of
Lemma

76
). However, the proof works just as well if all
2
-dimensional cones of
\Sigma^{*}
are smooth (i.e., if
Y^{*}
is smooth away from its strata of codimension
\geq 3
).

Before giving the proof of Lemma
73
, we note the following consequence:

Lemma 74
.

The class
b_{\mathsf{p}}
spans
\mathrm{HH}^{2\oplus\mathsf{p}}(\mathcal{B}_{0})
, for any
\mathsf{p}\in P
.

Proof 7.6
.

We have

\mathrm{HH}^{2\oplus\mathsf{p}}(\mathcal{B}_{0})\simeq\mathrm{HH}^{2\oplus\mathsf{p}}(\mathcal{A}_{0})\simeq SH^{2\oplus\mathsf{p}}(F;\Bbbk),

and we know that the latter space has rank one by Proposition
56
.
As
b_{\mathsf{p}}\neq 0
by Lemma
73
, it must span
\mathrm{HH}^{2\oplus\mathsf{p}}(\mathcal{B}_{0})
.

The key step in the proof of Lemma
73
will be to show that the corresponding deformation of homogeneous coordinate rings is non-trivial. We start by recalling the homogeneous coordinate ring of
(Y^{*},\mathcal{L}_{\Delta^{*}})
. It is the
\Z\oplus M^{*}
-graded algebra

S_{\Delta^{*}}:=\Bbbk[C(\Delta^{*})\cap\Z\oplus M^{*}],

where
C(\Delta^{*}):=\R_{\geq 0}\cdot\{\{1\}\times\Delta^{*}\}\subset\R\times M^{*}_{\R}
.
The element
z^{(j,n)}
has degree
(j,n)
. Let
\mathsf{p}\in P_{0}
, and let
X^{*}_{\mathsf{p}}
be the
\Bbbk_{\epsilon}
-scheme with defining equation
\{z^{0}=\epsilon z^{\mathsf{p}}\}
; this is an infinitesimal deformation of the hypersurface
X^{*}_{0}=\{z^{0}=0\}
. It has
\Z\oplus M^{*}
-graded homogeneous coordinate ring
S^{\epsilon}:=\left(S_{\Delta^{*}}\otimes_{\Bbbk}\Bbbk_{\epsilon}\right)/(z^{0}-\epsilon z^{\mathsf{p}})
, where as above we give
\epsilon
degree
(0,-\mathsf{p})\in\Z\oplus M^{*}
.

We can describe
S^{\epsilon}
explicitly as follows.
It is generated, as a
\Bbbk_{\epsilon}
-module, by monomials
z^{\tilde{n}}
for
\tilde{n}\in\partial C(\Delta^{*})\cap\Z\oplus M^{*}
.
(Indeed, it is easy to use the relation
z^{0}=\epsilon z^{\mathsf{p}}
to write any other monomial in terms of these.)
It is straightforward to check that the multiplication is given by

z^{\tilde{n}_{1}}\cdot z^{\tilde{n}_{2}}=\delta z^{\tilde{n}_{1}+\tilde{n}_{2}}+\epsilon\cdot\delta z^{\tilde{n}_{1}+\tilde{n}_{2}+(0,\mathsf{p})},

where we use the abbreviation

\delta z^{\tilde{n}}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}z^{\tilde{n}}&\text{if $\tilde{n}\in\partial C(\Delta^{*})$}\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

Now, consider the first-order deformation
\mathcal{B}_{\epsilon}
of
\mathcal{B}_{0}
corresponding to
b_{\mathsf{p}}
.
It is
M^{*}
-graded and linear over
\Bbbk_{\epsilon}:=\Bbbk[\epsilon]/\epsilon^{2}
, where
\epsilon
has degree
-\mathsf{p}\in M^{*}
.
Note that

\mathrm{Hom}^{0}_{\mathcal{B}_{\epsilon}}(\mathcal{L}^{i},\mathcal{L}^{j})_{n}=S^{\epsilon}_{(j-i,n)},

for
n\in M^{*}
.
We will denote the generator of
\mathrm{Hom}^{0}_{\mathcal{B}_{\epsilon}}(\mathcal{L}^{i},\mathcal{L}^{j})
corresponding to
z^{(j-i,n)}
by
z_{i}^{(j-i,n)}
.

Lemma 75
.

If
\mathsf{p}\in P
, then
H^{0}(\mathcal{B}_{\epsilon})
is non-trivial, as a
\Z\oplus M^{*}
-graded deformation of
H^{0}(\mathcal{B}_{0})
.

Proof 7.7
.

Suppose
\mathcal{B}_{\epsilon}
were a trivial graded deformation of
\mathcal{B}_{0}
.
Then there would exist a graded isomorphism
F_{\epsilon}:H^{0}(\mathcal{B}_{\epsilon})\xrightarrow{\sim}H^{0}(\mathcal{B}_{0})^{triv}
, with
F_{0}=\id
.
As each
M^{*}
-graded piece of a morphism space in
H^{0}(\mathcal{B}_{0})
has dimension
\leq 1
, and
\epsilon
has degree
(0,-\mathsf{p})
, this isomorphism must take the form

F_{\epsilon}(z_{i}^{\tilde{n}})=z_{i}^{\tilde{n}}+\epsilon a(i,\tilde{n})z_{i}^{\tilde{n}+(0,\mathsf{p})},

where
a(i,\tilde{n})\in\Bbbk
vanishes if
\tilde{n}+(0,\mathsf{p})\notin\partial C(\Delta^{*})
.

By Lemma
76
below, there exist points
\tilde{n}_{1}
,
\tilde{n}_{2}
in
\partial C(\Delta^{*})\cap\Z\oplus M^{*}
such that
\tilde{n}_{j}+(0,\mathsf{p})\notin\partial C(\Delta^{*})
, and in particular
F^{\epsilon}(z_{i}^{\tilde{n}_{j}})=z_{i}^{\tilde{n}_{j}}
, for both
j
;
\tilde{n}_{1}+\tilde{n}_{2}\notin\partial C(\Delta^{*})
, and in particular
\delta z^{\tilde{n}_{1}+\tilde{n}_{2}}=0
; and
\tilde{n}_{1}+\tilde{n}_{2}+(0,\mathsf{p})\in\partial C(\Delta^{*})
, and in particular
\delta z^{\tilde{n}_{1}+\tilde{n}_{2}+(0,\mathsf{p})}=z^{\tilde{n}_{1}+\tilde{n}_{2}+(0,\mathsf{p})}
.
Thus, from

F^{\epsilon}(z_{0}^{\tilde{n}_{1}})\cdot_{H^{0}(\mathcal{B}_{0})^{triv}}F^{\epsilon}(z_{i}^{\tilde{n}_{2}})=F^{\epsilon}(z_{0}^{\tilde{n}_{1}}\cdot_{H^{0}(\mathcal{B}_{\epsilon})}z_{i}^{\tilde{n}_{2}}),

we obtain

z^{\tilde{n}_{1}}\cdot_{H^{0}(\mathcal{B}_{0})^{triv}}z^{\tilde{n}_{2}}=F^{\epsilon}\left(\delta z_{0}^{\tilde{n}_{1}+\tilde{n}_{2}}+\epsilon\cdot\delta z_{0}^{\tilde{n}_{1}+\tilde{n}_{2}+(0,\mathsf{p})}\right),

and hence (using
F^{0}=\id
) that

0=\epsilon z_{0}^{\tilde{n}_{1}+\tilde{n}_{2}+(0,\mathsf{p})},

a contradiction.

Lemma 76
.

If
\mathsf{p}\in P
, then there exist points
\tilde{n}_{1}
,
\tilde{n}_{2}
in
\partial C(\Delta^{*})\cap\Z\oplus M^{*}
, such that

•

\tilde{n}_{i}+(0,\mathsf{p})\notin C(\Delta^{*})
, for both
i
;

•

\tilde{n}_{1}+\tilde{n}_{2}\notin\partial C(\Delta^{*})
;

•

\tilde{n}_{1}+\tilde{n}_{2}+(0,\mathsf{p})\in\partial C(\Delta^{*})
.

Proof 7.8
.

Because
\mathsf{p}\in P
, it lies on a facet
F
of
\Delta^{*}
having codimension
2
(it may lie on the boundary of
F
).
Let us choose a rational point
q
in the interior of
F
.
Consider the convex cone
C_{q}:=\R_{\geq 0}\cdot(\Delta^{*}-q)
, which contains
-\mathsf{p}
in its interior.

Because
F
has codimension
2
,
C_{q}
is an intersection of half spaces,
\{m_{1}\geq 0\}\cap\{m_{2}\geq 0\}
for
m_{i}\in M
linearly independent; because
\Sigma^{*}
is smooth, we may choose
m_{1},m_{2}\in M
to be a subset of a basis.
Therefore, any element in
C_{q}\cap M^{*}
can be written as a sum of two vectors in
M^{*}
, one pointing along each boundary facet of
C_{q}
. In particular, we have
-\mathsf{p}=v_{1}+v_{2}
.

There exists
k\in\Z_{>0}
such that
kq\in M^{*}
.
By taking
K
to be a sufficiently large multiple of
k
we may then arrange that
q+\frac{1}{K}v_{i}\in\partial\Delta^{*}
for each
i
, and
q+\frac{1}{2K}(v_{1}+v_{2})
lies in the interior of
\Delta^{*}
.
We now set
\tilde{n}_{i}=(K,Kq+v_{i})
.

We now check that
\tilde{n}_{1}
and
\tilde{n}_{2}
have the desired properties:

•

\tilde{n}_{i}\in\Z\oplus M^{*}
: this follows because we chose
K
so that
Kq\in M^{*}
.

•

\tilde{n}_{i}\in\partial C(\Delta^{*})
: this follows because
q+\frac{1}{K}v_{i}\in\partial\Delta^{*}
.

•

\tilde{n}_{i}+(0,\mathsf{p})\notin C(\Delta^{*})
: this follows because
-\mathsf{p}
points from
v_{i}
into the interior of
C_{q}
, so
v_{i}+\mathsf{p}\notin C_{q}
; and hence,
q+\frac{1}{K}(v_{i}+\mathsf{p})\notin\Delta^{*}
.

•

\tilde{n}_{1}+\tilde{n}_{2}\notin\partial C(\Delta^{*})
: this follows because
q+\frac{1}{2K}(v_{1}+v_{2})
lies in the interior of
\Delta^{*}
, and in particular, does not lie in
\partial\Delta^{*}
.

•

\tilde{n}_{1}+\tilde{n}_{2}+(0,\mathsf{p})\in\partial C(\Delta^{*})
: this follows because
v_{1}+v_{2}=-\mathsf{p}
, and
q\in\partial\Delta^{*}
.

{rmk}

Lemma
75
is false if
\mathsf{p}\notin P
: indeed, the points
\mathsf{p}\in P_{0}\setminus(\{0\}\cup P)
are known as ‘roots’ of
Y^{*}
and give rise to one-parameter families of automorphisms of the toric variety which trivialize the corresponding deformations of the hypersurface (see
[
CK99
, Section 6.1]
).

We now observe that the deformation
\mathcal{B}_{\epsilon}
defined in (
7.13
) is a graded uncurved first-order deformation of the
A_{\infty}
category
\mathcal{B}_{0}
.
It satisfies
\mathrm{Hom}^{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{0}}(L,L)=\mathrm{Hom}^{2}_{\mathcal{B}_{0}}(L,L)=0
for all objects
L
of
\mathcal{B}_{0}
, and furthermore,
\mathrm{Hom}^{*}_{\mathcal{B}_{0}}(K,L)
is concentrated in two degrees which differ by
n\geq 3
, for all pairs of objects
K,L
; in particular, it is concentrated in degrees, no two of which differ by
1
.
This ensures that
H^{*}(\mathcal{B}_{\epsilon})\simeq H^{*}(\mathcal{B}_{0})\otimes\Bbbk_{\epsilon}
is a flat deformation of
H^{*}(\mathcal{B}_{0})
, by considering the spectral sequence associated to the
\epsilon
-adic filtration on
(\mathcal{B}_{\epsilon},\mu^{1}_{\epsilon})
: the
E_{1}
page is
H^{*}(\mathcal{B}_{0})\otimes\Bbbk_{\epsilon}
, and the differential on the
E_{1}
page is necessarily
0
.

Lemma 77
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{B}_{\epsilon}
is any graded uncurved first-order deformation of
\mathcal{B}_{0}
,
\mathrm{Hom}^{*}_{\mathcal{B}_{0}}(L,L)=0
for
*=1,2
, and
\mathrm{Hom}^{*}_{\mathcal{B}_{0}}(K,L)
is concentrated in degrees, no two of which differ by
1
.
If
H^{*}(\mathcal{B}_{\epsilon})
is non-trivial as a graded first-order deformation of
H^{*}(\mathcal{B}_{0})
, then the corresponding Maurer–Cartan element
KS(\partial/\partial\epsilon)\in\mathrm{HH}^{2\oplus-\operatorname{deg}(\epsilon)}(\mathcal{B}_{0})
is non-zero.

Proof 7.9
.

Our first step is to show that we may assume that
\mathcal{B}_{\epsilon}
is minimal.
We do this by replacing it with a minimal model
\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{\epsilon}
; we need to prove that this exists, as the base ring
\Bbbk_{\epsilon}
is not a field.

Let
\beta\in CC^{2}(\mathcal{B}_{0})
be the Hochschild cocycle corresponding to the deformation
\mathcal{B}_{\epsilon}
; we want to show that
[\beta]\neq 0
.
Let
\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{0}
be a minimal model for
\mathcal{B}_{0}
(which exists because the base ring
\Bbbk
is a field), and let
\bar{\beta}
be a Hochschild cocycle representing the image of
[\beta]
under the isomorphism
\mathrm{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{B}_{0})\simeq\mathrm{HH}^{*}(\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{0})
.
Let
\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{\epsilon}
denote the first-order deformation of
\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{0}
corresponding to
\bar{\beta}
.
Note that
\bar{\beta}^{0}_{L}=0
for all
L
, because
\hom^{2}_{\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}(L,L)=\mathrm{Hom}^{2}_{\mathcal{B}_{0}}(L,L)=0
, so this deformation is uncurved.
Also note that
\bar{\beta}^{1}=0
by our assumption that
\hom^{*}_{\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}(L,L)=\mathrm{Hom}^{*}_{\mathcal{B}_{0}}(L,L)
is concentrated in degrees, no two of which differ by
1
.
Thus
\bar{\beta}
has length
\geq 2
(recall from Appendix
C.1
that the ‘length’ of a Hochschild cochain is the number of inputs).

It is easy to show from the definitions that the
A_{\infty}
quasi-isomorphism
F_{0}:\mathcal{B}_{0}\xrightarrow{\sim}\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{0}
extends to a (possibly curved)
A_{\infty}
homomorphism
F_{\epsilon}:\mathcal{B}_{\epsilon}\to\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{\epsilon}
; the curvature
F^{0}_{\epsilon}
lies in
hom^{1}_{\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}(L,L)=\mathrm{Hom}^{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{0}}(L,L)=0
by hypothesis, so in fact
F_{\epsilon}
is uncurved.
Thus it induces an isomorphism
H^{*}(\mathcal{B}_{\epsilon})\simeq H^{*}(\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{\epsilon})
.
Thus we have shown that
\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{\epsilon}
is a deformation of
\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{0}
satisfying all the hypotheses we imposed on
\mathcal{B}_{\epsilon}
; so it suffices to show that
[\bar{\beta}]\neq 0
.

Suppose, to the contrary, that
\bar{\beta}=\partial\gamma
for some
\gamma\in CC^{1}(\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{0})
.
Recall that we associate to a Hochschild cochain
\varphi^{s}
of length
s\geq 0
, and cohomological degree
t
, the bidegree
(s+t,s)\in\Z\oplus\Z
.
We expand
\gamma=\sum_{s\geq 0}\gamma^{s}
where
\gamma^{s}
is the part of length
s
; and we expand the Hochschild differential as
\partial=\sum_{s\geq 0}\partial^{s}
, where
\partial^{s}
is the part coming from
\mu^{s}
, which changes bidegree by adding
(1,s-1)
.

Note that
\gamma^{0}=0
, because
hom^{1}_{\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}(L,L)=0
; and
\partial^{s}=0
for
s\leq 1
because
\mu^{s}=0
.
Thus, the length two part of the equation
\bar{\beta}=\partial\gamma
says that
\bar{\beta}^{2}=\partial^{2}\gamma^{1}
.
It is well-known that this is equivalent to the associative algebra
H^{*}(\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{\epsilon})
being a trivial deformation of
H^{*}(\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{0})
, which contradicts our hypothesis; thus
[\bar{\beta}]\neq 0
as required.

Proof 7.10

(Proof of Lemma
73
)
.

Follows by combining
Lemma

75
and
Lemma

77
.

8
Proof of the main result

8.1
Applying versality

Consider the quasi-isomorphism
F_{0}:\mathcal{B}_{0}\to\mathcal{A}_{0}
from (
5.2
).
The upshot of Sections
6
and
7
is that we have prepared the way to apply Proposition A.6 from
[
Gan+24
]
, to construct an extension of
F_{0}
to a curved filtered quasi-isomorphism
F:\mathcal{B}_{R}\to\mathcal{A}_{R}
.
In the first place, we have that
\mathcal{A}_{R}
is topologically free over
R_{A}=\Bbbk[[\mathrm{NE}_{A}]]
(where
\mathrm{NE}_{A}
is ‘nice’ by construction), because its
\operatorname{hom}
-spaces consist of finite rank free modules; and
\mathcal{B}_{R}
is topologically free over
R_{B}=\Bbbk[[(\Z_{\geq 0})^{P}]]
by Lemma
72
.

Proposition 78
.

There exists an
M^{*}
-graded
\Bbbk
-algebra homomorphism
\Psi^{*}:R_{B}\to R_{A}
, sending
r_{p}\mapsto r_{p}\cdot\phi_{p}
for some units
\phi_{p}\in R_{A}
of degree
0\in M^{*}
, together with a curved filtered quasi-isomorphism
F:\Psi^{*}\mathcal{B}_{R}\to\mathcal{A}_{R}
.

Proof 8.1
.

This follows from the versality criterion
[
Gan+24
, Proposition A.6]
, whose hypotheses we now verify. Hypothesis (1) of the versality criterion is verified on the A-side by Lemma
62
, and on the B-side by Lemma
74
. Hypothesis (2) is verified by Proposition
63
, and Hypothesis (3) by Lemma
64
.

8.2
Specialization to Novikov field

We consider the
\Bbbk
-algebra homomorphism

\displaystyle a(\kappa)^{*}:R_{A}

\displaystyle\to\Lambda\qquad\text{sending}

\displaystyle r^{\beta}

\displaystyle\mapsto r^{\kappa(\beta)}

associated to the class
\kappa
in the interior of
\mathsf{N}
(defined in
[
She20a
, Lemma 5.20]
).
Let
\mathcal{A}_{\kappa}:=\mathcal{A}_{R}\otimes_{R_{A}}\Lambda
, where
\Lambda
is regarded as an
R_{A}
-algebra via
a(\kappa)^{*}
.
It is a curved filtered
A_{\infty}
category, where the filtration is the
r
-adic one, so we may define
\mathcal{A}_{\kappa}^{\mathsf{bc}}
as in
[
PS23
, Section 2.2]
.

On the other hand, consider the homomorphism
b(\kappa)^{*}:=a(\kappa)^{*}\circ\Psi^{*}
.
Let
\mathcal{B}_{\kappa}:=\mathcal{B}_{R}\otimes_{R_{B}}\Lambda
, where now
\Lambda
is regarded as an
R_{B}
-algebra via
b(\kappa)^{*}
.
By Proposition
78
, we have a curved filtered quasi-isomorphism

F_{\kappa}:\mathcal{B}_{\kappa}\to\mathcal{A}_{\kappa},

and therefore a quasi-equivalence

F_{\kappa}^{\mathsf{bc}}:\mathcal{B}_{\kappa}^{\mathsf{bc}}\to\mathcal{A}_{\kappa}^{\mathsf{bc}}

by
[
PS23
, Theorem 2.12]
.

Note that
\mathcal{B}_{R}
, and hence
\mathcal{B}_{\kappa}
, are uncurved and cohomologically unital by construction; so we have an embedding
\mathcal{B}_{\kappa}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{B}_{\kappa}^{\mathsf{bc}}
by equipping each object with the
0
bounding cochain.
Thus we have a quasi-embedding

\mathcal{B}_{\kappa}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{\kappa}^{\mathsf{bc}}.

(8.1)

8.3
Smoothness of the mirror

Lemma 79
.

The
\Lambda
-variety
X^{*}_{b(\kappa)}
is smooth.

Proof 8.2
.

The result follows from
[
Gan+24
, Proposition B.1]
, whose hypotheses we now verify.
Firstly, the coefficient field
\Lambda
is of the necessary type, and the fan
\Sigma^{*}
of the toric variety
Y^{*}
is smooth.
We recall that
X^{*}_{b(\kappa)}\subset Y^{*}
is the vanishing locus of the function

\sum_{\mathsf{p}\in P_{0}}c_{\mathsf{p}}\cdot z^{\mathsf{p}},

where

c_{\mathsf{p}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}-1&\mathsf{p}=0;\\
b(\kappa)^{*}(r_{\mathsf{p}})&\mathsf{p}\in P;\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

As a consequence, we have

\mathrm{val}(c_{\mathsf{p}})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}0&\mathsf{p}=0;\\
\kappa_{\mathsf{p}}&\mathsf{p}\in P\qquad\text{by Lemma \ref{lem:bplambda} below;}\\
\infty&\text{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

Therefore, the smallest concave function
\psi:\Delta^{*}\to\R
such that
\psi(\mathsf{p})\geq-\mathrm{val}(c_{\mathsf{p}})
is precisely
-\psi_{\kappa}
, where
\psi_{\kappa}
is the function from the introduction.
The induced subdivision of
\Delta^{*}
into domains of linearity of
\psi_{\kappa}
is a decomposition by simplices, by our assumption that
\Sigma_{\kappa}
is simplicial; and the affine volumes of the simplices are not divisible by
\mathrm{char}\,(\Lambda)=\mathrm{char}\,(\Bbbk)
, by the condition on characteristic.
Thus the hypotheses of
[
Gan+24
, Proposition B.1]
are satisfied, so
X^{*}_{b(\kappa)}
is smooth.

Lemma 80
.

We have
\mathrm{val}\left(b(\kappa)^{*}(r_{\mathsf{p}})\right)=\kappa_{\mathsf{p}}
.

Proof 8.3
.

Note that
a(\kappa)^{*}(r_{\mathsf{p}})=r^{\kappa_{\mathsf{p}}}
by definition. By Proposition
78
, the homomorphism
\Psi^{*}
sends
r_{\mathsf{p}}\mapsto r_{\mathsf{p}}\cdot\phi_{\mathsf{p}}
for some units
\phi_{\mathsf{p}}\in R_{A}.
In particular, we have
\phi_{\mathsf{p}}\neq 0\in R_{A}/\mathfrak{m}_{A}
, so
\mathrm{val}(a(\kappa)^{*}(\phi_{\mathsf{p}}))=0
. Thus we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{val}\left(b(\kappa)^{*}(r_{\mathsf{p}})\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{val}\left(a(\kappa)^{*}(\Psi^{*}(r_{\mathsf{p}}))\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{val}\left(a(\kappa)^{*}(r_{\mathsf{p}})\right)+\mathrm{val}\left(a(\kappa)^{*}(\phi_{\mathsf{p}})\right)

\displaystyle=\kappa_{\mathsf{p}}

as required.

8.4
Split-generation

Let
X^{*}_{b(\kappa)}:=\cup_{\sigma}\mathcal{W}_{\sigma,b(\kappa)}
be the affine cover of
X^{*}_{b(\kappa)}
given by base-changing the affine cover of
X^{*}_{R}
introduced in §
7.1
.

We introduce a dg category
\check{\mathcal{S}}(X^{*}_{b(\kappa)})
whose objects are algebraic vector bundles and whose morphism spaces are given by Čech complexes of internal
\underline{Hom}
sheaves:

\displaystyle\hom_{\check{\mathcal{S}}(X_{b(\kappa)}^{*})}(E_{0},E_{1}):=\check{C}^{*}\left(\{\mathcal{W}_{\sigma,{b(\kappa)}}\},\underline{Hom}(E_{0},E_{1})\right).

(8.2)

Definition 81
.

We define
\operatorname{Perf}_{dg}(X_{b(\kappa)}^{*}):=\operatorname{Perf}\check{\mathcal{S}}(X_{b(\kappa)}^{*}).

{rmk}

It is standard (
[
Sei14
, Lemma 5.1]
) that
\operatorname{Perf}_{dg}(X_{b(\kappa)}^{*})
gives a dg-enhancement of the triangulated category of perfect complexes on
X^{*}_{b(\kappa)}
. We also note that by the results of
[
LO10
,
CS18
]
, all dg-enhancements of this triangulated category are quasi-equivalent.

Proof 8.4

(Proof of Theorem
1.5
)
.

By construction, the category
\mathcal{B}^{dg}_{\kappa}:=\mathcal{B}^{dg}_{R}\otimes_{R_{B}}\Lambda
is a strict full subcategory of
\operatorname{Perf}_{dg}(X^{*}_{b(\kappa)})
containing the objects
\mathcal{O}(i)
. Moreover, these objects split generate
\operatorname{Perf}_{dg}(X^{*}_{b(\kappa)})
by
[
Orl09a
, Theorem 4]
.
The quasi-isomorphism
\mathcal{B}^{dg}_{R}\simeq\mathcal{B}_{R}
induces a quasi-isomorphism
\mathcal{B}^{dg}_{\kappa}\simeq\mathcal{B}_{\kappa}
.
As
X^{*}_{b(\kappa)}
is smooth,
\operatorname{Perf}_{dg}(X^{*}_{b(\kappa)})\cong D^{b}_{dg}Coh(X^{*}_{b(\kappa)})
.
Thus we have constructed a quasi-embedding

D^{b}_{dg}Coh(X^{*}_{b(\kappa)})\simeq\perf(\mathcal{B}_{\kappa})\hookrightarrow\perf(\mathcal{A}_{\kappa}^{\mathsf{bc}})\hookrightarrow\perf\EuF(X_{t,\kappa},D,\kappa;\Lambda)^{\mathsf{bc}};

this completes the proof of
Item

1
.

To prove
Item

2
, we first observe that because
X^{*}_{b(\kappa)}
is smooth,
\mathcal{B}_{\kappa}
is homologically smooth by
[
LS16
, Corollary 4.4]
.
By our assumption that
\EuF(X_{t,\kappa},D,\kappa;\Lambda)^{\mathsf{bc}}
satisfies the hypotheses enumerated in
[
SS21
, Section 2.5]
, we may apply automatic split-generation
[
Gan19
,
San21
]
,
which shows that the image of
\mathcal{B}_{\kappa}
in
\EuF(X_{t,\kappa},D,\kappa;\Lambda)^{\mathsf{bc}}
split-generates, because
\mathcal{B}_{\kappa}
is homologically smooth.
The result follows.

Appendix A
Adjoints to sectorial inclusions for arclike Lagrangians and the cap functor

Let
i:Y\to X
be an inclusion of Liouville sectors and
i_{*}:\mathcal{W}(Y)\to\mathcal{W}(X)
the corresponding functor between partially wrapped Fukaya categories defined in
[
GPS20
, Section 3.6]
. The functor
i_{*}
induces a pull-back functor on module categories
i^{*}:\operatorname{Mod}\mathcal{W}(X)\to\operatorname{Mod}\mathcal{W}(Y)
, the “large” right adjoint of
i_{*}
. In general, the pullback of a representable (i.e., Yoneda) module
i^{*}\mathcal{Y}_{L}
is not representable, reflecting the possibility that
i_{*}
may not have a right adjoint
\mathcal{W}(X)\to\mathcal{W}(Y)
on the level of the smaller categories. The first goal of this appendix is to exhibit a nice sub-class of Lagrangians in
X
, ‘negative pushoffs of arclike Lagrangians’ for which there is a clear geometric interpretation of the functor
i^{*}
, and to deduce from this the existence of a partial right adjoint on the level of the smaller categories (see
Theorem

82
and
Corollary

83
).

The second goal of this appendix is to then apply these general statements to the case that
X=S
is a Liouville sector with
Y=\Nbd(\partial S)=F\times T^{*}[0,1]
the boundary sector (strictly speaking, we may need to deform
S
to obtain boundary coordinates
\Nbd(\partial S)=F\times T^{*}[0,1]
; see
Section

A.3
. We will assume this is already done for the discussion here).
The composition of the fully faithful stabilization functor
stab:\mathcal{W}(F)\hookrightarrow\mathcal{W}(F\times T^{*}[0,1])
with the sector inclusion functor
i_{*}
is often denoted
\cup:\mathcal{W}(F)\to\mathcal{W}(S)
and called the
cup functor
.
Theorem

84
below asserts the existence of a partially defined right adjoint
\cap
, the
cap functor
, to
\cup
on the level of the smaller categories.

We say that a Lagrangian submanifold
K
of a Liouville sector
X
is
arclike
(
Definition

89
) if:

•

K
intersects
\partial X
transversely in
\partial K
and

•

there exists an
\alpha
-linear defining function
I:\partial X\to\R
(in the sense of
[
GPS20
, Definition 2.4]
) which is constant over
\partial K
, for some
\alpha>0
. For this
I
, we say
K
is ‘
I
-boundary-arclike’.

We note that by
Lemma

90
below, if
K
is arclike and
\alpha>0
is
any
positive number, there exists an
\alpha
-linear defining function
I:\partial X\to\mathbb{R}
for which
K
is
I
-boundary-arclike. By restricting to a level of such an
I
,
\partial K
can be viewed as an exact Lagrangian submanifold in the boundary sector
F=\partial X/\R
, inheriting all of its brane data from
K
. By
Lemma

93
, such an
I
function can always be extended to an
\alpha
-linear function defined on some neighbourhood
\Nbd^{Z}(\partial X)
of
\partial X
, which we call an ‘extended
\alpha
-defining function’, while still containing
K\cap\Nbd^{Z}(\partial X)
in a single level-set — if this condition holds for a given
K
and extended
I
, we say
K
is ‘
I
-arclike’ (see
Definition

92
).

Associated to an arclike Lagrangian
K
, there is a
(cylindrized) negative pushoff

K^{-}
(see §
A.3
), which is an object of
\mathcal{W}(X)
that (a priori depends on a choice of extended 1-defining function for which
K
is
I
-arclike but) is well-defined up to isomorphism (see
Section

A.4
).
The construction of this negative pushoff requires
X
to have
exact
boundary in the sense of
[
GPS20
, Definition 2.10]
so that
I
induces a Liouville identification
\Nbd^{Z}(\partial X)\cong F\times T^{*}[0,1]
, but this condition can always be arranged by a convex-at-infinity deformation of Liouville sectors which preserves the wrapped Fukaya category and the notion of
I
-arclike, see
Section

A.3
; hence, there is an induced isomorphism class of
K^{-}
even if
X
does not have exact boundary.

Theorem 82
.

Let
i:Y\to X
be an inclusion of Liouville sectors.
Let
K\subset X
be a (compact exact) Lagrangian
K
which is arclike for
X
such that
K\cap Y
is arclike for
Y
. Then there is an isomorphism of Yoneda modules

i^{*}\mathcal{Y}_{K^{-}}\simeq\mathcal{Y}_{(K\cap X)^{-}}.

In particular, there are functorial isomorphisms
Hom_{H\mathcal{W}(X)}(i(L),K^{-})=Hom_{H\mathcal{W}(Y)}(L,(K\cap X)^{-})
for any object
L
of
\mathcal{W}(Y)
(recall
Hom_{H\mathcal{C}}(B,A):=H^{*}(\hom_{\mathcal{C}}(A,B))
for an
A_{\infty}
category
\mathcal{C}
).

Corollary 83
.

Given
i:Y\to X
as above, let
\mathcal{P}({Y\subset X})\subset\mathcal{W}(X)
denote the full subcategory consisting of negative pushoffs (in
X
) of Lagrangians
K
such that
K\subset X
and
(K\cap Y)\subset Y
are both arclike.
Then,
i_{*}:\mathcal{W}(Y)\to\mathcal{W}(X)
has a partially defined right adjoint along
\mathcal{P}({Y\subset X})
,

i^{*}:\mathcal{P}({Y\subset X})\to\mathcal{W}(Y),

which on the level of objects sends
K^{-}\mapsto(K\cap Y)^{-}
(up to isomorphism).

Proof A.1
.

Apply
Corollary

87
to
Theorem

82
.

In the body of this article, we make use of the following application of the results above.
Let
(B,F_{0})
be a sutured Liouville domain, and
S
the associated Liouville sector as in
[
GPS20
, Definition 2.14]
. Fix an extended
\alpha=1
-defining function
I:\Nbd^{Z}(\partial S)\to\R
.
After a deformation of Liouville structure near
\partial S
as in
[
GPS20
, Proposition 2.28]
(see
Section

A.3
), the function
I
determines a boundary sector inclusion:
i:F\times T^{*}[0,1]\hookrightarrow S
covering a neighbourhood of
\partial S
, under which
I
becomes the projection to
\R
in
T^{*}[0,1]=[0,1]\times\R
, and where
F
is the completion of
F_{0}
.
By composing the induced functor
i_{*}
with stabilization
stab:\mathcal{W}(F)\to\mathcal{W}(F\times T^{*}[0,1])

[
GPS24a
, §8.4]
, one obtains a cup functor
\cup:\mathcal{W}(F)\to\mathcal{W}(S)
.

I
also determines a collection of
I
-arclike Lagrangians, which are (implicitly shrinking
F\times T^{*}[0,1]
if necessary or flowing) also
I
-arclike when intersected with
F\times T^{*}[0,1]
. By applying the above results to
i:F\times T^{*}[0,1]\to S
, we will obtain:

Theorem 84
.

Fix an extended
\alpha=1
-defining function
I
, and let
\mathcal{P}(S)\subset\mathcal{W}(S)
denote the associated full subcategory of negative pushoffs of
I
-arclike Lagrangians in
S
. The induced cup functor
\cup:\mathcal{W}(F)\to\mathcal{W}(S)
has a partially defined right adjoint along
\mathcal{P}(S)
, called the cap functor and denoted

\cap:\mathcal{P}(S)\to\mathcal{W}(F),

(A.1)

with image contained in the compact Fukaya category
\EuF(F)\subset\mathcal{W}(F)
and which sends
L^{-}
to an object isomorphic to
\partial L
.

After a brief subsection on algebraic preliminaries, the remaining subsections are devoted to the study of arclike Lagrangians and their negative pushoffs, and, from there, the proofs of
Theorem

82
and
Theorem

84
.

A.1
Results about
A_{\infty}
functors

To begin, we recall a few standard facts about modifying and constructing
A_{\infty}
functors which are used repeatedly in this appendix and elsewhere in the paper. The first fact concerns our ability to modify an
A_{\infty}
functor
\mathcal{F}
by replacing every object
\mathcal{F}(X)
by an isomorphic object. More generally, it allows us to modify
\mathcal{F}
to take values in any fully faithfully embedded category of the codomain with the same or larger essential image:

Lemma 85
.

Let
\mathcal{A}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{F}}\mathcal{C}\xleftarrow{\mathcal{G}}\mathcal{B}
be
A_{\infty}
functors, with
\mathcal{G}
cohomologically fully faithful, such that the image of
H(\mathcal{F})
is contained in the essential image of
H(\mathcal{G})
(i.e., for every object
X
of
\mathcal{A}
, there exists an object
Y
of
\mathcal{B}
such that
\mathcal{F}X
is quasi-isomorphic to
\mathcal{G}Y
).
Then, there exists an
A_{\infty}
functor
\mathcal{I}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
, unique up to quasi-isomorphism, such that
\mathcal{F}
is naturally quasi-isomorphic to
\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{I}
.
If
\mathcal{F}
is furthermore cohomologically fully faithful and has the same essential image as
\mathcal{G}
, then
\mathcal{I}
is a quasi-equivalence.

Proof A.2
.

We may replace
\mathcal{C}
with the essential image of
\mathcal{G}
.
As
\mathcal{G}
is cohomologically fully faithful, it is a quasi-equivalence onto this essential image.
Thus, we may invert it, see
[
Sei08
, Theorem 2.9]
, and set
\mathcal{I}=\mathcal{G}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{F}
(
\mathcal{G}^{-1}
is unique up to quasi-isomorphism; hence so is
\mathcal{I}
).
If
\mathcal{F}
is cohomologically fully faithful and has the same essential image as
\mathcal{G}
, then it is a quasi-equivalence onto the essential image of
\mathcal{G}
, so
\mathcal{I}
is also a quasi-equivalence.

The second fact, really a corollary of the first, concerns the construction of functors from representable bimodules.
Recall that given an
A_{\infty}
functor
f:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
, one can associate an
A_{\infty}

\mathcal{A}\!-\!\mathcal{B}
bimodule, the
right graph of
f

\Gamma_{f}^{R}=\hom_{\mathcal{B}}(f(-),-)
and a
\mathcal{B}\!-\!\mathcal{A}
bimodule, the
left graph of
f

\Gamma_{f}^{L}=\hom_{\mathcal{B}}(-,f(-))
(these are the two-sided pullbacks of the diagonal bimodule
\mathcal{B}_{\Delta}
along
(f,id)
and
(id,f)
respectively).
10
10

10

The confusing convention arises because when we define the cohomology category of an
A_{\infty}
category, the order of the inputs gets swapped:
\mathrm{Hom}^{*}_{H\mathcal{A}}(X,Y)\coloneqq H^{*}(hom^{*}(Y,X))
. In particular, after taking cohomology, our definitions of left/right graph (and subsequent definitions of left/right adjoint functors) agree with the usual ones. We remark that the cohomology category of an
A_{\infty}
category was defined in
[
She20
, Section 3.2]
as the
opposite
of the definition just stated (opposite in the usual sense, i.e., no Koszul signs involved); however that definition, when combined with the definition of the
A_{\infty}
category associated to a dg category [
op. cit.
, Definition 3.4], does not agree with the standard definition of the cohomology category of a dg category. The last-named author apologizes.

An
\mathcal{A}\!-\!\mathcal{B}
bimodule
\mathcal{P}
is said to be
right-representable
(respectively
left-representable
) if for all
x\in\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{P}(x,-)
(respectively
\mathcal{P}(-,y)
for all
y\in\mathcal{B}
) is representable, i.e., isomorphic to a Yoneda module.

Corollary 86
.

\mathcal{P}
is right representable if and only if there exists a functor
F:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
(unique up to isomorphism) such that the right graph of
F
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{P}
. Similarly, if
\mathcal{P}
is left representable, it must be isomorphic to the left graph of a (unique up to isomorphism) functor
G:\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
.

Proof A.3
.

For the first assertion, the “if” direction is tautological. For the “only if,” viewing
\mathcal{P}
as a functor from
\mathcal{P}:\mathcal{A}\to\operatorname{Mod}\mathcal{B}
, apply Lemma
85
to
\mathcal{A}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{P}}\operatorname{Mod}\mathcal{B}\xleftarrow{\mathcal{Y}}\mathcal{B}
where
\mathcal{Y}
is the Yoneda embedding. The Lemma also tells us
F
is unique up to isomorphism and that the graph of
F
, which as a functor
\mathcal{A}\to\operatorname{Mod}\mathcal{B}
is
\mathcal{Y}\circ F
, is isomorphic to
\mathcal{P}
. The second assertion is similar (and begins by viewing
\mathcal{P}
as
\mathcal{P}:\mathcal{B}^{op}\to\operatorname{Mod}\mathcal{A}^{op}
).

We will apply
Corollary

86
to the problem of constructing adjoints or partial adjoints to functors.
Recall that
f:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
is
right adjoint to
(or
has left adjoint
)
g:\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
if there is an isomorphism of
\mathcal{A}\!-\!\mathcal{B}
bimodules
\Gamma_{f}^{R}=\Gamma_{g}^{L}
.
More generally, given a subcategory
i:\mathcal{C}\subset\mathcal{B}
, we say that
f:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
has a
partially-defined left adjoint
along
\mathcal{C}
given by
g:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{A}
if there is an isomorphism of
\mathcal{A}\!-\!\mathcal{C}
bimodules between the
\mathcal{C}
-restricted right graph of
f
,
(\Gamma_{f}^{R})|_{\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{C}}=\hom_{\mathcal{B}}(f(-),i(-))
and the left graph of
g
,
\Gamma_{g}^{L}=\hom_{\mathcal{A}}(-,g(-))
.
An immediate consequence of Corollary
86
is:

Corollary 87
.

A functor
f:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
has a partially defined left adjoint along
i:\mathcal{C}\subset\mathcal{B}
if and only if the right graph of
f
restricted to
\mathcal{C}
,
(\Gamma_{f}^{R})|_{\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{C}}=\hom_{\mathcal{B}}(f(-),i(-))
is right representable; that is, if for every
c\in\mathcal{C}
, if the Yoneda module
i^{*}\hom_{\mathcal{B}}(-,c)
is representable. ∎

{rmk}

In the setting of
Corollary

87
, suppose that there exists a subcategory
\mathcal{D}\subset\mathcal{A}
such that for every
c\in\mathcal{C}
,
i^{*}\hom_{\mathcal{B}}(-,c)
is representable by an object of
\mathcal{D}
.
Then, by applying
Lemma

85
, we may assume that the partially-defined left adjoint
g
has image contained in
\mathcal{D}
.

A.2
Holomorphic curves in Liouville sectors with Lagrangians touching the boundary

Let
X
be a Liouville sector and fix a projection
\pi:\Nbd^{Z}(\partial X)\to\C_{\operatorname{Re}\geq 0}
as in
[
GPS20
, Definition 2.26]
. We begin by establishing a slight strengthening of the open mapping theorem argument from
[
GPS20
, Lemma 2.41]
, which will be used to confine disks with boundary on arclike Lagrangians in the following subsections.

Proposition 88
.

Let
\Sigma
be a Riemann surface with boundary. Suppose we have fixed a
J
on
X
such that
\pi:\Nbd^{Z}(\partial X)\to\C_{\operatorname{Re}\geq 0}
is
J
-holomorphic, and let
u:\Sigma\to X
be a
J
-holomorphic map. If
u^{-1}\pi^{-1}(\C_{\epsilon\geq\operatorname{Re}\geq 0})
is compact and its intersection with
\partial\Sigma
maps under
\pi\circ u
to a horizontal segment
\Gamma
, then
u^{-1}\pi^{-1}(\C_{\epsilon>\operatorname{Re}>0})
is either empty or consists of closed components of
\Sigma
along which
u
is constant.

Proof A.4
.

The proof follows
[
GPS20
, Lemma 2.41]
closely, and immediately extends to any more general
\Gamma
which is closed in
S:=\C_{\epsilon\geq\operatorname{Re}\geq 0}
, has empty interior and for which
S\backslash\Gamma
consists only of unbounded components. Let
P=(\pi\circ u)^{-1}(S)
, which is compact by hypothesis. Consider
f=\pi\circ u:P\to S
. Suppose for a contradiction that
f^{-1}(\mathrm{int}(S))
is neither empty nor a disjoint union of closed components along which
f
is constant.
It follows that there exists a connected component
Q
of
P
, such that
f(Q)
intersects
\mathrm{int}(S)
and
Q
is not a component of
\Sigma
on which
f
is constant. We have:

•

Q
is compact (as it is a closed subset of
P
, which is compact by hypothesis), hence
K:=f(Q)
is compact.

•

As
f|_{Q}^{-1}(\mathrm{int}(S))
is open and non-empty,
Q
contains an interior point
q
. As
Q
is not a component of
\Sigma
on which
f
is constant,
f
is not locally constant at
q
, by unique continuation. Therefore, by the open mapping theorem,
K
contains a neighbourhood of
f(q)
. Therefore
K
has non-empty intersection with the set
S^{\prime}=\mathrm{int}(S)\setminus\Gamma
, seeing as the latter is dense in
S
(as
\Gamma
has empty interior).

•

Let
Q^{\prime}=f^{-1}(S^{\prime})
. Note that
Q^{\prime}
is non-empty by the previous point, is open because
S^{\prime}
is open (as
\Gamma
is closed), and consists entirely of interior points of
\Sigma
because
u(\partial\Sigma)\subset\Gamma
is disjoint from
S^{\prime}
. Therefore, by the open mapping theorem,
K^{\prime}:=u(Q^{\prime})
is open and non-empty.

•

In addition to being open and non-empty,
K^{\prime}=K\cap S^{\prime}
is also closed in
S^{\prime}
, as
K
is compact; hence it is a non-empty union of connected components of
S^{\prime}
. However all connected components of
S^{\prime}
are unbounded, contradicting the compactness of
K
.

A.3
Arclike Lagrangians and their negative pushoffs

Let
X
be a Liouville sector and
K
a compact exact Lagrangian submanifold-with-boundary, intersecting
\partial X
transversely along its boundary
\partial K\subset\partial X
.

Definition 89
.

For an
\alpha
-defining function
I:\partial X\to\R
and a
K
as above, we say
K
is
I
-boundary-arclike if
I
is constant along
\partial K
. Call
K
arclike if one of the following equivalent conditions are satisfied (compare
[
GPS20
, Definition 2.4]
):

•

For some
\alpha>0
,
K
is
I
-boundary-arclike for some
\alpha
-defining function
I
.

•

For every
\alpha>0
,
K
is
I
-boundary-arclike for some
\alpha
-defining function
I
.

•

The projection of
\partial K\hookrightarrow\partial X
to the quotient by the characteristic foliation
F:=\partial X/\R
is an embedding.

Lemma 90
.

The three conditions appearing in Definition
89
are equivalent.

We make some preparatory remarks prior to the proof of
Lemma

90
.
Let
F=\partial X/\R
denote the leaf space of the characteristic foliation on
\partial X
, and
\pi:\partial X\to F
the quotient map. Fix some
\alpha
-defining function
I:\partial X\to\R
, inducing an identification
\partial X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\pi,I)}}{{\to}}F\times\R
under which
\pi
is projection to the first factor. We observe there is a large space of modifications of
I
coming from compactly supported functions on
F
(suitably cut off):

Lemma 91
.

Let
f:F\to\R
be any function with compact support contained inside
F_{0}\subseteq F
and
with
\pi^{*}f:\partial X\to\R
the lift. Then for any
N>0
there is a compactly supported
modification
\tilde{I}:\partial X\to\R
of
I
to an

\alpha
-defining function that is equal to
I-\pi^{*}f
on
F_{0}\times[-N,N]
.

Proof A.5
.

Pick an arbitrary compactly supported extension
\tilde{f}:F\times\R\to\R
of
\pi^{*}f|_{F_{0}\times[-N\times N]}
(e.g., multiply
\pi^{*}f
by a function of
I
which is equal to
1
for
|I|\leq N
and is compactly supported). As
\tilde{f}
is compactly supported,
|\partial_{t}\tilde{f}|
is bounded, where
t
denotes the coordinate on the
\R
factor. Therefore, by rescaling
\tilde{f}(y,t)\mapsto\tilde{f}(y,\frac{t}{K})
for sufficiently large
K
we obtain a function equal to

\pi^{*}f
on
F_{0}\times[-NK,NK]\supset F_{0}\times[-N,N]
where
|\partial_{t}\tilde{f}|<\frac{1}{2}
everywhere.
Now, set
\tilde{I}:=t-\tilde{f}(y,t)=I-\tilde{f}
. Observe that
\tilde{I}=I-\pi^{*}f
on
F_{0}\times[-N,N]
and satisfies
\partial_{t}\tilde{I}>\frac{1}{2}>0
, implying that
d\tilde{I}
is positive on the characteristic foliation of
\partial X
. Also at infinity
\tilde{I}=I
which is
\alpha
-linear.

Proof A.6

(Proof of Lemma
90
)
.

Evidently, the second condition implies the first condition implies the third (if
K
is
I
-boundary-arclike, then the embedding
\partial K\subset\partial X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\pi,I)}}{{\to}}F\times\R
is contained in
F\times\{c\}
for some
c
, and in particular embeds into
F
).
Therefore it suffices to prove that the third condition implies the second.
To do so, fix any
\alpha>0
and any
\alpha
-defining function
I:\partial X\to\R
inducing
\partial X\cong F\times\R_{t}
, and denote by
\overline{\partial K}:=\pi(\partial K)\subset F
. Assuming the third condition,
\overline{\partial K}
is a submanifold of
F
and
there is a function
f:\overline{\partial K}\to\R
whose graph is
\partial K
, namely
f:=I|_{\partial K}\circ\pi^{-1}|_{\overline{\partial K}}
.
Extend
f
to a compactly supported function on
F
whose support lies in a Liouville domain
F_{0}
containing
\partial K
. By Lemma
91
, we may modify
I
to another defining function
\tilde{I}
which equals
I-\pi^{*}f
on
F_{0}\times[-N,N]
where
N
is such that
\partial K\subset F_{0}\times[-N,N]
. Now, at any point
p\in\partial K
,
\tilde{I}(p)=I(p)-\pi^{*}f(p)=I(p)-I|_{\partial K}(p)=0
.

It will be helpful for the following Lemma to explicitly present our given Liouville sector
X
as the completion of a compact codimension-0 domain
X^{in}
with respect to the Liouville vector field along one of the boundary faces of
X^{in}
, analogous to the presentation of a Liouville manifold as the completion of a Liouville domain. As in the proof of
[
GPS20
, Lemma 2.5]
, we produce
X^{in}
by first picking a contact type hypersurface
Y
in
X
(sufficiently close to
\infty
) transversely intersecting
\partial X
in the region where
Z
is tangent to
\partial X
, whose projection to
\partial_{\infty}X
via Liouville flow is a diffeomorphism. The flow by
Z
from
Y
determines a symplectization region
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times Y\subset X
, and by definition
X^{in}
is the complement of this region
X\backslash(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times Y)
.
X^{in}
is a manifold with corners with two boundary faces,
Y
and
(\partial X)^{in}
(which is a codimension-0 subdomain of
\partial X
).

As in
[
GPS20
, §2.6]
, it is useful to consider extensions of an
\alpha
-defining function
I:\partial X\to\R
to
\alpha
-linear functions
I:\Nbd^{Z}(\partial X)\to\R
, which we call here
extended
\alpha
-defining functions
.

Definition 92
.

Let
K
be a compact exact Lagrangian-with-boundary
K\subset X
which intersects
\partial X
transversely along
\partial K\subset\partial X
, and
I:\Nbd^{Z}(\partial X)\to\R
an extended
\alpha
-defining function.
K
is called
I
-arclike if, after possibly shrinking
\Nbd^{Z}(\partial X)
,
I
is constant along
K\cap\Nbd^{Z}(\partial X)
.

Evidently, if
K
is
I
-arclike for a given
I
, then
K
is also
I|_{\partial X}
-boundary-arclike (and therefore arclike). Conversely we have:

Lemma 93
.

If
K
is arclike, then for any
\alpha>0
there exists an extended
\alpha
-defining function
I:\Nbd^{Z}(\partial X)\to\R
for which
K
is
I
-arclike.

Proof A.7
.

For any
\alpha
, fix an
\alpha
-defining function
I:\partial X\to\R
for which
K
is
I
-boundary-arclike, which exists by
Lemma

90
. With respect to a choice of domain
X^{in}
completing to
X
as described above
Lemma

90
, we will extend
I
first along the intersection
(\Nbd^{Z}(\partial X))\cap X^{in}
, then extend to all of
\Nbd^{Z}(\partial X)
by
\alpha
-linearity.
Recall that the boundary of
X^{in}
is a union of two faces:
(\partial X)^{in}\subset\partial X
, and
Y
, along which
Z
is outward pointing.
Over
\Nbd^{Z}(\partial X)\cap X^{in}
