one can construct an extension by pulling back
I
along the projection to
(\partial X)^{in}
associated to a choice of collar identification
\Nbd(\partial X)^{in}\cong(\partial X)^{in}\times[0,\epsilon)
induced by an inward pointing vector field defined near
(\partial X)^{in}
that is tangent to
Y
. We may further choose this inward pointing vector field to commute with
Z
near
Y
(so that the resulting extension of
I
remains
\alpha
-linear near
Y
hence smoothly extends to
\Nbd^{Z}(\partial X)
), and to be tangent to
K
(here we use the requirement that
K
is transverse to
\partial X
). This implies that the extension — also called
I
by abuse of notation — is constant along
K
.

Henceforth we fix such an extension
I
for some
\alpha
for which
K
is
I
-arclike.
Since
I|_{K}
is constant near
\partial X
, it follows that:

Lemma 94
.

At points of
K
near
\partial X
, the Hamiltonian vector field
\mathsf{X}_{I}
is tangent to
K
.

Proof A.8
.

Constancy of
I
along
K
implies
T_{p}K\subset T_{p}I^{-1}(c)

(where
I|_{K}\equiv c
), or equivalently that
\mathrm{span}(\mathsf{X}_{I})=(T_{p}I^{-1}(c))^{\omega\perp}
is contained in
T_{p}K=(T_{p}K)^{\omega\perp}
.

By
[
GPS20
, Proposition 2.25]
, the extended
\alpha
-linear function
I
determines a product decomposition

(\Nbd^{Z}(\partial X),\lambda_{X}|_{\Nbd^{Z}(\partial X)})=(F\times\C_{Re\geq 0},\lambda_{F}+\lambda^{\alpha}_{\C}+df),

(A.2)

over neighbourhoods of
\partial X
and
F\times\{Re=0\}
respectively, where the coordinates on
\C_{Re\geq 0}
are
R+iI=x+iy
,

\lambda_{F}=(\lambda_{X})|_{F:=I^{-1}(0)}
,
\lambda^{\alpha}_{\C}
is the Liouville form for
\omega_{\C}=dx\wedge dy
associated to Liouville vector field
Z_{\C}^{\alpha}=(1-\alpha)x\frac{\partial}{\partial x}+\alpha y\frac{\partial}{\partial y}
, and the function
f
is a function on
F\times\C_{Re\geq 0}
satisfying properties detailed in
loc. cit.

This product decomposition is induced by defining

R:\Nbd^{Z}(\partial X)\to\R,

(A.3)

to be the unique function characterized by the properties
R|_{\partial X}=0
,
dR(\mathsf{X}_{I})=-1
.

Lemma 95
.

If
K
is
I
-arclike, then
R|_{K}
is a submersion from
K
to
\R
over
\Nbd(\partial K):=\Nbd^{Z}(\partial X)\cap K
.

Proof A.9
.

By
Lemma

94
,
\mathsf{X}_{I}
must be tangent to
K
, and
dR(\mathsf{X}_{I})=-1
, hence
dR|_{TK}\neq 0
.

It follows that in the product coordinates
F\times\C_{Re\geq 0}
,
K
simply takes the form
\partial K\times\gamma
where
\gamma
is an arc of the form
\{Im=c\}
. By restricting to
I^{-1}(c)
, we see that
\partial K
is exact for
\lambda_{X}|_{I^{-1}(c)}=\lambda_{F}+df|_{I^{-1}(c)}
, hence exact for
\lambda_{F}
. Let
g_{K}:K\to\R
denote a chosen primitive of
\lambda_{X}|_{K}
. The restricted primitive for
\partial K
with respect to
\lambda_{F}
is by definition
g_{\partial K}:=(g_{K})|_{\partial K}-f|_{\partial K}
. Pick a primitive
g_{\gamma}
for
\gamma\subset\C_{Re\geq 0}
with respect to
\lambda_{\C}^{\alpha}
which is equal to
0
at
(R,I)=(0,c)
. Now observe that in
F\times\C_{Re\geq 0}
, with respect to the Liouville form
\lambda_{X}=\lambda_{F}+\lambda_{\C}^{\alpha}+df
, our (globally defined) primitive
g_{K}
for
K
must be equal to
g_{\partial K}+g_{\gamma}+f
(as both are primitives of
K=\partial K\times\gamma
in this region which agree along
\partial K\times(0,c)
).

Next, we explain how, if
X
has
exact boundary
in the sense of
[
GPS20
, Definition 2.10]
, to modify an arclike Lagrangian
K
in
X
into a cylindrical Lagrangian

K^{-}\subset X

(A.4)

avoiding
\partial X
(and hence defining an object of
\mathcal{W}(X)
), called its
(cylindrized) negative pushoff
. By construction, this modification is supported in an arbitrarily small cylindrical-at-infinity neighbourhood of
\partial X
. The exactness condition on the boundary is used to obtain an embedding of Liouville sectors
F\times T^{*}[0,1]\hookrightarrow\Nbd^{Z}(X)
covering a neighborhood of the boundary, or equivalently a Liouville identification
\Nbd^{Z}(X)\cong F\times T^{*}[0,1]
up to a compactly supported exact 1-form. As we recall, the exact boundary condition can always be arranged up to a deformation:

{rmk}

[Boundary sectors]

We recall here how an extended
\alpha=1
-defining function
I
induces a boundary sector inclusion
(F\times T^{*}[0,1],\lambda_{F}+\lambda_{T^{*}[0,1]})\hookrightarrow X
after possibly deforming the Liouville form near
\partial X
(cf. the proof of
[
GPS20
, Theorem 2.28]
). First,
I
induces an associated product decomposition (
A.2
), where
\lambda_{\C}^{1}=\lambda_{T^{*}\R_{\geq 0}}
. Now, after applying the convex-at-infinity Liouville deformation
\lambda_{X}\leadsto\lambda_{X}^{\prime}
described in the proof of
[
GPS20
, Proposition 2.28]
, we can arrange for
\lambda_{X}^{\prime}
in coordinates to simply equal
\lambda_{F}+\lambda_{T^{*}\R\geq 0}
for
R
near
0
and equal
\lambda_{X}
for
R
away from
0
(concretely in coordinates (
A.2
),
\lambda_{X}^{\prime}=\lambda_{F}+\lambda_{T^{*}\R_{\geq 0}}+d(\varphi(R)f)
for a cutoff function
\varphi
which equals
0
for
R\in[0,\epsilon]
and
1
for
R\notin[0,2\epsilon]
). The outcome is a (cylindrical-at-infinity) Liouville sector embedding
F\times T^{*}[0,\epsilon]\to(X,\lambda_{X}^{\prime})
. Now identify
T^{*}[0,\epsilon]=T^{*}[0,1]
via
(R,I)\mapsto(\frac{1}{\epsilon}R,\epsilon I)
.

If
X
already has exact boundary in the sense of
[
GPS20
, Definition 2.10]
, then after possibly modifying
\lambda_{F}
by a compactly supported exact 1-form, one can make the
df
term in (
A.2
) compactly supported. This gives a boundary sector embedding
F\times T^{*}[0,1]\hookrightarrow X
without further deforming
\lambda_{X}
; see the discussion below
[
GPS20
, Definition 2.33]
for more details. Indeed, the deformation from
[
GPS20
, Proposition 2.28]
was used to show that any
X
can be deformed (in a homotopically unique way) to have exact boundary.

Returning to the construction of
K^{-}
, use
Lemma

90
and
Lemma

93
to
fix an extended
\alpha=1
-defining function
I
for which
K
is
I
-arclike.
As we are assuming
X
has exact boundary (or implicitly deforming
\lambda_{X}
to do so as in
Section

A.3
),
we may consider the resulting boundary sector embedding
F\times T^{*}[0,1]\hookrightarrow X
(where
\partial X
is the image of
F\times T^{*}[0,1]|_{0}
); in this smaller region,
Lemma

95
implies that
K
must have the form
\partial K\times\gamma
, where
\gamma:[0,1]\hookrightarrow T^{*}[0,1]
is of the form
\{I=c\}
(this constant may differ from the original
c
as we may scale
I
per
Section

A.3
). The Liouville deformation performed in
Section

A.3
also changes the primitive in this region to
g_{\partial K}+g_{\gamma}
.
Let
\gamma^{-}
be a smoothing of
\{R=\epsilon,I\geq c\}\cup\{R\geq\epsilon,I=c\}
; concretely, one could take

\gamma^{-}:=(\gamma^{-}_{R},\gamma^{-}_{I}):(0,1]\to T^{*}[0,1]=[0,1]_{R}\times\R_{I}
any properly embedded curve satisfying:

•

\gamma^{-}
agrees with
\gamma
(i.e., the segment
I=c
) near
t=1

•

\gamma^{-}
lies on the cotangent fiber at
R=\epsilon
near
t=0

•

\gamma^{-}_{R}
is weakly monotonically increasing in
t
and
\gamma^{-}_{I}
is weakly monotonically decreasing in
t
. In particular, since
d\gamma^{-}\neq 0
,
\gamma^{-}_{R}
is strictly monotonically increasing near
t=1
and
\gamma^{-}_{I}
is strictly monotonically decreasing near
t=0
.

Let
K^{-}_{split}:=(K\backslash(\partial K\times\gamma))\cup(\partial K\times\gamma^{-})
.
K^{-}_{split}
is evidently Lagrangian and, since the codimension-0 inclusions of
K\backslash(\partial K\times\gamma)
into both
K
and
K^{-}_{split}
are homotopy equivalences,
K^{-}_{split}
is exact and inherits all brane data from choices of such data on
K
. Moreover, outside a compact set,
K^{-}_{split}=\partial K\times(T^{*}_{\epsilon})^{+}
where
(T^{*}_{\epsilon})^{+}=\{R=\epsilon,I>0\}
is the positive part of the cotangent fiber to
\epsilon
.
K^{-}_{split}
inherits a primitive of the form
g_{\partial K}+g_{\gamma^{-}}
(which extends to
g_{K}
up to a constant shift in the region where
K^{-}_{split}
is unmodified), where without loss of generality
g_{\gamma^{-}}
vanishes outside a compact subset of
\gamma^{-}
.
Applying the cylindrization procedure of
[
GPS24a
, §7.2]
in the product chart
F\times T^{*}[0,1]
, we therefore obtain a cylindrical exact Lagrangian
K^{-}
which is well-defined and independent of choice of
\epsilon
or smoothing up to isotopy. A priori
K^{-}
depends on the choice of 1-defining
I
function for which
K
is
I
-arclike; however we will see that any two choices give isomorphic objects (see
Section

A.4
).

{rmk}

The definition of cylindrization of a Lagrangian equal (outside a compact set) to
\partial K\times\gamma^{-}
in
F\times T^{*}[0,1]
given in
[
GPS24a
, §7.2]
involves flowing near infinity (and outside the aforementioned compact set) by an isotopy taking place in the larger stopped convex completion
F\times(\C,\{\pm\infty\})\supset F\times T^{*}[0,1]
, which is well-defined up to contractible choice. However, as
loc. cit.
describes, the cylindrization isotopy can be made to be supported in an arbitrarily small neighborhood of
\mathfrak{f}\times\partial_{\infty}\gamma^{-}
(where
\mathfrak{f}
is the core of
F
and using the fact that
\partial_{\infty}\partial K=\emptyset
) — and in particular within
F\times T^{*}[0,1]
.

Lemma 96
.

The negative pushoff
K^{-}
is forward stopped, i.e., it admits a cofinal positive wrapping converging into
\partial X
.

Proof A.10
.

Choose a product-like cofinal wrapping of
K^{-}_{split}
converging to
\partial X
. Now,
[
GPS24a
, Proposition 7.5]
says that (suitable cylindrizations of) products of cofinal wrappings remain cofinal. While the original result was stated for stopped Liouville manifolds, the result remains true in this case because wrappings of a given Lagrangian
L
in a Liouville sector
Y
are cofinal if and only if they are cofinal when thought of as being wrappings in the stopped convex completion
(\bar{Y},\partial_{\infty}\bar{Y}\backslash(\partial_{\infty}Y)^{\circ})
(cf.
[
GPS24a
, Lemma 3.8]
).

{rmk}

Dually, there is a corresponding
(cylindrized) positive pushoff

K^{+}
, obtained by applying the same construction to
K
using the curve
\gamma^{+}
defined by negating the
I
coordinate of
\gamma^{-}
. Identical arguments imply
K^{+}
is ‘backward stopped’, i.e., cofinally negatively wraps into the stop.

For the next lemma, recall that for any Liouville manifold
F
there is a stabilization functor
stab:\mathcal{W}(F)\to\mathcal{W}(F\times T^{*}[0,1])
, which sends
L
to the cylindrization of
L\times[\mathrm{fiber}]

[
GPS24a
, §8.4]
.

Lemma 97
.

Let
\gamma=\{I=c\}\subset T^{*}[0,1]=[0,1]_{R}\times\R_{I}
, and let
V\subset F
be a compact exact Lagrangian. Then
V\times\gamma\subset F\times T^{*}[0,1]
is
I
-arclike near
0
and
-I
-arclike near
1
, and with respect to these functions, its negative pushoff
(V\times\gamma)^{-}
is isotopic to the stabilization
stab(V)
.

Proof A.11
.

The first assertion (that
I
near
0
and
-I
near
1
are
(\alpha=1)
defining functions, and that they make
V\times\gamma
arclike near
0
and
1
) is tautological. We can then run the negative pushoff construction near
R=0
by using the embedding

F\times T^{*}[0,1]\xrightarrow{(f,R,I)\mapsto(f,\delta R,I/\delta)}F\times T^{*}[0,\delta]\hookrightarrow F\times T^{*}[0,1],

and near
R=1
by using the embedding

F\times T^{*}[0,1]\xrightarrow{(f,R,I)\mapsto(f,1-\delta R,-I/\delta)}F\times T^{*}[1-\delta,1]\hookrightarrow F\times T^{*}[0,1].

The resulting negative pushoff is a cylindrization of
\partial K\times\tilde{f}
where
\tilde{f}
, a smoothing of
\{R=\delta\epsilon,I\geq c\}\cup\{\delta\epsilon\leq R\leq 1-\delta\epsilon,I=c\}\cup\{R=1-\delta\epsilon,I\leq c\}
, is isotopic to a cotangent fiber. By cylindrizing the induced isotopy between
\partial K\times[\mathrm{fiber}]
and
\partial K\times\tilde{f}
as in
[
GPS24a
, Lemma 7.3]
we obtain a cylindrical isotopy between
(V\times\gamma)^{-}
and
stab(V)
.

A.4
Wrapped Floer theory with arclike Lagrangians

Let
X
be a Liouville sector and
K
an arclike Lagrangian in
X
.
Using
Lemma

90
and
Lemma

93
to fix an
extended
\alpha=\frac{1}{2}
-defining function
I
for which
K
is

I
-arclike, we obtain by
Lemma

95
a map
\pi:\Nbd^{Z}(\partial X)\to\C_{\operatorname{Re}\geq 0}
in the sense of
[
GPS20
, Def
2.26]
such that
\pi|_{K\cap\Nbd^{Z}(\partial X)}

submerses
K\cap\Nbd^{Z}(\partial X)
onto a horizontal segment
\Gamma
.

Lemma 98
.

K
induces a well-defined module
CF^{*}(K,-)
over the wrapped Fukaya category
\mathcal{W}(X)
(defined implicitly using a fixed choice of
\pi
as above), whose cohomology is given by ordinary (rather than wrapped) Floer homology. The resulting module is independent up to isomorphism of choices of Floer data made in its construction.

Proof A.12
.

For any cylindrical Lagrangian
L
which is transverse to
K
,
Proposition

88
along with monotonicity as in
[
GPS20
, Proposition 3.19]
implies that the Floer chain complex
CF^{*}(K,L)
is well-defined for a generic cylindrical (near infinity)
J
which makes this
\pi:\Nbd^{Z}(\partial X)\to\C_{\operatorname{Re}\geq 0}
holomorphic (recall from
[
GPS20
, §2.10.1]
that, as
\pi
was built from an
\alpha=\frac{1}{2}
-defining function, there is an abundance of such
J
satisfying both conditions simultaneously). The same arguments imply that we can define
A_{\infty}
operations of the form
CF^{*}(K,L_{0})\otimes CF^{*}(L_{0},L_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes CF^{*}(L_{k-1},L_{k})\to CF^{*}(K,L_{k})
for any tuple of
L_{0},\ldots,L_{k}
of cylindrical exact Lagrangians avoiding
\partial X
mutually transverse to each other and
K
and any surface-dependent
J
which is uniformly cylindrical in the sense of
[
GPS20
, §3.2]
, makes
\pi:\Nbd^{Z}(\partial X)\to\C_{\operatorname{Re}\geq 0}
holomorphic (shrinking this neighbourhood when applying
Proposition

88
if needed so it doesn’t intersect any
L_{i}
), and achieves transversality.

To construct the desired
A_{\infty}
module, modify/augment the construction of the wrapped Fukaya category
\mathcal{W}(X):=\mathcal{O}[C^{-1}]
from
[
GPS20
, §3.5]
as follows. First, in the construction of the directed category
\mathcal{O}
, only use Lagrangians which are transversal to
K
(imposing such a constraint on objects is unproblematic, see, e.g.,
[
GPS20
, Remark 3.28]
). Then, add a final object
K
to the directed category, so
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}:=\langle\Z,\mathcal{O}\rangle
. The construction of Floer data for
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
and
A_{\infty}
operations from that data carries through given the above discussion, in particular using
Proposition

88
. By localizing
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
at the continuation elements
C
in
\mathcal{O}
, we obtain a semi-orthogonal decomposition
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}:=\langle\Z,\mathcal{W}\rangle
, and hence an induced
\mathcal{W}
-module
\hom_{\mathcal{W}^{\prime}}(K,-)|_{\mathcal{W}}
. Note that this latter module
\hom_{\mathcal{W}^{\prime}}(K,-)|_{\mathcal{W}}
, which we might call “
CW^{*}(K,-)
’’
11
11

11

For instance, its cohomology can be calculated by taking the direct limit of
HF^{*}(K,L^{-})
over a cofinal collection of negative wrappings of
L
.
is by definition the localization with respect to
C
,
CF^{*}(K,-)_{C^{-1}}
, of the module
CF^{*}(K,-)=\hom_{\mathcal{O}^{\prime}}(K,-)|_{\mathcal{O}}
in the sense of
[
GPS20
, Definition 3.17]
.

Now, because
K
is compact, the cylindrical Lagrangians
L\subset X
and positive isotopies thereof
L\leadsto L^{+}
considered in the wrapping category of
X
do not intersect
K
at infinity and by definition do not touch
\partial X
; hence, these isotopies stay disjoint from
\Nbd^{Z}(\partial X)
(by shrinking this neighbourhood if necessary). As a result, the usual invariance arguments (cf.
[
GPS20
, §3.3]
) imply that the continuation maps
HF^{*}(K,L^{+})\to HF^{*}(K,L)
are defined and induce isomorphisms.
This can be reformulated as saying that for any cone
cone(c)
of any continuation element
c\in C
,
CF^{*}(K,cone(c))
is acyclic. It follows from

[
GPS20
, Lemma 3.13]
that the natural map of
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
-modules
CF^{*}(K,-)\to\hom_{\mathcal{W}^{\prime}}(K,-)
is a quasi-isomorphism; hence, the same is true of the restriction of these modules to
\mathcal{O}
. In particular,
CF^{*}(K,-)\cong CW^{*}(K,-)|_{\mathcal{O}}
. Going forward, we therefore refer to the latter module
CW^{*}(K,-)
over
\mathcal{W}(Y)
simply as
CF^{*}(K,-)
.

Finally, to check independence of choices, suppose we have made two constructions of directed categories
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}_{1}=\langle\Z,\mathcal{O}_{1}\rangle
and
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}_{2}=\langle\Z,\mathcal{O}_{2}\rangle
, for same or possibly different
\mathcal{O}_{1}
and
\mathcal{O}_{2}
computing the same wrapped Fukaya category, and for different choices of Floer data for tuples containing
K
(to extend to
\mathcal{O}_{i}^{\prime}
); which give different modules
CF^{*}(K,-)
over
\mathcal{O}_{i}[C_{i}^{-1}]
for
i=1,2
. We can include
\mathcal{O}_{1}\coprod\mathcal{O}_{2}
into a sufficiently wrapped (in the sense of
[
GPS24a
, Definition 2.15]
, see also
[
GPS20
, Proposition 3.39]
)
\mathcal{O}_{3}
consisting of Lagrangians transverse to
K
, and then build
\mathcal{O}_{3}^{\prime}:=\langle\Z,\mathcal{O}_{3}\rangle
by inductively extending the previously chosen Floer data for tuples containing
K
involving
\mathcal{O}_{1}
and
\mathcal{O}_{2}
separately. The construction of the previous paragraph applied to
\mathcal{O}_{3}^{\prime}
then produces a module
CF^{*}(K,-)
which restricts along the quasi-isomorphisms
\mathcal{O}_{i}[C_{i}^{-1}]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}\mathcal{O}_{3}[C_{3}^{-1}]
(
i=1,2
) to the two previously constructed modules, therefore implying they are isomorphic.

The modules constructed in
Lemma

98
satisfy a nice locality property with respect to Liouville sector inclusions in the following sense:

Lemma 99
.

If
K
is an arclike Lagrangian in
X
, and
Y\subset X
is a Liouville subsector such that
K\cap Y
is also an arclike Lagrangian in
Y
, then there is an isomorphism of
\mathcal{W}(Y)
-modules between
i^{*}CF^{*}(K,-)
(where
i:\mathcal{W}(Y)\to\mathcal{W}(X)
is the inclusion functor and
CF^{*}(K,-)
is constructed by applying
Lemma

98
to
K\subset X
) and
CF^{*}(K\cap Y,-)
(constructed by applying
Lemma

98
to
(K\cap Y)\subset Y
).

Proof A.13
.

We work with a chain-level model for
\mathcal{W}(X):=\mathcal{O}_{X}[C_{X}^{-1}]
for which the chain-level inclusion functor
i:\mathcal{W}(Y)\to\mathcal{W}(X)
comes from a directed subcategory
\mathcal{O}_{Y}\subset\mathcal{O}_{X}
as in
[
GPS20
, §3.6]
.
In the construction of
CF^{*}(K,-)
in
Lemma

98
, we can arrange (given the independence of the outcome from choices of Floer data up to isomorphism) that the Floer datum used for any tuple
(K,L_{0},\ldots,L_{k})
with
L_{0},\ldots,L_{k}\in\mathcal{O}_{Y}
coincides, after restricting to
Y
, with the Floer datum used for the tuple
(K\cap Y,L_{0},\ldots,L_{k})
and, in particular, makes
\pi=R+iI:\Nbd^{Z}\partial Y\to\C_{\operatorname{Re}\geq 0}

J
-holomorphic for an
I
-function for which
K\cap Y
is
I
-arclike.
Proposition

88
therefore implies that the associated
A_{\infty}
operations for such tuples agree. Hence, we obtain the isomorphism from a chain level equality of
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
modules
CF^{*}(K\cap Y,-)=CF^{*}(K,-)|_{\mathcal{O}_{Y}}
. By
Lemma

98
,
CF^{*}(K,-)
is, as an
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module, isomorphic to the restriction of the corresponding (localized)
\mathcal{W}(X)
module. Hence, we learn that the corresponding localized
\mathcal{W}(X)
module restricts along
\mathcal{O}_{Y}\to\mathcal{O}_{X}\to\mathcal{W}(X)
to
CF^{*}(K\cap Y,-)
. Since the restriction from
\mathcal{W}(X)
to
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
factors through the localization
\mathcal{O}_{Y}\to\mathcal{W}(Y)
, it follows that the
\mathcal{W}(X)
module
CF^{*}(K,-)
restricts to the corresponding (localized)
\mathcal{W}(Y)
module
CF^{*}(K\cap Y,-)
.

{rmk}

As constructed in
Lemma

98
, the module
CF^{*}(K,-)
over
\mathcal{W}(X)
appears to depend implicitly on a fixed choice of
\pi
for which
K
is
\operatorname{Im}\pi
-arclike. However, thanks to
Lemma

99
, one can see that
CF^{*}(K,-)
is independent of this choice of
\pi
along the same lines as the discussion above
[
GPS20
, Convention 3.1]
(which addressed the independence of
\mathcal{W}(X)
from
\pi
). The argument is as follows: given constructions
CF_{1}^{*}(K,-)
over
\mathcal{W}_{1}(X)
using
\pi_{1}=R_{1}+iI_{1}
and
CF_{2}^{*}(K,-)
over
\mathcal{W}_{2}(X)
using
\pi_{2}=R_{2}+iI_{2}
, one observes that there exists a trivial inclusion
X^{-\delta}\subset X
for which
K\cap X^{-\delta}
is still arclike (e.g., image of a small inward flow by either of the
X_{I_{i}}
). Now
Lemma

99
tells us that
CF_{1}^{*}(K,-)
and
CF_{2}^{*}(K,-)
both restrict to the same
CF^{*}(K\cap X^{-\delta},-)
(constructed using any
\pi
for which
K\cap X^{-\delta}
is
\operatorname{Im}\pi
-arclike). Since trivial inclusions are quasi-equivalences
[
GPS20
, Lemma 3.41]
, it follows that the induced quasi-equivalence
\mathcal{W}_{1}(X)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\leftarrow}}\mathcal{W}(X^{-\delta})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathcal{W}_{2}(X)
identifies
CF_{1}^{*}(K,-)
up to isomorphism with
CF^{*}_{2}(K,-)
.

{rmk}

Let us look at an example where the above locality could possibly fail if
K
is allowed to be non-compact (even if it has empty boundary). Consider
K=
a cotangent fiber inside
Y=T^{*}[0,1]
, included into
X=T^{*}S^{1}
. Evidently, making the same choices as before
CF^{*}_{X}(K,-)
as an
\mathcal{O}_{X}
module restricts along
\mathcal{O}_{Y}\subset\mathcal{O}_{X}
to
CF^{*}_{Y}(K\cap Y,-)=CF^{*}_{Y}(K,-)
as an
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
module. However, if
CW^{*}_{X}(K,-)
(the localization of the former module) were to restrict to
CW^{*}_{Y}(K,-)
, we would learn (by testing against a fiber) that the self-wrapped Floer homology of a cotangent fiber in
T^{*}[0,1]
and
T^{*}S^{1}
agree which is false. What fails in the above argument is that the
\mathcal{O}_{X}
module
CF_{X}^{*}(K,-)
is not equivalent to its localization
CW^{*}_{X}(K,-)|_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}
, hence arguments involving restricting
\mathcal{O}_{X}
modules don’t necessarily descend to localized modules.

Now, let
Y\subset X
be a subsector and
K\subset X
a cylindrical Lagrangian such that
K^{in}:=K\cap Y
is an arclike (and, in particular, compact) Lagrangian. Let us further assume that the Legendrian
\partial_{\infty}K
, which is therefore contained in
\partial_{\infty}X\backslash\partial_{\infty}Y
, admits a cofinal wrapping which does not cross
\partial\partial_{\infty}Y
.
Note that
K^{in}
, an arclike Lagrangian in
Y
, induces a module
CF^{*}(K^{in},-)
over
\mathcal{W}(Y)
by
Lemma

98
. There is another module over
\mathcal{W}(Y)
induced by
K
, namely the Yoneda module over
K
(a
\mathcal{W}(X)
module) restricted to
\mathcal{W}(Y)
along the inclusion functor,
i^{*}\hom_{\mathcal{W}(X)}(K,-)
. The next result says that these modules are equal:

Lemma 100
.

In the situation above, there is an isomorphism of
\mathcal{W}(Y)
-modules between

i^{*}\hom_{\mathcal{W}(X)}(K,-)
and
CF^{*}(K^{in},-)
(the module constructed by applying
Lemma

98
to
K^{in}\subset Y
).

Proof A.14
.

We implicitly work with a chain-level model for
\mathcal{W}(X):=\mathcal{O}_{X}[C_{X}^{-1}]
for which the chain-level inclusion functor
i:\mathcal{W}(Y)\to\mathcal{W}(X)
comes from a directed subcategory
\mathcal{O}_{Y}\subset\mathcal{O}_{X}
. We choose
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
arbitrarily to consist of objects transversal to
K^{in}
.
Next, we choose
\mathcal{O}_{X}
by first appending
K
to be greater than all elements of
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
, and then continuing to add elements so that
\mathcal{O}_{X}
contains every isotopy class of Lagrangians in
X
and is sufficiently wrapped in the sense of
[
GPS24a
, Definition 2.15]
(see also
[
GPS20
, Proposition 3.39]
), meaning every Lagrangian
L
added after
K
possesses a cofinal wrapping (
L=L^{0}\leadsto L^{1}\leadsto\cdots
) in
X
which is also cofinal in the poset
\mathcal{O}_{X}
(an “
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-wrapping sequence”). See
[
GPS20
, §3.6]
for one such construction. For
K
, we can also specifically arrange for its
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-wrapping sequence
\{K^{i}\}
to be induced by a cofinal collection of draggings (in the sense of
[
GPS24a
, Lemma 2.2]
)
of
K
along the hypothesized cofinal-in-
\partial_{\infty}X
wrapping
(\partial_{\infty}K)^{t}\subset(\partial_{\infty}X\backslash\partial_{\infty}Y)
.

Finally, in order to construct the module
CF^{*}(K^{in},-)
, we append a maximal element
K^{in}
to
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
as in
Lemma

98
and call the result
\mathcal{O}_{Y}^{\prime}
. When choosing Floer data for
\mathcal{O}_{Y}^{\prime}
and respectively
\mathcal{O}_{X}
, we can arrange for any sequence of elements
L_{0}>\cdots>L_{k}
in
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
(contained in
Y
) that the Floer data chosen to define the
A_{\infty}
structure maps for the sequence
(K^{in},L_{0},\ldots,L_{k})
in
\mathcal{O}_{Y}^{\prime}
(as in
Lemma

98
) and the sequence
(K,L_{0},\ldots,L_{k})
in
\mathcal{O}_{X}

agree within
Y
and use an almost-complex structure
J
making
\pi=R+iI:\Nbd^{Z}\partial Y\to\C_{\operatorname{Re}\geq 0}

J
-holomorphic, for a
\pi
with
I
making
K^{in}
arclike (this is possible because these tuples are equal when intersected with
Y
, and because each
L_{i}
by definition avoids
\partial Y
). For this choice of Floer data,
Proposition

88
applied to
Y
implies that there is a chain level equality of
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
modules
CF^{*}(K^{in},-)=CF^{*}(K\cap Y,-)=CF^{*}(K,-)|_{\mathcal{O}_{Y}}
.

By cofinality of the wrapping
\{K^{i}\}
, there is an isomorphism of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules

\hom_{\mathcal{W}(X)}(K,-)\cong\varinjlim_{i}\hom_{\mathcal{W}(X)}(K^{i},-)\cong\varinjlim_{i}\hom_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(K^{i},-)
(here we define
\lim_{i}\mathcal{M}_{i}
for a sequence of
modules
\mathcal{M}_{0}\to\mathcal{M}_{1}\to\cdots
as in

[
GPS20
, (3.27)]
). On the other hand, when restricted to
the subcategory
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
, there is a further isomorphism

CF^{*}(K,-)|_{\mathcal{O}_{Y}}=\hom_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(K,-)|_{\mathcal{O}_{Y}}=\hom_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(K^{0},-)|_{\mathcal{O}_{Y}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}\varinjlim_{i}\hom_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(K^{i},-)|_{\mathcal{O}_{Y}},

as the isotopies
\partial_{\infty}K^{i}\leadsto\partial_{\infty}K^{i+1}
don’t cross into
Y
by hypothesis, hence don’t cross
\partial_{\infty}L
for any
L\subset Y
, implying that
HF^{*}(K^{i},L)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}HF^{*}(K^{i+1},L)
for each
i
(see, e.g.,
[
GPS20
, Lemma 3.26]
). From the previous paragraph, we know that as
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
-modules,
CF^{*}(K,-)=CF^{*}(K^{in},-)
. So all together we obtain an isomorphism of
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
-modules
i^{*}\hom_{\mathcal{W}(X)}(K,-)|_{\mathcal{O}_{Y}}\cong CF^{*}(K^{in},-)
.
Now, the
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
-module
i^{*}\hom_{\mathcal{W}(Y)}(K,-)|_{\mathcal{O}_{Y}}
is by construction
C_{Y}
-local, i.e., pulled back from the
\mathcal{W}(Y)=\mathcal{O}_{Y}[C_{Y}^{-1}]
module
i^{*}\hom_{\mathcal{W}(Y)}(K,-)
, as is
CF^{*}(K^{in},-)
by
Lemma

98
. Given that the pullback to
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
induces a fully faithful functor from
\mathcal{W}(Y)
modules to
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
modules, we conclude that the
\mathcal{W}(Y)
-modules
i^{*}\hom_{\mathcal{W}(Y)}(K,-)
and
CF^{*}(K^{in},-)
(constructed using
Lemma

98
) are isomorphic as desired.

For the next result, let us return to the setting of an arclike Lagrangian
K
in a Liouville sector
X
.
Assume
X
has been already deformed to have exact boundary as in
Section

A.3
so that there is a boundary sector inclusion of
F\times T^{*}[0,1]
for any extended
\alpha=1
-defining function.
Although
K
itself is not an object of
\mathcal{W}(X)
(because it doesn’t avoid
\partial X
), the module
CF^{*}(K,-)
defined in
Lemma

98
is in fact representable by an object in
\mathcal{W}(X)
:

Proposition 101
.

The module
CF^{*}(K,-)
is representable by the negative pushoff
K^{-}\in\mathcal{W}(X)
of
K
(defined in §
A.3
). That is, the
\mathcal{W}(X)
-modules
CF^{*}(K,-)
and
\hom_{\mathcal{W}(X)}(K^{-},-)
are isomorphic.

Proof A.15
.

Let
X^{-\delta}
denote a sufficiently small inward pushoff of
X
(by definition this is the image of
X
under the flow generated by
-X_{I}
for
I
a fixed defining function for
X
, cf.,
[
GPS24a
, Proof of Lemma 3.4]
). Since the inclusion functor
\mathcal{W}(X^{-\delta})\to\mathcal{W}(X)
is a quasi-equivalence
[
GPS20
, Lemma 3.41]
, it suffices to prove the stated isomorphism of modules after restriction to
\mathcal{W}(X^{-\delta})
.
We can arrange the construction of the negative pushoff
K^{-}
(which is forward stopped by
Lemma

96
) to satisfy the following conditions by performing the relevant modifications in a
F\times T^{*}[0,1]
subsector contained in a sufficiently small neighbourhood of
\partial X
:

•

K^{-}\cap X^{-\delta}
is arclike and equal to
K\cap X^{-\delta}=K\backslash(\gamma^{\prime}\times\partial K)
for a subsegment
\gamma^{\prime}\subset\gamma
;

•

there exists a cofinal wrapping
\{(K^{-})^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
in
X
of
K^{-}
which does not cross into
X^{-\delta}
at infinity and for which
(K^{-})^{t}\cap X^{-\delta}=K\cap X^{-\delta}
.

Lemma

100
, applied to the inclusion
X^{-\delta}\subset X
therefore implies that there is an isomorphism of
\mathcal{W}(X^{-\delta})
-modules between the restriction of the Yoneda module
\hom_{\mathcal{W}(X)}(K^{-},-)|_{\mathcal{W}(X^{-\delta})}
and
CF^{*}(K\cap X^{-\delta},-)
(the latter module constructed by applying
Lemma

98
to
(K\cap X^{-\delta})
in
X^{-\delta}
). On the other hand, since
K
is arclike in
X
and
K\cap X^{-\delta}
is arclike in
X^{-\delta}
,
Lemma

99
implies that there is an isomorphism of
\mathcal{W}(X^{-\delta})
-modules
CF^{*}(K\cap X^{-\delta},-)=CF^{*}(K,-)|_{\mathcal{W}(X^{-\delta})}
.
Putting these together, we obtain an isomorphism of
\mathcal{W}(X^{-\delta})
-modules
\hom_{\mathcal{W}(X)}(K^{-},-)|_{\mathcal{W}(X^{-\delta})}=CF^{*}(K,-)|_{\mathcal{W}(X^{-\delta})}
, which as argued earlier completes the proof.

{rmk}

As constructed,
K^{-}
a priori depends on a choice of
\alpha=1
-extended defining function
I
for which
K
is
I
-arclike. However, given
Proposition

101
and the independence of the module
CF^{*}(K,-)
from choices (see
Lemma

98
and
Section

A.4
), Yoneda implies that
K^{-}
is well-defined up to isomorphism in
\mathcal{W}(X)
.

{rmk}

Suppose
K
is
I
-arclike in
X
but
\partial X
is not exact.
Observe that the deformation
\lambda_{X}\leadsto\lambda_{X}^{\prime}
recalled in
Section

A.3
preserves
I
and (if performed close enough to
\partial X
) the
I
-arclikeness of
K
. Under the resulting deformation invariance quasi-isomorphism
\mathcal{W}(X)\cong\mathcal{W}(X^{\prime})
, we claim that the resulting arclike modules
CF^{*}(K,-;\lambda_{X})
and
CF^{*}(K,-;\lambda_{X}^{\prime})
are isomorphic. In particular, the representability of the latter module by
K^{-}\in\mathcal{W}(X,\lambda_{X}^{\prime})
implies the representability of the former by an isomorphic object. To see the claim, let
X^{-\delta}
again denote a sufficiently small inward pushoff of
X
as in the above Lemma so that
K\cap X^{-\delta}
is
I
-arclike in
X^{-\delta}
and so that the deformation
\lambda_{X}\leadsto\lambda_{X}^{\prime}
is supported in
X\backslash(X^{-\delta})^{\circ}
. We therefore have
X^{-\delta}\hookrightarrow(X,\lambda_{X}^{\prime})
, which by deformation invariance/invariance under trivial inclusions
[
GPS20
, Lemma 3.41]
gives us a zig-zag of quasi-equivalences
\mathcal{W}(X)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\leftarrow}}\mathcal{W}(X^{-\delta})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathcal{W}(X,\lambda_{X}^{\prime})
. Under these quasi-equivalences
Lemma

99
implies that
CF^{*}(K,-;\lambda_{X})
and
CF^{*}(K,-;\lambda_{X}^{\prime})
have isomorphic restrictions to
\mathcal{W}(X^{-\delta})
, namely
CF^{*}(K\cap X^{-\delta},-)
. Therefore the induced quasi-equivalence
\mathcal{W}(X)\simeq\mathcal{W}(X,\lambda_{X}^{\prime})
carries
CF^{*}(K,-;\lambda_{X})
to
CF^{*}(K,-;\lambda_{X}^{\prime})
as desired.

Proof A.16

(Proof of
Theorem

82
)
.

If the boundaries of
Y
and
X
are exact, then combining
Lemma

99
with Proposition
101
applied to both
K
in
X
and
K^{in}=K\cap Y
in
Y
, we see that
i^{*}\hom_{\mathcal{W}(X)}(K^{-},-)
is isomorphic to the Yoneda module over the cylindrized negative pushoff of
K\cap Y
,
\hom_{\mathcal{W}(Y)}((K\cap Y)^{-},-)
.

In general, we may deform
Y
near
\partial Y
and
X
near
\partial X
as in
Section

A.3
(see also
Section

A.4
) without changing the arclike property of
K
and
K\cap Y
, thereby reducing to the above case.

Now we return to the setting that
(B,F_{0})
is a sutured Liouville domain,
S
the associated Liouville sector (deformed as in
Section

A.3
),
I:\Nbd^{Z}(\partial S)\to\R
an extended
1
-defining function, and
\cup:\mathcal{W}(F)\to\mathcal{W}(S)
the corresponding cup functor defined above the statement of
Theorem

84
.
Let
L\subset S
be an
I
-arclike Lagrangian. For what follows, we think of the boundary
\partial L\subset\partial S
as a compact exact Lagrangian in
F
, either via restricting to the level set of
I
containing
\partial L
or by projection along the characteristic foliation.

Proposition 102
.

In the situation described above, the pullback
\cup^{*}\mathcal{Y}_{L^{-}}
is representable by
\partial L
; that is, there is an isomorphism of
\mathcal{W}(F)
-modules
\cup^{*}\mathcal{Y}_{L^{-}}\simeq\mathcal{Y}_{\partial L}.

Proof A.17
.

The subsector
F\times T^{*}[0,1]\subset S
determined by
I
has the property that the restriction of
I
and its associated
R
-function
R
are simply the projection to the factors of
T^{*}[0,1]=[0,1]_{R}\times\R_{I}
. It follows (see
Lemma

95
and the discussion that follows) that
by shrinking the subsector
F\times T^{*}[0,1]\subset S
(or by flowing
L
), we may assume that
L\cap(F\times T^{*}[0,1])=\partial L\times\gamma
where
\gamma:[0,1]\hookrightarrow T^{*}[0,1]
is of the form
\{I=c\}
. By
Lemma

97
, we learn that
(L\cap(F\times T^{*}[0,1]))^{-}=(\partial L\times\gamma)^{-}
is isomorphic to the stabilization
stab(\partial L)
of
\partial L
.
Let
i:F\times T^{*}[0,1]\subset S
denote the embedding defined above the statement of
Theorem

84
. Theorem
82
implies that
i^{*}\mathcal{Y}_{L^{-}}\simeq\mathcal{Y}_{(L\cap(F\times T^{*}[0,1])^{-}}
which we have just argued is isomorphic to
\mathcal{Y}_{stab(\partial L)}
. Now pull back by
stab
to conclude, as
stab^{*}i^{*}=\cup^{*}
and
stab^{*}\mathcal{Y}_{stab(\partial L)}
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{Y}_{\partial L}
by full-faithfulness of
stab
(which is a special case of
[
GPS24a
, Theorem 1.5]
).

Proof A.18

(Proof of
Theorem

84
)
.

Apply Corollary
87
to
Proposition

102
. To arrange that
\cap
has image contained in the compact Fukaya category, we apply
Section

A.1
.

Appendix B
Liouville structures on submanifolds of
(\C^{*})^{n}

In this section, we will consider a (real) submanifold
A
of the complex manifold

M^{*}_{\C^{*}}\cong M^{*}_{\R}\times M^{*}_{S^{1}}

for some lattice
M^{*}
.

Let
\varphi:M^{*}_{\R}\to\R
be a strictly convex, proper, bounded-below function.
We will denote its pullback to
M^{*}_{\C^{*}}
by
\varphi
also.
Being strictly convex,
\varphi
is the potential for a Kähler form
\omega=d\lambda
, with Liouville one-form
\lambda=-d^{c}\varphi
.

The main results of this appendix give various conditions under which the Liouville structures for different classes of Kähler potentials can be compared. A version of the comparison of Liouville structures for homogeneous potentials of weight 2 has also recently been obtained in
[
SB24
, Theorem 9.2]
.

Lemma 103

(cf. Section 4b of
[
Sei06
]
)
.

Suppose that
A
is a complex submanifold which admits a smooth toric compactification (Definition
118
), and
\varphi
is a convex combination of a compactification Kähler potential (Definition
120
) and a potential which is homogeneous of weight
2
(Definition
111
).
Then
A\cap\{\varphi\leq C\}
, equipped with the Liouville one-form
\lambda|_{A}
, is a Liouville domain for sufficiently large
C
; as a consequence, the Liouville completion is independent of
C
for
C
sufficiently large.

Proof B.1
.

Combines Lemmas
113
and
119
, and Corollary
123
below.

Lemma 104
.

Suppose that
A
is a complex submanifold admitting a smooth toric compactification,
\varphi_{0}
is a compactification Kähler potential, and
\varphi_{1}
a potential which is homogeneous of weight
2
.
Then the corresponding Liouville completions
\widehat{(A,\lambda_{0})}
and
\widehat{(A,\lambda_{1})}
are Liouville isomorphic.

Proof B.2
.

Consider the family of potentials
\{\varphi_{s}\}_{s\in[0,1]}
linearly interpolating between
\varphi_{0}
and
\varphi_{1}
.

It follows from Lemmas
113
and
119
, and Corollary
123
, that
A\cap\{\varphi_{s}\leq C\}
is a Liouville subdomain for all
s
, for
C
sufficiently large.
Thus we have a homotopy of Liouville domains associated to these potentials, which in turn induces an isomorphism of Liouville manifolds
\widehat{(A,\lambda_{0})}\cong\widehat{(A,\lambda_{1})}
by
[
CE12
, Proposition 11.8]
.

Lemma 105
.

Suppose that
A
admits a smooth toric compactification, and is
\delta
-approximately holomorphic (Definition
114
) for some
0<\delta<\pi/6
; and
\varphi
is homogeneous of weight
2
.
Then
A\cap\{\varphi\leq C\}
, equipped with the Liouville one-form
\lambda|_{A}
, is a Liouville domain for sufficiently large
C
; as a consequence, the Liouville completion is independent of
C
.

Proof B.3
.

Follows from Lemmas
117
and
119
, together with
[
CE12
, Proposition 11.8]
.

Lemma 106
.

Suppose that
(A_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
is a family of submanifolds which admits a family of smooth toric compactifications, and is
\delta
-approximately holomorphic for some
0<\delta<\pi/6
; and
\varphi
is homogeneous of weight
2
.
Then the Liouville completions
\widehat{(A_{0},\lambda_{0})}
and
\widehat{(A_{1},\lambda_{1})}
are Liouville isomorphic.

Proof B.4
.

The proof follows the pattern of that of
Lemma

104
, again using
[
CE12
, Proposition 11.8]
. The key step is to show that the sublevel sets
A_{t}\cap\{\varphi\leq C\}
are all Liouville subdomains, for
C
sufficiently large. This follows from the family versions of Lemmas
117
and
119
.

B.1
Angles

We recall some material from
[
Don96
]
and
[
CM07
, Section 8]
.

Given a finite-dimensional real inner product space
(V,\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle)
, the angle between two non-zero vectors
x,y\in V
is defined to be

\angle(x,y):=\cos^{-1}\left(\frac{\langle x,y\rangle}{|x||y|}\right)\in[0,\pi].

The angle between a linear subspace
X\subset V
and a vector
y\in V
is

\angle(X,y):=\inf_{0\neq x\in X}\angle(x,y)\in[0,\pi/2].

We remark that
\angle(X,y)=\angle(\pi^{\perp}y,y)
, where
\pi^{\perp}
denotes orthogonal projection to
X
.
12
12

12

Proof: if
\angle(x,y)=\theta
, we may rescale
x
so that
|x|=|y|\cos\theta
, then
|x-y|=|y|\sin\theta
. Thus the minimum of
\angle(x,y)
is achieved when
|x-y|
is minimal, which is precisely for
x=\pi^{\perp}(y)
.

The angle between linear subspaces
X,Y\subset V
is

\angle(X,Y):=\sup_{0\neq y\in Y}\inf_{0\neq x\in X}\angle(x,y)\in[0,\pi/2].

Lemma 107
.

Suppose that we have two inner products satisfying

c|x|^{2}_{1}\leq|x|^{2}_{2}\leq C|x|^{2}_{1}

for all
x\in V
, for some
c,C>0
.
Then

\cos\angle_{2}(x,y)\geq 1-\frac{C}{c}\left(1-\cos\angle_{1}(x,y)\right).

In particular,
\angle_{1}(x_{j},y_{j})\to 0
if and only if
\angle_{2}(x_{j},y_{j})\to 0
.

Proof B.5
.

We may rescale
x
and
y
so that
|x|_{1}=|y|_{1}
, then

\cos\angle_{1}(x,y)=1-\frac{|x-y|^{2}_{1}}{2|x|_{1}|y|_{1}}.

Then we have

\displaystyle\cos\angle_{2}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\frac{|x|_{2}^{2}+|y|_{2}^{2}-|x-y|_{2}^{2}}{2|x|_{2}|y|_{2}}

\displaystyle\geq 1-\frac{C|x-y|_{1}^{2}}{2c|x|_{1}|y|_{1}}

\displaystyle=1-\frac{C}{c}\left(1-\cos\angle_{1}(x,y)\right)

as required.

Now suppose that
\omega
is a linear symplectic structure on
V
,
J
a compatible complex structure, and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle=\omega(\cdot,J(\cdot))
the corresponding inner product.
The
angle
of a real subspace
X\subset V
is
\angle(V):=\angle(JV,V)
.
Note that
\angle(V)=0
if and only if
V
is
J
-holomorphic.

This notion is closely related to the ‘Kähler angle’
\theta(X)
associated to an oriented real linear subspace, which was introduced in
[
Don96
]
: in particular, in the case that
X
is of codimension
2
,
\angle(X)=\min\{\theta(X),\pi-\theta(X)\}
by
[
CM07
, Lemma 8.3 (d)]
.

Lemma 108
.

If
x\in X
and
y\in X^{\omega}
(the symplectic orthogonal complement), then

|\langle x,y\rangle|\leq|x||y|\sqrt{2-2\cos\angle(X)}.

Proof B.6
.

There exists
\tilde{x}\in X
with
\angle(Jx,\tilde{x})\leq\angle(X)
, and we may rescale it so that
|\tilde{x}|=|x|
.
It follows easily that
|Jx-\tilde{x}|\leq|x|\sqrt{2-2\cos\angle(X)}
.
Thus

|\langle x,y\rangle|=|\omega(y,Jx)|=|\omega(y,Jx-\tilde{x})|\leq|y||Jx-\tilde{x}|\leq|y||x|\sqrt{2-2\cos\angle(X)}

as claimed.

Corollary 109
.

If
\angle(X)<\pi/2
, then
X
is symplectic.

Proof B.7
.

By Lemma
108
,
X\cap X^{\omega}=\{0\}
.

Lemma 110
.

Suppose that
\angle(X)<\pi/6
, and let
\pi^{\omega}:V\to X
denote the symplectic orthogonal projection. Then for any
v\in V
we have

|\pi^{\omega}(v)|\leq\frac{|v|}{\sqrt{2\cos\angle(X)-1}}.

Proof B.8
.

Let
x\in X
and
y\in X^{\omega}
, so that
\pi^{\omega}(x+y)=x
.
We have

\displaystyle|x+y|^{2}

\displaystyle=|x|^{2}+|y|^{2}+2\langle x,y\rangle

\displaystyle\geq|x|^{2}+|y|^{2}-|x||y|\sqrt{2-2\cos\angle(X)}\quad\text{by Lemma \ref{lem:pair_orthog}}

\displaystyle=|x|^{2}+\left(|y|-|x|\sqrt{2-2\cos\angle(X)}\right)^{2}-|x|^{2}(2-2\cos\angle(X))

\displaystyle\geq(2\cos\angle(X)-1)|x|^{2}

from which the result follows.

B.2
Conditions on
A
and
\varphi

A choice of basis for
M^{*}
determines coordinates
x_{j}
on
M^{*}_{\R}
,
\theta_{j}
on
M^{*}_{S^{1}}
, and holomorphic coordinates
z_{j}=\exp(x_{j}+i\theta_{j})
on
M^{*}_{\C^{*}}
.

Let
E\in T(M^{*}_{\R})
be the Euler vector field (equal to
\sum_{j}x_{j}\partial_{x_{j}}
in coordinates).
We shall also use
E
for the corresponding vector field in
T(M^{*}_{\C^{*}})
.
If
\varphi:M^{*}_{\R}\to\R
is strictly convex, let
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\varphi}
denote the Kähler metric associated to the Kähler form with potential
\varphi
: it is the product of the Riemannian metrics on
M^{*}_{\R}
and
M^{*}_{S^{1}}
given by the Hessian of
\varphi
.

Definition 111
.

We say that
\varphi:M^{*}_{\R}\to\R
is
k
-homogeneous (near
\infty
)
if
E(\varphi)=k\cdot\varphi
(outside a compact set).

We will call the Riemannian metric associated to a
2
-homogeneous strictly convex function, a
2
-homogeneous metric.
For example, a Euclidean metric on
M^{*}_{\R}
induces a
2
-homogeneous metric on
M^{*}_{\C^{*}}
(we take
\varphi(x)=|x|^{2}
).
A straightforward compactness argument shows that any two
2
-homogeneous metrics are uniformly equivalent:

c|v|_{\varphi_{1}}^{2}\leq|v|_{\varphi_{2}}^{2}\leq C|v|_{\varphi_{1}}^{2}

for any tangent vector
v
to
M^{*}_{\C^{*}}
, for some constants
c,C>0
depending on the
\varphi_{i}
.

Definition 112
.

We say that the submanifold
A\subset M^{*}_{\C^{*}}
is
asymptotically cylindrical
if

\lim_{a\to\infty}\angle(T_{a}A,E_{a})=0,

where the angle is taken with respect to any
2
-homogeneous metric.
More precisely, for every
\delta>0
, there exists a subset
U_{\delta}\subset A
with compact complement, such that for all
a\in U_{\delta}
,
\angle(T_{a}A,E_{a})<\delta
.
(Note that the choice of
2
-homogeneous metric doesn’t matter, by Lemma
107
, and our observation that any two such metrics are uniformly equivalent.)

More generally, we say that a family of submanifolds
(A_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
is
uniformly asymptotically cylindrical
if for every
\delta>0
, there exists
U_{\delta}\subset M^{*}_{\C^{*}}
with compact complement, such that for all
t
and for all
a\in A_{t}\cap U_{\delta}
,
\angle(T_{a}A_{t},E_{a})<\delta
.

B.3
A criterion for holomorphic submanifolds

The Liouville vector field
Z
corresponding to the Liouville one-form
\lambda
is equal to
\nabla\varphi
, where the gradient is taken with respect to the Kähler metric induced by
\varphi
.
We will always assume that
\omega_{A}=\omega|_{A}
is a symplectic form.
It has Liouville one-form
\lambda_{A}=\lambda|_{A}
, and Liouville vector field
Z_{A}
.
It is immediate from the definitions that

Z_{A}=\pi^{\omega}(\nabla\varphi),

where
\pi^{\omega}:T(M^{*}_{\C^{*}})\to TA
is the
\omega
-orthogonal projection.

Suppose that
A
is a holomorphic submanifold, i.e.,
\angle(TA)=0
.
Then the
\omega
-orthogonal projection
T(M^{*}_{\C^{*}})\to TA
coincides with the orthogonal projection.

Lemma 113
.

Suppose that
A
is an asymptotically cylindrical holomorphic submanifold, and
\varphi_{s}:M^{*}_{\R}\to\R
is a smooth family of potentials parametrized by
s\in[0,1]
such that outside of some compact set,
\angle(E,\nabla\varphi_{s})\leq c
for all
s
, for some
c<\pi/2
independent of
s
, where the angle and the gradient
\nabla
are taken with respect to some fixed
2
-homogeneous metric.
Then for sufficiently large
C
, the sublevel sets
A\cap\{\varphi_{s}\leq C\}
are Liouville domains, for all
s
.

Proof B.9
.

We must show that
Z_{A,s}(\varphi_{s})>0
along
\{\varphi_{s}=C\}
.
Using the fact that
A
is holomorphic, we have that
Z_{A,s}(\varphi_{s})=|Z_{A,s}|^{2}
, so it suffices to prove that
Z_{A,s}\neq 0
.
This is equivalent to
d\varphi_{s}|_{A}\neq 0
, which is equivalent to
\angle(T_{a}A,\nabla\varphi_{s})\neq\pi/2
.
We have
\angle(E,\nabla\varphi_{s})\leq c<\pi/2
outside a compact set, and
\angle(T_{a}A,E_{a})\to 0
as
a\to\infty
.
Hence, for
a
outside some compact set
K
, we have
\angle(T_{a}A,\nabla\varphi_{s})\leq c^{\prime}<\pi/2
for all
s
.
Now
\varphi(K\times[0,1])
is a compact subset of
\R
, so it suffices to take
C
larger than its supremum.

{rmk}

The proof of
Lemma

113
works for any continuous family of potentials parametrized by a compact topological space, but we only need the case of a point and an interval.

B.4
A criterion for
2
-homogeneous potentials

Let
\varphi:M^{*}_{\R}\to\R
be strictly convex, proper, bounded below, and
2
-homogeneous near
\infty
.
We will use the associated
2
-homogeneous metric on
M^{*}_{\C^{*}}
below.

Definition 114
.

Let
0\leq\delta<\pi/2
. We say that
A
is
\delta
-approximately holomorphic
if
\angle(TA)<\delta
. (Note that
\angle(TA)=\angle_{\varphi}(TA)
depends on
\varphi
.)

Note that if
A
is
\delta
-approximately holomorphic, then
\omega_{A}:=\omega|_{A}
is symplectic by Corollary
109
, with Liouville one-form
\lambda_{A}=-d^{c}\varphi|_{A}
.

Lemma 115

(Proposition 2.7 of
[
Zho20
]
)
.

We have
\angle(Z,E)=0
in the locus where
\varphi
is
2
-homogeneous (in particular, near
\infty
).

Lemma 116
.

We have

1.

\lim_{a\to\infty}\varphi(a)/|E_{a}|=\infty
, and

2.

\lim_{a\to\infty}|\nabla\varphi|=\infty

(where ‘
\lim_{a\to\infty}
’ is to be interpreted as in Definition
112
).

Proof B.10
.

(
1
) follows from the fact that along any ray,
\varphi
is strictly convex and eventually quadratic, while
|E|
is eventually linear.

For (
2
), note that as
\varphi
is
2
-homogeneous near
\infty
, we have

\displaystyle E(\varphi)

\displaystyle=2\varphi

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\langle\nabla\varphi,E\rangle

\displaystyle=2\varphi

\displaystyle\Rightarrow|\nabla\varphi||E|

\displaystyle=2\varphi\quad\text{by Lemma \ref{lem:ZpropE}}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow|\nabla\varphi|

\displaystyle=\frac{2\varphi}{|E|}.

The result now follows from (
1
).

Lemma 117
.

Suppose that
\varphi
is homogeneous of weight
2
near
\infty
, and
(A_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
is a family of submanifolds which are uniformly asymptotically cylindrical and
\delta
-approximately holomorphic for some
0<\delta<\pi/6
.
Then
Z_{A_{t}}(\varphi)>0
outside a compact subset of
M^{*}_{\C^{*}}
.

Proof B.11
.

Note that as
A_{t}
is uniformly asymptotically cylindrical, Lemma
115
implies that

\lim_{a\to\infty}\angle(\pi^{\perp}_{t}\nabla\varphi,\nabla\varphi)=\lim_{a\to\infty}\angle(TA_{t},\nabla\varphi)=0

uniformly in
t
, from which it follows that

\displaystyle\lim_{a\to\infty}\frac{|\nabla\varphi-\pi^{\perp}_{t}\nabla\varphi|}{|\nabla\varphi|}

\displaystyle=\lim_{a\to\infty}\sin\angle(\nabla\varphi,\pi^{\perp}_{t}\nabla\varphi)=0,\qquad\text{and}

(B.1)

\displaystyle\lim_{a\to\infty}\frac{|\pi^{\perp}_{t}\nabla\varphi|}{|\nabla\varphi|}

\displaystyle=\lim_{a\to\infty}\cos\angle(\nabla\varphi,\pi^{\perp}_{t}\nabla\varphi)=1

(B.2)

uniformly in
t
.

By Lemma
110
, we have

|\pi^{\omega}_{t}(\nabla\varphi-\pi^{\perp}_{t}\nabla\varphi)|\leq\frac{|\nabla\varphi-\pi^{\perp}_{t}\nabla\varphi|}{\sqrt{2\cos\delta-1}}.

Combining with (
B.1
), it follows that

\lim_{a\to\infty}\frac{|\pi^{\omega}_{t}(\nabla\varphi-\pi^{\perp}_{t}\nabla\varphi)|}{|\nabla\varphi|}=0

(B.3)

uniformly in
t
.

We now have

\displaystyle\frac{Z_{A}(\varphi)}{|\nabla\varphi|^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\langle\pi^{\omega}_{t}(\nabla\varphi),\nabla\varphi\rangle}{|\nabla\varphi|^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\langle\pi^{\omega}_{t}(\nabla\varphi-\pi^{\perp}_{t}\nabla\varphi)+\pi^{\perp}_{t}\nabla\varphi,\nabla\varphi\rangle}{|\nabla\varphi|^{2}}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{|\pi^{\perp}_{t}\nabla\varphi|^{2}}{|\nabla\varphi|^{2}}-\frac{|\pi^{\omega}_{t}(\nabla\varphi-\pi^{\perp}_{t}\nabla\varphi)|}{|\nabla\varphi|}.

Applying (
B.2
) and (
B.3
), we see that the RHS
\to 1
uniformly in
t
, as
a\to\infty
.
The result now follows from Lemma
116
(
2
).

B.5
Submanifolds admitting a toric compactification are asymptotically cylindrical

Let
\Sigma
be a simplicial fan in
M^{*}_{\R}
, giving rise to a toric orbifold
Y_{\Sigma}
.
The complement of the toric boundary divisor in
Y_{\Sigma}
is the complex manifold
M^{*}_{\C^{*}}
.

Definition 118
.

A
smooth toric compactification
of a submanifold
A\subset M^{*}_{\C^{*}}
is a smooth submanifold
X\subset Y_{\Sigma}
, for some simplicial fan
\Sigma
, such that
A=X\cap M^{*}_{\C^{*}}
,
X
avoids the orbifold points, and the intersection of
X
with every stratum of the toric boundary divisor is transverse.

Lemma 119
.

If
(A_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
is a family of submanifolds which admits a family
(X_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
of smooth toric compactifications, then it is uniformly asymptotically cylindrical.

Proof B.12
.

Suppose to the contrary that there exists a sequence of points
a_{j}\in A_{t_{j}}
converging to
\infty
, with
\angle(T_{a_{j}}A_{t_{j}},E)>\delta>0
.
Taking a subsequence, we may assume that the
(a_{j},t_{j})
converge to a point
(p,s)
with
p
in the toric boundary and
s\in[0,1]
.

Suppose that
p
lies in the torus orbit
Y_{\sigma}
corresponding to the cone
\sigma
of
\Sigma
.
As
Y
is smooth along
Y_{\sigma}
by hypothesis, we may choose a basis
(m_{j})_{j=1}^{n}
of
M^{*}_{\C^{*}}
such that
m_{1},\ldots,m_{k}
span
\sigma
.
The choice of basis determines coordinates
x_{j}
on
M^{*}_{\R}
,
\theta_{j}
on
M^{*}_{S^{1}}
, and holomorphic coordinates
z_{j}=\exp(x_{j}+i\theta_{j})
on
M^{*}_{\C^{*}}
.
These extend to coordinates
(z_{j})_{j=1}^{n}\in\C^{k}\times(\C^{*})^{n-k}
on a Zariski neighbourhood of
Y_{\sigma}
, with
Y_{\sigma}=\{z_{1}=\ldots=z_{k}=0\}
.

In a neighbourhood of
p
in
Y
we may choose a real coordinate system
(u_{1},v_{1},\ldots,u_{k},v_{k},w_{2k+1},\ldots,w_{2n})
such that:

•

p=(0,\ldots,0)
in these coordinates;

•

z_{j}=u_{j}+iv_{j}
for
j=1,\ldots,k
;

•

(w_{2k+1},\ldots,w_{2n})
are related to
(x_{k+1},\theta_{k+1},\ldots,x_{n},\theta_{n})
by an affine-linear change of variables;

•

(u_{1},v_{1},\ldots,u_{k},v_{k},w_{2k+1},\ldots,w_{\dim X})
define a coordinate system on a neighbourhood of
p\in X_{s}
.

In these coordinates,
X_{t}
can then be written as the graph of a smooth function

F_{t}:U\to\R^{2n-\dim X}

for some
U\subset\R^{\dim X}
an open neighbourhood of the origin, and all
t
sufficiently close to
s
.

Now consider the Euler vector field
v=\sum_{j=1}^{k}u_{j}\partial/\partial u_{j}+v_{j}\partial/\partial v_{j}
on
U
.
It admits the following lift to
A_{t}
:

\tilde{v}_{t}=v+\sum_{j=\dim X+1}^{2n}v(F_{t}^{j})\frac{\partial}{\partial w_{j}}.

Thus we have

E-\tilde{v}_{t}=\sum_{j>k}x_{j}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}-\sum_{j=\dim X+1}^{2n}v(F_{t}^{j})\frac{\partial}{\partial w_{j}}.

We claim that
|E-\tilde{v}_{t_{j}}|
is bounded, as
(a_{j},t_{j})\to(p,s)
.
Indeed, this follows from the fact that all of the following quantities are bounded:
|\partial/\partial x_{j}|
,
|\partial/\partial\theta_{j}|
(these are bounded for any
2
-homogeneous metric, by compactness);
|\partial/\partial w_{j}|
(as these are a linear combination of
\partial/\partial x_{j}
and
\partial/\partial\theta_{j}
);
x_{j}
for
j>k
(as
z_{j}(p)\neq 0
);
v(F_{t}^{j})
(this follows as
v
is smooth and vanishes at
0
, so
v(F_{t}^{j})
is smooth and vanishes at
0
).

On the other hand,
|E|
is unbounded (as it is eventually linear with positive slope along any ray).
It follows that
\angle(E,\tilde{v}_{t})\to 0
as
a\to\infty
; as
\tilde{v}_{t}\in TA_{t}
by construction, the claim follows.

B.6
Compactification Kähler potentials

Definition 120
.

Let
\Sigma
be a simplicial fan in
M^{*}_{\R}
, determining a toric orbifold
Y_{\Sigma}
with toric boundary divisor
D
.
We say that a strictly convex, proper, bounded below function
\varphi:M^{*}_{\R}\to\R
is a
compactification Kähler potential (for
\Sigma
)
if the corresponding Kähler form extends to an orbifold Kähler form on
Y_{\Sigma}
, so that
(\omega,\varphi)
define a relative Kähler form on
(Y_{\Sigma},D)
in the sense of
[
She20a
, Definition 3.2]
, and
d\varphi
extends to a moment map
d\varphi:Y_{\Sigma}\to M_{\R}
for the action of
M^{*}_{S^{1}}
on
Y_{\Sigma}
.

Lemma 121
.

Let
\sum_{i\in\Sigma(1)}\kappa_{i}D_{i}
be an ample
\R
-divisor on
Y_{\Sigma}
, with all
\kappa_{i}>0
.
Then there exists a compactification Kähler potential
\varphi
for
\Sigma
with linking numbers
\kappa_{i}
.
The image of the moment map
d\varphi
is the moment polytope

\Delta_{\kappa}=\{m\in M_{\R}:v_{i}(m)\geq-\kappa_{i}\text{ for all $i\in\Sigma(1)$}\}\subset M_{\R}.

Proof B.13
.

The claim follows from Guillemin’s formula for toric Kähler potentials
[
Gui94
]
extended to orbifolds in
[
CDG01
]
.

Lemma 122
.

Suppose that
\varphi
is a compactification Kähler potential. Then
\angle(E,\nabla\varphi)\leq c
near
\infty
for some
c<\pi/2
, where the angle and gradient are taken with respect to some
2
-homogeneous metric.

Proof B.14
.

It suffices to show that
E(\varphi)/(|d\varphi||E|)
is bounded below near
\infty
.
Note that
|d\varphi|
is bounded above (as it lies in
\Delta
which is compact), so it suffices to bound
E(\varphi)/|E|
below.

Suppose, to the contrary, that there exists a sequence
p_{i}\in M^{*}_{\R}
with
E(\varphi)/|E|\to d
for some
d\leq 0
.
We consider the
p_{i}
as lying inside
M^{*}_{\R}\times\{1\}\subset M^{*}_{\R}\times M^{*}_{S^{1}}\subset Y_{\Sigma}
.
Taking a subsequence, we may assume that the
p_{i}
converge to a point
y
lying in some orbit
Y_{\sigma}
.
Then
d\varphi_{p_{i}}
converges to
d\varphi_{y}
, which lies on a facet of
\Delta_{\kappa}
contained in the affine space where
\langle m,v_{j}\rangle=-\kappa_{j}
for all rays
v_{j}
of
\sigma
.

We choose a basis
(m_{j})_{j=1}^{n}
for
M^{*}_{\R}
such that
m_{j}=v_{j}
for
j=1,\ldots,k
, where
v_{j}
are the primitive vectors spanning
\sigma
(this need not come from a basis for
M^{*}
).
The basis induces coordinates
x_{j}
on
M^{*}_{\R}
, with
x_{j}(p_{i})\to-\infty
for
j\leq k
and
x_{j}(p_{i})
bounded for
j>k
.
Thus we have, as
i\to\infty
:

\displaystyle\frac{E(\varphi)}{|E|}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{n}x_{j}\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x_{j}}}{|E|}

\displaystyle\to\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{n}x_{j}d\varphi_{y}(m_{j})}{|E|}

\displaystyle\to\frac{-\sum_{j=1}^{k}x_{j}\kappa_{j}}{|\sum_{j=1}^{k}x_{j}\partial_{x_{j}}|}.

(B.4)

In the last line, we have used the fact that
|E|\to\infty
, while
x_{j}
is bounded for
j>k
, as is
d\varphi_{y}(m_{j})
for any
j
.

Now observe that as
x_{j}\to-\infty
for
j\leq k
, we may truncate our sequence so that
x_{j}<0
for all
j\leq k
.
Then we have

\displaystyle-\sum_{j=1}^{k}x_{j}\kappa_{j}

\displaystyle\geq\max_{j=1,\ldots,k}(-x_{j})\cdot\min_{j=1,\ldots,k}\kappa_{j}

\displaystyle\geq\min_{j=1,\ldots,k}\kappa_{j}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{k}}\sqrt{\sum_{j=1}^{k}x_{j}^{2}}

\displaystyle\geq\min_{j=1,\ldots,k}\kappa_{j}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{k}}\cdot c\left|\sum_{j=1}^{k}x_{j}\partial_{x_{j}}\right|.

The last line uses the fact that
\sqrt{\sum_{j}x_{j}^{2}}=|\sum_{j}x_{j}\partial_{x_{j}}|^{\prime}
with respect to the Euclidean metric
|\cdot|^{\prime}
, which is uniformly equivalent to
|\cdot|
.
This gives the desired lower bound for (
B.4
).

Corollary 123
.

Suppose that
\varphi_{0}
is a compactification Kähler potential,
\varphi_{1}
a homogeneous potential of weight
2
, and
\varphi_{s}=(1-s)\varphi_{0}+s\varphi_{1}
.
Then
\angle(E,\nabla\varphi_{s})\leq c<\pi/2
, for some
c
independent of
s
, where the angle and gradient are taken with respect to some fixed
2
-homogeneous metric.

Proof B.15
.

Follows immediately from Lemmas
122
and
115
.

Appendix C
Quasi-isomorphisms which respect gradings

The goal of this appendix is to show that the quasi-isomorphism
\mathcal{A}_{0}\simeq\mathcal{B}_{0}
can be made to respect the
\Z\oplus M^{*}
-gradings on both categories.

C.1
A_{\infty}
terminology

We follow the conventions concerning
A_{\infty}
categories from
[
She20
]
. Let
\Bbbk
be a field. Recall that a pre-
A_{\infty}
category
\mathcal{C}
consists of a collection of objects, and a graded
\Bbbk
-vector space
hom^{*}(X_{0},X_{1})
for each pair of objects
X_{0},X_{1}.
Set

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}(X_{0},X_{1},\cdots,X_{s}):=hom^{*}(X_{0},X_{1})[1]\otimes\cdots\otimes hom^{*}(X_{s-1},X_{s})[1].

Define the Hochschild cochains (of length
s
) to be given by:

\displaystyle CC^{*}(\mathcal{C})^{s}:=\prod_{X_{0},X_{1},\cdots,X_{s}}Hom^{*}(\mathcal{C}(X_{0},X_{1},\cdots,X_{s}),\mathcal{C}(X_{0},X_{s}))[-1]

We then set

\displaystyle CC^{*}(\mathcal{C}):=\prod_{s\geq 0}CC^{*}(\mathcal{C})^{s}

Given
a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{s}\in\mathcal{C}(X_{0},X_{1},\cdots,X_{s})
, it is convenient to introduce the notation

\displaystyle\epsilon_{j}(a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{s})=|a_{1}|+\cdots+|a_{j}|-j\in\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}

(C.1)

Given two Hochschild cochains
\phi,\psi
, their Gerstenhaber product is defined by

\displaystyle\phi\circ\psi(a_{1},\cdots,a_{s}):=\sum_{j,k}(-1)^{(|\psi|-1)\cdot\epsilon_{j}}\phi^{*}(a_{1},\cdots,\psi^{*}(a_{j+1},\cdots),a_{k+1},\cdots),

(C.2)

and their Gerstenhaber bracket is given by

\displaystyle[\phi,\psi]:=\phi\circ\psi-(-1)^{(|\phi|-1)(|\psi|-1)}\psi\circ\phi.

This bracket operation turns
CC^{*}(\mathcal{C})
into a graded Lie algebra.

Definition 124
.

An
A_{\infty}
category is a pre-
A_{\infty}
category
\mathcal{C}
equipped with an
A_{\infty}
-structure, that is an element
\mu\in CC^{2}(\mathcal{C})
with
\mu\circ\mu=0
and
\mu^{0}=0
.

Given an
A_{\infty}
category
(\mathcal{C},\mu)
, we define the Hochschild cohomology differential to be given by

\displaystyle d_{\mu}:CC^{*}(\mathcal{C})\to CC^{*+1}(\mathcal{C})

(C.3)

\displaystyle d_{\mu}(-):=[\mu,-].

The Hochschild cohomology is by definition
HH^{*}(\mathcal{C},\mu):=H^{*}((CC^{*}(\mathcal{C}),[\mu,-])).

{rmk}

Suppose that
\mathcal{C}
carries a grading by some free abelian group
G
and that the
A_{\infty}
structure
\mu
respects this grading. We say that a Hochschild cochain
\phi
has
G
-grading
g\in G
if whenever
a_{1},\cdots,a_{s}
are homogeneous elements,

\displaystyle\operatorname{deg}_{G}(\phi(a_{1},\cdots,a_{s}))=\operatorname{deg}_{G}(a_{1})+\cdots+\operatorname{deg}_{G}(a_{s})+g.

(C.4)

It is elementary to see that Hochschild cochains of cohomological
G
-degree
g
define a subcomplex

\displaystyle CC^{*}_{g}(\mathcal{C},\mu)\subset CC^{*}(\mathcal{C},\mu).

(C.5)

We let
HH^{*}_{g}(\mathcal{C},\mu):=H^{*}(CC^{*}_{g}(\mathcal{C},\mu))
. Furthermore, if
\mathcal{C}
has finite dimensional hom-spaces, we have a decomposition:

\displaystyle CC^{*}(\mathcal{C},\mu)\cong\prod_{g\in G}CC^{*}_{g}(\mathcal{C},\mu).

(C.6)

Definition 125
.

Let
\mathcal{C},\tilde{\mathcal{C}}
be two pre-
A_{\infty}
categories. A pre-functor
F:\mathcal{C}\to\tilde{\mathcal{C}}
is an assignment on the level of objects together with a collection of linear maps

\displaystyle F^{s}:\mathcal{C}(X_{0},\cdots,X_{s})\to\tilde{\mathcal{C}}(F(X_{0}),F(X_{s}))

(C.7)

for every sequence of objects
X_{0},\cdots,X_{s}\in\operatorname{Ob}(\mathcal{C})
(with
s\geq 1
).

A strict formal diffeomorphism from
\mathcal{C}
to itself is a pre-functor
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}
such that the assignment on objects is the identity and
F^{1}=\operatorname{id}
.
13
13

13

This is a slightly more restrictive notion of formal diffeomorphism than the one introduced in
[
Sei08
, §(1c)]
.

Definition 126
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},\mu)
,
(\tilde{\mathcal{C}},\tilde{\mu})
be two
A_{\infty}
-categories. An
A_{\infty}
functor
F:(\mathcal{C},\mu)\to(\tilde{\mathcal{C}},\tilde{\mu})
is a pre-functor between the two pre-
A_{\infty}
categories which satisfies:

\displaystyle\sum{\tilde{\mu}}^{*}(F^{*}(a_{1},\cdots),F^{*}(a_{j_{1}+1}\cdots),F^{*}(a_{j_{k}+1},\cdots,a_{s}))=\sum(-1)^{\epsilon_{j}}F^{*}(a_{1},\cdots,\mu^{*}(a_{j+1},\cdots),\cdots,a_{s}).

(C.8)

Let
(\mathcal{C},\mu)
,
(\mathcal{C},\tilde{\mu})
be two
A_{\infty}
-structures on a pre-
A_{\infty}
category
\mathcal{C}
. An
A_{\infty}
functor
F:(\mathcal{C},\mu)\to(\mathcal{C},\tilde{\mu})
will be called a strict isomorphism if it arises from a strict formal diffeomorphism. Conversely, given a strict formal diffeomorphism
F
and an
A_{\infty}
structure
(\mathcal{C},\mu)
, there is a unique
A_{\infty}
structure

\displaystyle(\mathcal{C},F_{*}(\mu))

(C.9)

such that
F
defines a strict isomorphism
F:(\mathcal{C},\mu)\to(\mathcal{C},F_{*}(\mu))
(
[
Sei08
, §(1c)]
).

C.2
Obstructions in Hochschild cohomology

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a
\Bbbk
-linear graded category. To match the conventions from
[
She20
]
, we view the compositions in
\mathcal{D}
as being given by operations

\displaystyle Hom_{\mathcal{D}}^{*}(X_{1},X_{2})\otimes Hom_{\mathcal{D}}^{*}(X_{0},X_{1})\to Hom_{\mathcal{D}}^{*}(X_{0},X_{2}).

(C.10)

This determines an
A_{\infty}
category
(\mathcal{C},\mu_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{D}}})
with the same objects by setting:

\displaystyle hom_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}(X,Y):=Hom_{\mathcal{D}}^{*}(X,Y)

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{D}}}^{1}(f)=0,\quad\mu_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{D}}}^{2}(f,g):=(-1)^{|f|}g\cdot f,\quad\mu_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{D}}}^{k}=0,k\geq 3.

In this context, the
A_{\infty}
operations respect the cohomological degree grading. As a consequence by
Section

C.1
, we can consider the Hochschild cochains of cohomological degree
t
,
CC^{*}_{t}(\mathcal{C},\mu_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{D}}}).

Going forward, we assume that

\displaystyle Hom_{\mathcal{D}}^{*}(X,Y)\textrm{ is concentrated in degrees 0 and }d\textrm{ for any }X,Y\in Ob(\mathcal{D})

(C.11)

and some
d>0.
We then have that

\displaystyle CC^{*}_{t}(\mathcal{C},\mu_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{D}}})=0\text{ unless }t=kd.

(C.12)

We say that
(\mathcal{C},\mu)
is an admissible
A_{\infty}
structure on
\mathcal{C}
if

\displaystyle\mu^{k}=\mu^{k}_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{D}}},\quad k\leq 2.

(C.13)

The following is
[
Pol03
, Lemma 2.2]
(stated for categories as opposed to algebras) and is easily verified by comparing (
C.2
) with (
C.8
).

Lemma 127
.

Let
\mu
and
\tilde{\mu}
be two admissible
A_{\infty}
-structures on
\mathcal{C}
. Assume that
\mu^{k}=\tilde{\mu}^{k}
for
k<s
, where
s\geq 3
.

1.

Set
c(a_{1},\cdots,a_{s}):=(\tilde{\mu}^{s}-\mu^{s})(a_{1},\cdots,a_{s}).
Then
c\in CC^{2}_{2-s}(\mathcal{C},\mu_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{D}}})
is a Hochschild cocycle.

2.

Suppose
\tilde{\mu}=F_{*}(\mu)
(recall (
C.9
)) for some formal diffeomorphism
F
. Then
c
is a Hochschild coboundary, i.e., we have

\displaystyle[c]=0\in HH^{2}_{2-s}(\mathcal{C},\mu_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{D}}}).

(C.14)

Proposition 128
.

Suppose
\mathcal{D}
is a proper, graded
\Bbbk
-linear category satisfying (
C.11
) and

\displaystyle HH^{2}_{-kd}(\mathcal{C},\mu_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{D}}})=0,\quad k>1.

(C.15)

Suppose further that
\mathcal{C}
carries a
G
-grading and that
\mu,\tilde{\mu}
are two admissible
A_{\infty}
structures which preserve this grading. If there is a strict isomorphism

\displaystyle F:(\mathcal{C},\mu)\xrightarrow{\sim}(\mathcal{C},\tilde{\mu}),

(C.16)

then there exists a strict isomorphism

\displaystyle\tilde{F}:(\mathcal{C},\mu)\xrightarrow{\sim}(\mathcal{C},\tilde{\mu}).

(C.17)

which preserves the
G
-grading.

Proof C.1
.

To begin, notice that because
\mathcal{C}
has finite dimensional hom-spaces, by (
C.6
) we have a decomposition:

\displaystyle CC^{*}_{-kd}(\mathcal{C},\tilde{\mu})\cong\prod_{g\in G}CC^{*}_{g\oplus-kd}(\mathcal{C},\tilde{\mu})

(C.18)

where
CC^{*}_{g\oplus-kd}(\mathcal{C},\mu_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{D}}})
refers to Hochschild cochains with
G
-grading
g\in G
and cohomological grading
-kd.
It follows in particular that
HH^{2}_{0\oplus-kd}(\mathcal{C},\mu_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{D}}})=0
for
k>1
.

We follow the inductive argument on the length
s
in the proof of
[
Pol03
, Theorem 1.1 (i)]
(page 11 of
loc. cit.
). The only difference is that we carry out the argument above in
CC^{*}_{0\oplus-kd}(\mathcal{C},\mu_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{D}}})
to construct
\tilde{F}
to preserve gradings. Note that we have
\tilde{F}^{s}=0
unless
s=1+kd
for some
k
. Let us begin with
k=1
. By Lemma
127
(2), we have
\partial_{\mu_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{D}}}}F^{1+d}=\tilde{\mu}^{d+2}-\mu^{d+2}
. Because
\mu
,
\tilde{\mu}
, and
\partial_{\mu_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{D}}}}
respect the
G
-grading, we have

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{D}}}}\tilde{F}^{1+d}=\tilde{\mu}^{d+2}-\mu^{d+2}

(C.19)

for some
\tilde{F}^{1+d}\in CC^{*}_{(0\oplus-kd)}(\mathcal{C},\mu_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{D}}}).
Putting it together with
\tilde{F}^{1}=F^{1}=\id
,
\tilde{F}^{1+d}
defines a graded formal diffeomorphism
\tilde{F}
such that
(\tilde{F}_{*}\mu)^{s}=\tilde{\mu}^{s}
for
s\leq d+2
.

Now we take
k>1
and proceed by induction. For a given
k
, we now are in the situation of Lemma
127
where there is an equivariant formal diffeomorphism which makes the
A_{\infty}
structures agree up to operations of length
2+(k-1)d
. As
HH^{2}_{(0\oplus-kd)}(\mathcal{C},\mu_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{D}}})=0
, we can apply Lemma
127
to find
\tilde{F}^{1+kd}\in CC^{*}_{(0\oplus-kd)}(\mathcal{C},\mu_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{D}}})
which makes the
A_{\infty}
structures agree to order
s=2+kd
. Composing this sequence of formal diffeomorphisms produces the desired
\tilde{F}.
Note that the infinite product of formal diffeomorphisms involved in this construction converges because the length increases at each stage.

C.3
Obstructions for
H^{*}(\mathcal{B}_{0})

We now compute the obstructions from Section
C.2
in the case where
\mathcal{D}=H^{*}(\mathcal{B}_{0})
.
Note that this category satisfies (
C.11
) with
d=n
(cf.
Lemma

47
).
Our main computation is as follows:

Proposition 129
.

We have

\displaystyle HH^{j}_{-kn}(\mathcal{C},\mu_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{D}}})=0

(C.20)

for all
j<2k
.

In order to prove
Proposition

129
, we introduce the ‘extended homogeneous coordinate ring’:

\displaystyle A_{L}:=\bigoplus_{p\oplus q\in\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}}\operatorname{Hom}^{q}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O}(p)),

(C.21)

with its natural ring structure. Because
A_{L}
is graded by
p\oplus q\in\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}
, we can consider the
\Z\oplus\Z
-graded Hochschild cochains,
CC^{*}_{p\oplus q}(A_{L}).

Our proof of
Proposition

129
is modelled on the following result of Polishchuk:

Theorem 130

(Theorem 3.3 of
[
Pol03
]
)
.

We have

\displaystyle HH^{j}_{0\oplus-kn}(A_{L})=0

(C.22)

for all
j<2k
.

Proof C.2

(Proof of
Proposition

129
)
.

We reduce the computation to that of
Theorem

130
.
As both
A_{L}
and
(\mathcal{C},\mu_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{D}}})
are strictly unital, it suffices to work with reduced Hochschild cochains, which we denote by
\bar{CC}
. We let

\displaystyle C^{*}_{A}:=\bar{CC}^{*}_{0\oplus-kn}(A_{L})

(C.23)

\displaystyle C^{*}_{\mathcal{C}}:=\bar{CC}^{*}_{-kn}(\mathcal{C},\mu_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{D}}}).

For a given
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, the Hochschild cochains
\psi\in C^{*}_{\mathcal{C}}
with output in
hom^{*}(\mathcal{O}(i),\mathcal{O}(i^{\prime}))
are identified with
C^{*}_{A}.
This gives rise to an identification of cochains

\displaystyle C^{*}_{\mathcal{C}}\cong\prod_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}C^{*}_{A}.

(C.24)

For the moment, this is only a decomposition of cochains and not of complexes because the Hochschild differential does not respect this product decomposition.

Next, as in the proof of
[
Pol03
, Theorem 3.3]
, we decompose

\displaystyle C^{*}_{A}=C^{*}_{A}(0)\oplus C^{*}_{A}(n)

(C.25)

\displaystyle C^{*}_{\mathcal{C}}=C^{*}_{\mathcal{C}}(0)\oplus C^{*}_{\mathcal{C}}(n)

where
C^{*}_{A}(0)
,
C^{*}_{\mathcal{C}}(0)
are the vector subspaces of maps whose output lies in degree
0
and
C^{*}_{A}(n),C^{*}_{\mathcal{C}}(n)
those whose output lies in degree
n.

C^{*}_{\mathcal{C}}(n)
is a subcomplex and we give
C^{*}_{\mathcal{C}}(0)
the structure of a complex via the exact sequence

\displaystyle 0\to C^{*}_{\mathcal{C}}(n)\to C^{*}_{\mathcal{C}}\to C^{*}_{\mathcal{C}}(0)\to 0.

(C.26)

The same considerations also apply to
C^{*}_{A}(0)
and
C^{*}_{A}(n).
It suffices to prove that

\displaystyle H^{j}(C^{*}_{\mathcal{C}}(0))=H^{j}(C^{*}_{\mathcal{C}}(n))=0

(C.27)

for
j<2k.
Let us first consider the case of
C^{*}_{\mathcal{C}}(0).
For any
r\geq 0
, let
C^{*}_{\mathcal{C}}(0)_{r}\subset C^{*}_{\mathcal{C}}(0)
denote the subspace of maps whose output lies in
hom(\mathcal{O}(i),\mathcal{O}(i+s))
for some
i\in\mathbb{Z}
and
s\geq r
. Let
C^{*}_{A}(0)_{r}\subset C^{*}_{A}(0)
denote the subspace of maps whose output lies in
Hom(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O}(s))
for
s\geq r
. These define filtrations of complexes and we let
C^{*}_{\mathcal{C}}(0)^{gr}
and
C^{*}_{A}(0)^{gr}
denote the associated graded complexes. It is easy to see that the piece of the Hochschild differential which fails to respect the product decomposition (
C.24
) strictly increases the
r
filtration. As a consequence, we have a product decomposition of complexes:

\displaystyle C^{*}_{\mathcal{C}}(0)^{gr}\cong\prod_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}C^{*}_{A}(0)^{gr}.

(C.28)

Polishchuk proves that
C^{*}_{A}(0)^{gr}
is acyclic in the relevant ranges and so the same applies to
C^{*}_{\mathcal{C}}(0)^{gr}.
The vanishing (
C.27
) then follows from a spectral sequence argument, using the fact that the filtration is manifestly bounded above and complete.

For
C^{*}_{\mathcal{C}}(n)
, we similarly consider the natural extension of the filtration of
C^{p}_{A}(n)
used in
[
Pol03
, Proof of Theorem 3.3]
. Namely, given a simple tensor
a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{s}\in hom^{q_{1}}(X_{0},X_{1})[1]\otimes\cdots\otimes hom^{q_{s}}(X_{s-1},X_{s})[1],
we let
r_{0}
be the number of consecutive degree-zero components
a_{1},\ldots,a_{r_{0}}
at the beginning of the tensor and
r_{1}
be the number of consecutive degree-zero components
a_{s-r_{1}+1},\ldots,a_{s}
at the end of the tensor. Let
C^{*}_{\mathcal{C}}(n)_{r}
denote the subcomplex of Hochschild cochains which vanish on inputs with
r_{0}+r_{1}\leq r
. As above, it is not difficult to see that the associated graded complex
C^{*}_{\mathcal{C}}(n)^{gr}
becomes a direct product of
\mathbb{Z}
-copies of
C^{*}_{A}(n)^{gr}
, which Polishchuk proves to be acyclic. Again the filtration is bounded above and complete, so the vanishing (
C.27
) follows from a spectral sequence argument.

Corollary 131
.

We have

\displaystyle HH^{2}_{-kn}(\mathcal{C},\mu_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{D}}})=0

(C.29)

for all
k>1
.

Proof C.3
.

Apply Proposition
129
with
j=2.

We now have everything we need to prove our main cochain-level result:

Lemma 132
.

There exists a
\Z\oplus M^{*}
-graded quasi-isomorphism
\mathcal{A}_{0}\simeq\mathcal{B}_{0}
compatible with the isomorphism
H^{*}(\mathcal{A}_{0})\cong H^{*}(\mathcal{B}_{0})
from
Lemma

48
.

Proof C.4
.

We begin by constructing minimal models
\mathcal{A}_{0,min}\simeq\mathcal{A}_{0}
and
\mathcal{B}_{0,min}\simeq\mathcal{B}_{0}
using
[
Sei08
, Proposition 1.12]
. The minimal model depends on a choice of contracting homotopy for each morphism space.
We choose these contracting homotopies to respect
\mathbb{Z}\oplus M^{*}
-gradings. The resulting minimal models
\mathcal{A}_{0,min}
,
\mathcal{B}_{0,min}
will then be
\mathbb{Z}\oplus M^{*}
-graded
A_{\infty}
categories and the quasi-isomorphisms
\mathcal{A}_{0,min}\simeq\mathcal{A}_{0}
,
\mathcal{B}_{0,min}\simeq\mathcal{B}_{0}
will be
\mathbb{Z}\oplus M^{*}
-graded.

The quasi-isomorphism
\mathcal{A}_{0}\simeq\mathcal{B}_{0}
from §
5
gives rise to a
\Z
-graded quasi-isomorphism
\mathcal{A}_{0,min}\simeq\mathcal{B}_{0,min}
of these minimal models. We can view this quasi-isomorphism of minimal models as defining two quasi-isomorphic admissible
A_{\infty}
structures on
(\mathcal{C},\mu_{\mathcal{C}_{H^{*}(\mathcal{B}_{0})}})
, the
A_{\infty}
category corresponding to
H^{*}(\mathcal{B}_{0}).
By Corollary
131
,

HH^{2}_{-kn}(\mathcal{C},\mu_{\mathcal{C}_{H^{*}(\mathcal{B}_{0})}})=0

for all
k>1
. The result then follows from Proposition
128
.
